Chapter 5 Depressive and Bipolar Disorders, and Suicide

In this chapter
  1. 5.1 Mood, Mood Episodes and How Mood Disorders Are Classified: tell normal sadness and joy apart from mood episodes, define the depressive, manic and hypomanic episodes, and trace how the mood disorders came to be classified as they are today.
  2. 5.2 Major Depressive Disorder: describe the symptoms, diagnosis, epidemiology and course of major depressive disorder, and how it presents in India.
  3. 5.3 The Other Depressive Disorders and the Specifiers: distinguish persistent depressive disorder, disruptive mood dysregulation disorder, premenstrual dysphoric disorder and the other depressive disorders, and apply the course and feature specifiers.
  4. 5.4 Bipolar and Related Disorders: distinguish bipolar I, bipolar II and cyclothymic disorder, and describe their course, rapid cycling, mixed features and the problems of diagnosing them.
  5. 5.5 Biological Causes: evaluate the genetic, neurochemical, endocrine, sleep and brain evidence on the mood disorders, including the monoamine hypotheses and kindling.
  6. 5.6 Psychological and Social Causes: evaluate the psychodynamic, behavioural, learned helplessness, hopelessness, cognitive, rumination, interpersonal and social theories of depression and the psychosocial triggers of bipolar episodes.
  7. 5.7 Treating Mood Disorders: Drugs, ECT and Other Physical Treatments: compare the antidepressant classes and the mood stabilisers, explain how ECT and the newer physical treatments are used, and place the psychotherapies alongside them.
  8. 5.8 Suicide I: Definitions, Scale and Risk: define the suicidal behaviours and their terms, describe the scale of suicide in the world and in India, and list the risk and protective factors.
  9. 5.9 Suicide II: Explanations and Prevention: evaluate the sociological, psychological and biological explanations of suicide and the strategies that prevent it, in India and elsewhere.

Weightage. 49 of the 128 questions mapped in this book (38.3%) test this chapter, 20 of them from real past papers. 2 of its 9 sections are High yield.

In February 1942 Singapore fell to the Japanese army, and among the Australian soldiers marched into captivity at Changi was a 30-year-old army doctor named John Cade. He had trained in psychiatry before the war, but for the next three and a half years he was simply a doctor among hungry and sick prisoners. Freed in September 1945, he recovered his health and in January 1946 took up a post at the Repatriation Mental Hospital at Bundoora, on the northern edge of Melbourne, a hospital for former servicemen. Psychiatry then had little to offer a patient in a manic state beyond sedatives, electroconvulsive therapy and the locked ward. Cade wondered whether mania might be a kind of poisoning by some substance the body makes in excess, rather as an overactive thyroid gland makes a person restless, excitable and sleepless. In an unused pantry at the hospital he set up a makeshift laboratory, collected urine from manic patients, from patients with schizophrenia and depression and from healthy people, and injected it into guinea pigs. All urine is poisonous in a large enough dose, largely because of its urea, but the urine of the manic patients seemed the most toxic of all. Suspecting that uric acid made urea more poisonous, he needed a form of uric acid that would dissolve in water, and he chose its lithium salt, lithium urate, which dissolves well. To his surprise, lithium urate made the mixture less toxic, not more. He then injected lithium carbonate on its own, and after a couple of hours the normally jumpy guinea pigs became so lethargic that they could be rolled onto their backs and would simply lie there. (Later writers have pointed out that at those doses the animals were probably not calm but sick with nausea.) Before giving the salt to any patient, Cade swallowed it himself to see whether it was safe.

His first patient was a man identified in the published paper only as W.B., aged 51, who had been "in a state of chronic manic excitement for five years". Restless, dirty, destructive, mischievous and interfering, he had long been regarded as the most troublesome patient on his ward, and two courses of electroconvulsive therapy in 1946 had settled him for only a few months. On 29 March 1948 Cade started him on lithium citrate. Within three weeks W.B. was calm enough to move to the convalescent ward, and soon he seemed perfectly normal to the staff and to his relatives. He was kept under observation for two months more, partly because of housing difficulties, and on 9 July 1948 he left hospital with a maintenance dose of lithium carbonate to take twice a day. Within weeks he was back at work at his old firm. Then, as so many people who feel well will do, he grew casual about his medicine and stopped it. In November his son-in-law wrote to Cade that W.B. had become excitable and argumentative; he stopped working just before Christmas, and on 30 January 1949 he was readmitted. Back on lithium, he settled within a fortnight. By then Cade had given lithium to ten patients with mania, six with schizophrenia and three with depression. Only the manic patients changed, but they changed dramatically.

Cade published his results in the Medical Journal of Australia on 3 September 1949, under the modest title "Lithium salts in the treatment of psychotic excitement". W.B.'s story did not end happily. By early 1950 he was vomiting, had lost his appetite and had developed a slow pulse; Cade stopped the lithium in March 1950 and the mania promptly returned. In May he restarted it, judging the mania as great a danger to life as the drug, and within a week W.B. had seizures and became semi-comatose, and he died on 23 May 1950. Cade recorded the first cause of death as toxaemia due to lithium salts. Lithium was in trouble elsewhere too. In the United States, lithium chloride sold as a salt substitute for heart patients on low-salt diets had caused severe poisonings and deaths, and its sale was banned in February 1949. A naturally occurring salt could not be patented, so no drug company had much reason to pay for trials, and until blood levels could be measured routinely the gap between a helpful dose and a poisonous one was frighteningly narrow. Lithium was rescued by the Danish psychiatrist Mogens Schou, who in 1954 compared it with a dummy tablet (a placebo) in one of the earliest controlled trials in psychiatry, and who later, with Poul Baastrup, showed that lithium taken every day also prevents new episodes of both mania and depression (1967). The United States approved it for mania only in 1970, more than twenty years after Cade's paper.

Notice what this story says, and what it does not. A cheap, simple salt, useless in schizophrenia, calmed a mania that had lasted five years and held it off for as long as it was taken. That is strong evidence that the mood disorders are, at least in part, disorders of the body's chemistry, and lithium went on to become the standard drug for preventing relapse in bipolar disorder. Yet W.B.'s recovery also depended on things that no chemistry can supply: a home to go to, an old firm willing to take him back, relatives who noticed the first signs of relapse and wrote to the doctor, and his own willingness to keep taking a medicine once he felt well. Both halves of that story run through this chapter, which deals with the depressions, with the bipolar disorders in which a person swings between the two poles of mood, and with suicide, the gravest outcome of both. Remember W.B. and John Cade; you will return to them at the end of the chapter.

5.1 Mood, Mood Episodes and How Mood Disorders Are ClassifiedMedium yield · 3 Qs

Everyone knows what it is to feel low after a disappointment and high after a success, and most people have lain awake with worry or spent a week in the fog of a bereavement. The disorders in this chapter are not simply bigger versions of those ordinary feelings. They are conditions in which a low or a high mood takes hold for weeks or months, drags sleep, appetite, energy, thinking and behaviour along with it, and stops responding to the events that ought to shift it. To study them you need three things, and this section supplies them: a clear sense of where normal mood ends, a precise definition of the episodes (depressive, manic and hypomanic) from which every mood disorder is built, and a map of how the two great classification systems arrange the disorders those episodes make up. Everything in Sections 5.2 to 5.4 rests on this foundation.

Mood, affect and the ordinary ups and downs

Two words need fixing first. mood is the pervasive, sustained emotional state that a person experiences over days or weeks and that colours how they see themselves and the world; because it is subjective, the clinician learns it by asking. affect is the observable expression of emotion from moment to moment: what the face, voice and posture show during the interview. Mood is the emotional climate, affect the emotional weather, and Book 2, Section 2.4 explains how each is examined in the mental status examination. The disorders in this chapter are disorders of mood in exactly this sense: what goes wrong is the sustained background state, not merely its passing expression. That is why DSM-5-TR and ICD-10 call them mood disorders. The older name, affective disorders, survives in ICD-10's heading "mood (affective) disorders" and in many exam questions, and it means the same thing.

A handful of descriptive words recur throughout the chapter (Chapter 2 treats them fully). euthymia is normal mood, neither lowered nor raised. dysphoria is any unpleasant mood: sad, irritable, tense or discontented. euphoria is an exaggerated feeling of well-being out of keeping with the person's real circumstances, and elation is a mood of joy, triumph and intense self-satisfaction. Irritability, a readiness to become annoyed or angry, belongs to neither pole in particular: a depressed adolescent and a manic adult may both be chiefly irritable. A mood is labile when it swings quickly from one state to another, as when a manic patient bursts into tears in the middle of a joke.

Where, then, does an ordinary low end and a depressive illness begin? Consider Neha, a 24-year-old commerce graduate in Pune who has just been turned down after the final interview for a job she had wanted for two years. For about ten days she cries easily, sleeps badly for a couple of nights and tells her friends that she is "useless". But at her cousin's wedding that weekend she laughs at the jokes and dances with her sisters, she eats normally, she applies for two more posts, and by the third week she is mostly herself again. This is sadness: a response in proportion to a real loss, which comes in waves when something reminds her of the loss, leaves her capacity for pleasure intact and fades as life moves on.

Now imagine Neha eighteen months later, settled in a new job, with nothing obviously wrong in her life. For five weeks she has woken at four every morning and been unable to get back to sleep. Food tastes of nothing, and she has lost four kilograms. She cannot follow the plot of a television serial she used to love, and she sits at her desk reading the same email again and again. She feels heavy and slowed, as if she were wading through water. She is convinced that she is a burden to her parents and that they would be better off without her. Oddly, she does not cry much: she says she feels "empty, not even sad". Her family's efforts to cheer her up, a trip to her grandmother's village, her favourite food, make no difference at all. This is not more of the same sadness. It is a different state, a major depressive episode, and the contrast with her first experience shows the features that separate the two. Depression is out of proportion to any cause, or has no apparent cause at all. It lasts, here for weeks. It is pervasive, colouring every part of the day, rather than coming in waves. It is unreactive: good news and pleasant company do not lift it. It attacks self-esteem, so that the person feels worthless or guilty rather than simply unlucky. It brings bodily changes in sleep, appetite, weight and energy. And it stops the person from working and living as before.

Grief is the hardest case, because a bereavement can produce intense sadness, insomnia, poor appetite and weight loss, all of which also appear on the list of depressive symptoms. DSM-5-TR offers a set of practical distinctions, summarised in Table 5.1. In grief the main feeling is emptiness and loss; in a depressive episode it is a persistent low mood together with an inability to look forward to any happiness. The pain of grief comes in waves, the so-called pangs of grief, set off by thoughts and reminders of the dead person, and it can sit alongside moments of warmth and even humour; the misery of depression is steady and is not tied to particular thoughts. The grieving person is preoccupied with memories of the dead, the depressed person with self-criticism. Above all, in grief self-esteem is usually preserved: if there is self-reproach, it concerns failings towards the dead person ("I should have visited more often"). When a bereaved person thinks of death, it is usually of joining the one who has died, whereas the depressed person thinks of ending a life that feels worthless. Sigmund Freud (1856 to 1939) put the difference memorably in 1917: in mourning it is the world that has become poor and empty, in melancholia it is the ego itself (his theory of depression is in Section 5.6). The two states can coexist, and a depressive episode on top of grief is more severe and has a worse outlook than grief alone. Whether a diagnosis of depression should be held back after a death, the "bereavement exclusion" that DSM-5 removed, is discussed in Section 5.2; normal and complicated grief are covered in Book 2, Section 9.5, and prolonged grief disorder in Chapter 7.

Table 5.1 Ordinary sadness, grief and a major depressive episode compared.
FeatureOrdinary sadnessGriefMajor depressive episode
CauseA clear disappointment or lossA death or other major lossOften a stressor, often none; out of proportion
Main feelingSadnessEmptiness and lossPersistent low mood; cannot anticipate pleasure
PatternComes and goes; fades in daysWaves ("pangs") set off by reminders; eases over weeksSteady, most of the day, nearly every day
Pleasure and humourKeptPossible between pangsLost (anhedonia); mood unreactive
Self-esteemKeptUsually kept; guilt about the dead personWorthlessness and self-loathing
Thoughts of deathRareOf joining the dead personOf ending one's own life
FunctioningBriefly dentedDisturbed, then recoversClearly impaired

The same questions separate ordinary happiness from an abnormally raised mood. When Harish, a 29-year-old bank officer in Hyderabad, heard that his promotion had come through, he was elated: he distributed sweets to the whole branch, talked late into the night with his wife and slept only five hours. That is joy. It was tied to an event, everyone around him understood it and shared it, and within a few days he was back to his usual self. An abnormal elevation of mood differs in the same ways that depression differs from sadness. It persists for days or weeks, it is out of proportion to anything that has happened or arises from nothing, it comes with a surge of energy and a reduced need for sleep, it pulls judgement and behaviour along with it, and the people who know the person best find it strange and worrying rather than pleasant. You will meet Harish again in a few pages.

Exam trap

Mood is the sustained state the patient reports; affect is the momentary expression the clinician observes. Do not swap them. "Affective disorder" is simply the older name for a mood disorder, not a disorder of affect alone. Grief and depression share sadness, insomnia and poor appetite; the features that point to a depressive episode are pervasive, unreactive low mood, lost self-esteem and thoughts of ending one's own life.

From melancholia to manic-depressive insanity: a short history

Depression is one of the oldest described of all mental disorders. The physicians of the Hippocratic school in ancient Greece, followers of Hippocrates (about 460 to about 370 BCE), described melancholia, a state of prolonged fear and despondency, and explained it by an excess of black bile, one of the four bodily humours; the word itself means "black bile" (melaina chole), and it lives on in our word melancholy. The same writers described mania, a state of wild excitement. The first physician known to have linked the two in the same person was Aretaeus of Cappadocia, a Greek doctor who practised in about the first or second century CE. He described patients who were gloomy and inert at one time and furious, elated and sleepless at another, and suggested that melancholia might be the beginning and a part of mania. For the next sixteen centuries, though, melancholia and mania were usually treated as separate conditions.

The most famous book on depression ever written came from an Oxford scholar, Robert Burton (1577 to 1640), who published The Anatomy of Melancholy in 1621 under the pen name Democritus Junior. It is a vast, rambling survey of everything then believed about melancholy, its causes, symptoms and cures (from exercise, music and friendship to purging and bloodletting). Burton was himself a sufferer and said that he wrote of melancholy by being busy to avoid melancholy.

The modern idea of a single illness with two poles was born in Paris in 1854. Within a fortnight of each other, two psychiatrists presented to the Académie de Médecine descriptions of patients whose mania and melancholia alternated in regular cycles. On 31 January Jules Baillarger (1809 to 1890) described folie à double forme ("insanity in double form"), insisting that the manic and melancholic phases were two halves of a single attack. On 14 February Jean-Pierre Falret (1794 to 1870) described folie circulaire ("circular insanity"), a continuous cycle of mania, melancholia and a free interval, and claimed that he had been teaching the idea for years. The priority quarrel between the two men was never settled, and textbooks usually credit both. (Do not confuse Jean-Pierre with his son Jules Falret (1824 to 1902), who with Charles Lasègue (1816 to 1883) described folie à deux, shared psychotic disorder, in 1877.) A little later the German psychiatrist Karl Ludwig Kahlbaum (1828 to 1899) gave the name cyclothymia to a milder form of circular mood disturbance.

The decisive step came from Emil Kraepelin (1856 to 1926) at Heidelberg. Kraepelin classified psychoses not by their symptoms at a single moment but by their course over years, and in the sixth edition of his textbook, in 1899, he drew the great dividing line of modern psychiatry. On one side he put dementia praecox (later called schizophrenia, Chapter 4), which he saw as a progressive illness ending in deterioration. On the other he put manic-depressive insanity, into which he gathered almost every recurrent disorder of mood: periodic mania, periodic melancholia, circular insanity and their milder forms. What united them, he argued, was an episodic course with recovery between attacks and no lasting deterioration. Notice that Kraepelin did not separate depression-only illness from illness with both poles: for him they were one disease. His colleague Wilhelm Weygandt (1870 to 1939) published a monograph on mixed states in the same year, a topic taken up below.

The split that shapes today's classification came in 1957, when the German psychiatrist Karl Leonhard (1904 to 1988) divided Kraepelin's single illness into unipolar forms, in which only depressions (or, more rarely, only manias) occur, and bipolar forms, in which both poles appear. Leonhard noticed that the bipolar patients had more relatives with mood disorders. Nine years later, in 1966, Jules Angst in Zurich and Carlo Perris in Sweden independently tested the idea with large family studies, and George Winokur and his colleagues in St Louis reached the same conclusion soon after. Their findings, summarised in the Key study box below, persuaded the field. The American manual adopted the split in DSM-III (1980), which introduced the official terms bipolar disorder and major depression under the heading "affective disorders"; DSM-III-R (1987) renamed the class "mood disorders", the name DSM-IV (1994) kept. Figure 5.1 sets out the milestones.

Key study: Splitting unipolar from bipolar (Angst and Perris, 1966)

The question was simple: are depression-only illness and illness with manias as well one disease, as Kraepelin thought, or two? Angst in Switzerland and Perris in Sweden each collected patients with recurrent mood disorders, divided them into those who had only ever had depressions and those who had also had manias, and then traced the illnesses of their parents, brothers, sisters and children. Both found the same thing. Relatives of bipolar patients had more mood disorder overall and, in particular, more bipolar disorder; relatives of unipolar patients had mostly unipolar depression. Bipolar illness also began earlier and brought more frequent episodes. The conclusion was that the two forms "breed true" often enough to be treated as distinct. The criticism is that the boundary is not watertight: many bipolar patients begin with depressions, and some people diagnosed as unipolar later have a manic episode, which is why Section 5.4 returns to the problem of misdiagnosis.

Exam trap

Folie circulaire is Jean-Pierre Falret's; folie à double forme is Baillarger's; both were presented in 1854. Folie à deux (shared psychosis) is Lasègue and Jules Falret, 1877, a different idea altogether. Kraepelin named manic-depressive insanity in 1899 and separated it from dementia praecox, but he did not separate unipolar from bipolar illness: that was Leonhard, 1957, confirmed by Angst and Perris in 1966. Lithium (Cade) is 1949, a treatment milestone, not a classification one.

The three mood episodes: the building blocks

Diagnosing a mood disorder is a two-step job, and keeping the steps apart prevents most of the confusion students feel about this chapter. First you decide what kind of mood episode the person is having now, or has had in the past: a distinct period, with a beginning and an end, during which the mood and a defined set of other symptoms are present together. There are three kinds: the major depressive episode, the manic episode and the hypomanic episode. Second, you look at the pattern of episodes across the person's whole life and name the disorder that this pattern makes. A single manic episode in a lifetime makes bipolar I disorder; hypomanic and major depressive episodes, but never mania, make bipolar II disorder; major depressive episodes alone make major depressive disorder. In DSM-5-TR an episode on its own is never a diagnosis, which is why so many examination questions turn on the exact definition of each one. Table 5.2 sets the three side by side, and the rest of this subsection explains the reasoning behind each rule.

The major depressive episode requires five or more of nine symptoms, present together during the same 2-week period, most of the day, nearly every day, and representing a change from how the person was before. At least one of the five must be one of the two core symptoms: depressed mood (sad, empty or hopeless, by the person's own account or as others observe it, for example tearfulness) or anhedonia, a markedly reduced interest or pleasure in all or almost all activities. All nine are remembered as depressed mood plus the mnemonic SIG E CAPS, usually read as a doctor's prescription, "Sig: energy capsules":

  • Sleep: insomnia or hypersomnia, nearly every day.
  • Interest: loss of interest or pleasure (anhedonia, the second core symptom).
  • Guilt: worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt, sometimes delusional.
  • Energy: fatigue or loss of energy.
  • Concentration: poor concentration or indecisiveness.
  • Appetite: a clear change in appetite, or in weight (more than about 5 percent in a month).
  • Psychomotor change: agitation or retardation that other people can see.
  • Suicide: recurrent thoughts of death, suicidal ideas, a plan or an attempt.

Each rule has a reason. The 2-week minimum and the "most of the day, nearly every day" clause rule out the bad days and bad weeks that everyone has. The demand for five symptoms rules out isolated complaints. The core-symptom rule exists because the remaining symptoms (poor sleep, poor appetite, fatigue, poor concentration, slowing) are just as typical of physical illness, pain, anxiety or exhaustion; without low mood or lost pleasure, the syndrome would not be a disorder of mood at all. For the same reason DSM-5-TR tells the clinician not to count a symptom that is clearly due to another medical condition, and the episode as a whole must not be caused by a drug, a medicine or an illness such as hypothyroidism. Finally, the symptoms must cause clinically significant distress or impairment, the general rule for any mental disorder (Chapter 1). In children and adolescents the mood may be irritable rather than sad. Section 5.2 takes each symptom in turn and shows what it looks and sounds like at interview.

Return now to Harish. Eight months after his promotion, his brother brings him to the emergency department at two in the morning. For ten days Harish has slept two or three hours a night and says he feels "fresher than ever". He has taken a personal loan to put down deposits on a flat and two cars, told his colleagues that he will soon be chairman of the bank, and sent long messages through the night to a film actress he has never met. He talks without a pause, jumps from one topic to the next, cannot sit through a sentence of the doctor's questions without being drawn to the noise in the corridor, and turns angry when his brother tries to take away his phone. His wife adds a detail. Two years earlier, she says, he had a week when he was unusually cheerful and full of plans, cleared a backlog of files at the office, slept five hours instead of seven and was "the life of every party", but nothing went wrong, and nobody thought of a doctor. The present episode is mania; the earlier one was, in all probability, hypomania. The rules below explain why.

The manic episode begins with a two-part core. There must be a distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive or irritable mood, and of abnormally and persistently increased activity or energy. Elevated mood is euphoric, "on top of the world"; expansive mood is an unrestrained outpouring of enthusiasm for people, plans and ideas, in which the person may strike up long conversations with strangers; irritable mood flares into anger, especially when the person's wishes are blocked. The energy requirement was added in DSM-5 (and is also required in ICD-11) so that a merely cheerful or merely irritable person is not mistaken for a manic one. The period must last at least 1 week, most of the day, nearly every day, or any length of time if admission to hospital is needed: treatment often cuts an episode short, and a man admitted on the third day of a dangerous mania plainly has the condition. During this period at least three of seven further symptoms must be present to a significant degree, and four if the mood is only irritable. The extra symptom is demanded because irritability on its own is so unspecific: it is common in depression, anxiety, substance use and many personality disorders. The seven are remembered as DIG FAST:

  • Distractibility: attention pulled away by trivial things, as Harish's was by the corridor.
  • Indiscretion: risky pleasures such as buying sprees, sexual indiscretions or foolish investments.
  • Grandiosity: inflated self-esteem, from uncritical confidence to delusional grandiosity.
  • Flight of ideas: flight of ideas, rapid jumps between loosely linked ideas, or racing thoughts.
  • Activity: more goal-directed activity (social, occupational or sexual) or psychomotor agitation.
  • Sleep: a decreased need for sleep, feeling rested after three hours. This is not insomnia, in which the person wants to sleep and cannot; it often heralds an episode.
  • Talkativeness: more talkative than usual, or pressured speech, rapid and hard to interrupt.

Section 5.4 shows what each of these looks like on the ward. The episode must also be severe in at least one of three ways: it causes marked impairment in social or occupational functioning, or it makes hospitalisation necessary to prevent harm to the person or others, or there are psychotic features. Any one of the three is enough. Finally, the episode must not be due to a drug of abuse, a medicine or a medical condition. One exception was written into DSM-5 and kept in DSM-5-TR: a full manic episode that begins during antidepressant treatment (medication or electroconvulsive therapy) but continues at full strength after the treatment's direct physiological effect would have worn off counts as a true manic episode, and therefore establishes bipolar I disorder. Harish meets every part of this definition. His mood is elevated and irritable, his energy is boundless, he has at least five DIG FAST symptoms, and the episode has wrecked his finances and needs admission.

The hypomanic episode is mania in a milder key. Its core (elevated, expansive or irritable mood plus increased activity or energy) and its symptom list (three of DIG FAST, four if the mood is only irritable) are exactly the same. What differs is everything to do with severity. It need last only 4 consecutive days, most of the day, nearly every day. It must bring an unequivocal change in functioning that is uncharacteristic of the person, and the change in mood and functioning must be observable by others. But it must not be severe enough to cause marked impairment or to require hospitalisation, and it has no psychotic features: in the words of the criteria, if there are psychotic features the episode is, by definition, manic. DSM-5-TR adds a caution that has real clinical importance. A full hypomanic episode that emerges during antidepressant treatment and outlasts the drug's effect counts, but one or two symptoms, especially the irritability, edginess or agitation that antidepressants sometimes produce, are not enough to diagnose hypomania or to label a person bipolar.

Why insist that others must notice? Because hypomania often feels wonderful. People describe it as their most creative and productive time, not as an illness, and rarely seek help for it; Harish's earlier week was remembered by his wife, not by him. The requirement that the change be visible to others protects against counting an ordinary good week as an episode, and in practice it means that a history of hypomania usually has to come from a relative or friend. The difference between mania and hypomania is, then, one of degree, not of kind. The two share the same mood, the same energy and the same symptoms; they differ in four things only: duration (1 week against 4 days), severity (marked impairment in mania, none of that degree in hypomania), the need for hospitalisation, and psychosis, which only mania can have. Figure 5.2 places all three episodes on a single line of mood.

Table 5.2 The three mood episodes in DSM-5-TR.
FeatureMajor depressive episodeManic episodeHypomanic episode
Core featureDepressed mood or loss of interest or pleasure (at least one must be present)Elevated, expansive or irritable mood plus increased activity or energyAs for mania
Number of symptoms5 or more of 9 (depressed mood plus SIG E CAPS)3 or more of 7 (DIG FAST); 4 if mood is only irritableAs for mania
Minimum duration2 weeks, most of the day, nearly every day1 week, or any duration if hospitalised4 consecutive days
ImpairmentClinically significant distress or impairmentMarked impairment (or hospitalisation, or psychosis)Clear change seen by others, but no marked impairment
Psychotic featuresMay occurMay occur; their presence alone makes the episode manicNever; psychosis makes it mania
HospitalisationMay be neededNeeding it qualifies the episode as manicNever needed; if needed, it is mania
Role in diagnosisDefines major depressive disorder; needed for bipolar II; common but not required in bipolar IOne in a lifetime establishes bipolar IWith a major depressive episode, establishes bipolar II; common but not required in bipolar I

Psychotic features deserve one more sentence, because they explain a question that appears in many papers. Both a severe depressive episode and a manic episode can bring delusions or hallucinations, and in mood disorders these are usually mood-congruent: their content matches the mood. The depressed patient believes that she has committed an unforgivable sin, that her organs are rotting or that the family is ruined; the manic patient believes that he has a special mission or is related to a famous person. In a mood disorder the psychosis also occurs only during the mood episodes. These two features, mood congruence and confinement to the episodes, are what best separate a mood disorder with psychotic features from a primary psychotic disorder such as schizophrenia; the specifier is taught in Section 5.3, and schizoaffective disorder, which sits on the boundary, in Chapter 4.

Clinical link

Before any depression is called "unipolar", ask about hypomania, and ask a relative too. A person in a depressive episode does not volunteer that two years ago they had a marvellous week of five-hour nights, and many people with bipolar II disorder are treated for years as if they had major depressive disorder. The question matters because an antidepressant given alone to a person with bipolar disorder can trigger a switch into mania or hypomania (Section 5.7), and because the long-term treatment of the two conditions is different.

Exam trap

The symptoms of a major depressive episode include loss of interest or pleasure and changes in appetite. Specific phobias and an excessively euphoric mood are not on the list: phobias belong to the anxiety disorders (Chapter 6), and euphoria belongs to the opposite pole. The episode needs five or more symptoms over at least 2 weeks, one of them depressed mood or anhedonia; options of three, four or seven symptoms, or of one week, one month or six months, are distractors. Persistent sadness with loss of interest is the hallmark of depression, not of schizophrenia, obsessive-compulsive disorder or bipolar disorder.

Exam trap

The two key symptoms of a manic episode are an extremely elevated mood and grandiose ideas. Hallucinations can occur in severe mania but are not among the seven DIG FAST symptoms; social phobia and an intense fear of weight gain (anorexia nervosa, Chapter 8) are not manic symptoms at all. Mania is separated from hypomania by duration (1 week against 4 days), marked impairment, hospitalisation and psychotic features. Distractors to reject: "hypomania has no elevated mood", "hypomania requires hallucinations", "only hypomania can be psychotic", "hypomania lasts longer", "hypomania needs no observable change" (it must be observable by others) and "mania is always preceded by depression" (it need not be).

Mixed states: when the two poles meet

If mania and depression were simply opposite ends of one line, a person could only be at one point on it at a time. Clinicians have known for more than a century that this is not so. Kraepelin and Weygandt, both writing in 1899, argued that three parts of mental life (mood, the flow of thinking and the level of activity) can each be raised or lowered independently. When they move together the result is pure mania or pure depression; when they move apart the result is a mixed state, such as an agitated depression in which despair comes with racing thoughts and restless pacing, or a mania in which the person is driven, sleepless and talkative but also tearful and hopeless. Mixed states are among the most dangerous conditions in psychiatry, because the energy and impulsivity of mania are joined to the despair of depression.

The manuals have handled mixed states in two different ways. DSM-IV defined a mixed episode as one in which the full criteria for both a manic episode and a major depressive episode were met nearly every day for at least a week. The definition was so strict that few patients met it, although many had obvious mixtures of the two. DSM-5 therefore dropped the mixed episode and replaced it with a specifier, with mixed features, which can be added to a manic, a hypomanic or a major depressive episode when at least three symptoms of the opposite pole are present on most days. A manic episode with mixed features is a mania with concurrent depressive symptoms (prominent low mood, loss of pleasure, psychomotor slowing, fatigue, worthlessness or guilt, thoughts of death); a depressive episode with mixed features is a depression with concurrent manic symptoms (elevated mood, grandiosity, pressured speech, racing thoughts, increased energy, risky behaviour, decreased need for sleep). Notice what is left off both lists: irritability, distractibility, agitation, insomnia and poor concentration occur at both poles, so they cannot show that two poles are mixed. If a person meets the full criteria for mania and for depression at the same time, DSM-5-TR calls it a manic episode with mixed features, because full mania is the more severe and impairing state.

The specifier carries real information. DSM-5-TR notes that depressive symptoms occur in about 35 percent of manic episodes, and that mixed features go with a poorer outcome and more suicide attempts; clinical studies have also found that mixed mania responds less well to lithium than pure mania does. Mixed features during a major depressive episode are a significant risk factor for later developing bipolar I or bipolar II disorder. ICD-11, by contrast, kept the mixed episode: a period of at least 2 weeks with either a mixture or a very rapid alternation of prominent manic and depressive symptoms on most days, and a mixed episode, like a manic one, establishes bipolar type I disorder. Section 5.3 sets this specifier alongside the others, and Section 5.4 returns to mixed features in bipolar disorder.

Exam trap

"With mixed features" applied to a manic or hypomanic episode means concurrent depressive symptoms, not psychosis, anxiety (that is the separate "with anxious distress" specifier) or substance withdrawal. DSM-5 replaced DSM-IV's mixed episode with this specifier; ICD-11 kept the mixed episode. The specifier applies to depressive episodes too, so "depressive symptoms occur only in mania" is false.

Unipolar and bipolar, and the older ways of dividing depression

Leonhard's distinction is now the first fork in every classification. A unipolar disorder is one in which mood moves away from normal in one direction only, almost always downwards: major depressive disorder and persistent depressive disorder are the unipolar disorders. A bipolar disorder is one in which mood moves in both directions, so that mania or hypomania occurs as well as depression; in the words of a common examination stem, it is the affective disorder in which a person swings from one mood extreme to another. Notice, though, how the manuals actually draw the line. They define a bipolar disorder by the presence of an elevated episode, not by an observed swing. One manic episode is enough for bipolar I disorder in DSM-5-TR even if no depression ever follows, and ICD-10 states that patients who have only repeated episodes of hypomania or mania are classified as bipolar. A person who has only ever had manias, a pattern sometimes called unipolar mania, is therefore given a bipolar diagnosis. The reasoning is practical: most people who have one mania go on to have depressions, and people with mania alone resemble other bipolar patients in their family histories and in their response to mood stabilisers.

Indian context

Unipolar mania has long interested Indian psychiatrists, because clinicians here see patients who return again and again with mania and seldom with depression. In a 2021 analysis from the multicentre Bipolar Disorder Course and Outcome study from India, of 773 patients who had been ill for at least ten years, between about 5 and 20 percent had a unipolar manic course, depending on how strictly the pattern was defined. Compared with other bipolar patients they had more manic episodes per year of illness and more often had an episode with psychotic symptoms, but fewer of them had ever attempted suicide. Whatever its proper place, the pattern is classified as bipolar disorder in both DSM-5-TR and ICD.

Before the unipolar-bipolar split took over, depression was divided along two other lines, and you will still meet both in older books, in case notes and in examination options. The first was cause. endogenous depression ("arising from within") was thought to be a biological illness that came out of the blue, with a characteristic picture: waking in the early hours, mood worst in the morning and lifting a little as the day wears on, loss of appetite and weight, psychomotor slowing, an unreactive mood and deep, often irrational guilt. It was expected to respond to physical treatment. reactive depression (also called exogenous) was thought to be an understandable reaction to a loss or stress, with a mood that fluctuated with circumstances, difficulty falling asleep rather than early waking, prominent anxiety and a tendency to blame others or the situation rather than oneself. The second line was severity. psychotic depression meant a severe depression with delusions, loss of contact with reality or stupor; neurotic depression meant a milder, anxious depression with insight preserved, often in a person with long-standing personality difficulties. In practice the two lines merged, so that "endogenous" and "psychotic" came to be used almost interchangeably, as did "reactive" and "neurotic".

Key study: One illness or two? The great depression debate

For half a century British psychiatry argued over whether endogenous and reactive depression were separate diseases. Aubrey Lewis (1900 to 1975) studied 61 depressed patients in minute detail at the Maudsley Hospital in London and in 1934 reported that he could find no clean way of sorting them into two types: the differences were of degree. The Newcastle school led by Martin Roth (1917 to 2006) disagreed. Using a statistical technique that looks for underlying groupings in lists of symptoms, Leslie Kiloh and Robert Garside reported in 1963 that endogenous and neurotic depression formed two independent clusters. In London, Robert Kendell (1935 to 2002) analysed large hospital samples and in 1968 concluded that the scores were spread along a single continuum, with most patients in the middle. The debate was never won outright, but it left a lasting compromise: there may be no separate endogenous disease, but there is a recognisable endogenous, or melancholic, pattern of symptoms, and it is worth recording because it predicts a good response to physical treatments.

Two discoveries sank the cause-based labels. Studies of life events showed that depressions with a typical "endogenous" picture are very often preceded by stressful events too, especially early in the illness, so "out of the blue" was frequently untrue (the kindling hypothesis in Section 5.5 offers one explanation of why later episodes seem to need less provocation). And the labels mixed up three different things, cause, severity and symptom pattern, that are better recorded separately. What survived was the symptom pattern. The endogenous picture lives on as the DSM-5-TR specifier "with melancholic features" (Section 5.3), as ICD-10's somatic syndrome, which ICD-10 itself notes is also called melancholic, vital, biological or endogenomorphic, and as ICD-11's "with melancholia" qualifier. Psychotic depression survives as the specifier "with psychotic features". Neurotic depression was renamed dysthymic disorder (or depressive neurosis) in DSM-III, and its descendant today is persistent depressive disorder (Section 5.3). A last old pair, primary depression (arising in a person with no earlier psychiatric or serious medical illness) and secondary depression (arising on top of one), came from the St Louis research criteria of John Feighner and colleagues in 1972; it survives in today's categories of substance-induced depression and depression due to another medical condition (Section 5.3 and Chapter 10).

Exam trap

A disorder in which mood swings from one extreme to the other, or in which mania and depression alternate, is bipolar; "unipolar" means depression only, and "manic" and "depressive" name episodes, not classes of disorder. But a person with mania alone is still classified as bipolar. "Endogenous" never meant "no stressor ever": its heir is the melancholic specifier, a symptom pattern, not a cause.

How DSM-5-TR classifies the mood disorders

DSM-IV had a single chapter, "Mood Disorders", which first defined the four episodes (major depressive, manic, mixed and hypomanic) and then built the disorders out of them. DSM-5 (2013) broke the chapter in two, bipolar and related disorders and depressive disorders, and DSM-5-TR (2022) kept the arrangement. It also changed where the bipolar chapter sits: it now comes between the chapter on schizophrenia spectrum and other psychotic disorders and the chapter on depressive disorders, a placement the manual explains as recognising bipolar disorder's place as a bridge between those two classes in symptoms, family history and genetics. Several reasons lay behind the split. Bipolar disorder shares more of its genetic risk and family pattern with schizophrenia than unipolar depression does (Section 5.5), and psychotic features are common in mania. Its treatment is different, built on mood stabilisers and antipsychotics rather than antidepressants, which can be harmful when given alone (Section 5.7). Its course, with recurrent episodes at both poles, is different too. And the DSM-5 authors wanted the criteria to be easier to use at the bedside, so instead of defining the episodes once at the start of the chapter, they wrote the episode criteria into the criteria for each disorder.

The depressive disorders are united, in DSM-5-TR's words, by sad, empty or irritable mood together with bodily and cognitive changes that significantly affect a person's capacity to function; what separates them is duration, timing or presumed cause. Three placements are worth noticing. Disruptive mood dysregulation disorder, a disorder of chronic irritability and temper outbursts in children, is in the depressive chapter, not the bipolar one, because such children usually grow up to develop depressive and anxiety disorders rather than bipolar disorder. Persistent depressive disorder consolidates DSM-IV's dysthymic disorder and chronic major depressive disorder. And premenstrual dysphoric disorder was promoted in DSM-5 from the appendix to the main text. Cyclothymic disorder, the chronic mild two-pole pattern, sits in the bipolar chapter, while its one-pole cousin, persistent depressive disorder, sits in the depressive chapter. The phrase "mood disorder" survives in only one place: DSM-5-TR added unspecified mood disorder (code F39) for presentations in which mood symptoms dominate but it is hard to choose between an unspecified bipolar and an unspecified depressive disorder, for example in acute agitation. Table 5.3 lists both chapters; the disorders themselves are taught in Sections 5.2 to 5.4.

Table 5.3 The two mood chapters of DSM-5-TR.
Bipolar and related disordersDepressive disorders
Bipolar I disorderDisruptive mood dysregulation disorder
Bipolar II disorderMajor depressive disorder (single or recurrent episode)
Cyclothymic disorderPersistent depressive disorder (formerly dysthymia)
Substance/medication-induced bipolar and related disorderPremenstrual dysphoric disorder
Bipolar and related disorder due to another medical conditionSubstance/medication-induced depressive disorder
Other specified bipolar and related disorderDepressive disorder due to another medical condition
Unspecified bipolar and related disorderOther specified depressive disorder; unspecified depressive disorder
Unspecified mood disorder (listed in both chapters, new in DSM-5-TR)Unspecified mood disorder

The classification can be read as a decision procedure, and Figure 5.3 draws it. The order of the questions matters, because the manuals rank the episodes. A manic episode outranks everything: a person with one mania and twenty depressions has bipolar I disorder, not "major depressive disorder with one mania". Below mania comes the combination of hypomania and major depression, which makes bipolar II. Major depressive episodes with no history of elevation make major depressive disorder. Only when no full episode has ever occurred do the chronic, milder patterns come into play: two years of subthreshold highs and lows (cyclothymic disorder), or two years of depressed mood on more days than not (persistent depressive disorder). And at every step the same three things must be ruled out: a drug or medicine, a medical illness, and a psychotic disorder (for example, mood episodes that are part of schizoaffective disorder, Chapter 4).

Exam trap

DSM-5 has no "Mood Disorders" chapter: it split DSM-IV's chapter into bipolar and related disorders and depressive disorders, and placed the bipolar chapter between the psychotic and the depressive chapters as a bridge. Disruptive mood dysregulation disorder is a depressive disorder, not a bipolar one; cyclothymic disorder is bipolar; persistent depressive disorder merges dysthymia and chronic major depression. The only surviving "mood disorder" is the DSM-5-TR category unspecified mood disorder.

ICD-10 and ICD-11

The World Health Organization's ICD-10, approved in 1990 with its clinical guidelines published in 1992, places the mood (affective) disorders in the block F30 to F39. Its most important difference from DSM is that a single episode can itself be a diagnosis. F30 manic episode is used for a single episode only, and is divided into F30.0 hypomania, F30.1 mania without psychotic symptoms and F30.2 mania with psychotic symptoms. In ICD-10, mania lasts at least a week and is severe enough to disrupt ordinary work and social life more or less completely; hypomania lasts at least several days, and while it may interfere considerably with work or social activity, a severe or complete disruption makes the diagnosis mania. As soon as a second episode of any kind occurs, the diagnosis becomes bipolar affective disorder (F31), defined by repeated (at least two) episodes in which mood and activity are significantly disturbed, on some occasions by elevation (hypomania or mania) and on others by lowering (depression), usually with full recovery between them. ICD-10 has no separate code for bipolar II disorder, which it lists under F31.8, other bipolar affective disorders.

F32 depressive episode lasts at least about 2 weeks and is built from three typical symptoms (depressed mood, loss of interest and enjoyment, and reduced energy with increased fatigability) and seven other common ones, such as poor concentration, low self-esteem, guilt, a bleak view of the future, ideas of self-harm, disturbed sleep and poor appetite. It is graded mild, moderate or severe by counting these, a mild episode needing as few as four (two typical and two others); a severe episode may have psychotic symptoms (F32.3). Mild and moderate episodes are also coded with or without the somatic syndrome, ICD-10's version of the melancholic picture (loss of pleasure and reactivity, early morning waking, morning worsening, psychomotor change, loss of appetite, weight and libido). Section 5.2 sets out the grading and the syndrome in full. A second depressive episode, with no history of elevation, turns F32 into recurrent depressive disorder (F33).

ICD-10 then groups the chronic milder forms as F34 persistent mood (affective) disorders. F34.0 cyclothymia is a persistent instability of mood, with numerous periods of mild depression and mild elation, none severe or long enough to justify a diagnosis of bipolar affective disorder or recurrent depressive disorder. F34.1 dysthymia is a chronic depression of mood lasting at least several years (the research criteria ask for at least 2) that is not severe enough to count as recurrent depressive disorder. F38 collects other mood disorders, among them a single mixed affective episode (a mixture or rapid alternation of manic and depressive symptoms over at least 2 weeks) and recurrent brief depressive disorder; F39 is the unspecified category. One related condition lies outside the mood block altogether: mixed anxiety and depressive disorder (F41.2), placed among the neurotic disorders, is used when symptoms of both anxiety and depression are present but neither is severe enough to justify a diagnosis on its own. Because it straddles two classes, it is the disorder exam papers call the "hybrid" disorder; Section 5.3 discusses it.

ICD-11, approved by the World Health Assembly in May 2019 and in use for reporting from 1 January 2022, keeps mood disorders as the umbrella heading, as DSM-5 does not, but divides it into two groupings, much as DSM-5 does: bipolar or related disorders (6A60 bipolar type I disorder, 6A61 bipolar type II disorder, 6A62 cyclothymic disorder) and depressive disorders (6A70 single episode depressive disorder, 6A71 recurrent depressive disorder, 6A72 dysthymic disorder and 6A73 mixed depressive and anxiety disorder; premenstrual dysphoric disorder is coded in the chapter on the genitourinary system and cross-listed here).

Five changes from ICD-10 stand out. First, mood episodes are no longer diagnoses in their own right; their pattern over time decides the disorder, and a single manic or mixed episode is enough for bipolar type I. Second, bipolar type II at last has its own category, and the separate ICD-10 subgroup of persistent mood disorders is abolished, so dysthymia moves to the depressive disorders and cyclothymia to the bipolar ones. Third, the depressive episode now needs at least five of ten symptoms, grouped into three clusters (affective, cognitive-behavioural and neurovegetative), and at least one must be from the affective cluster, that is, depressed mood or diminished interest on almost every day for at least 2 weeks. Fatigue is no longer enough as an entry symptom, as reduced energy was in ICD-10, and hopelessness has been added because it predicts depression well. Severity is judged by the intensity of symptoms and the degree of impairment as well as their number, and psychotic symptoms can now be coded with moderate as well as severe episodes. Fourth, a manic episode, as in DSM-5, must include increased activity or a subjective sense of increased energy, and the mixed episode is kept. Fifth, the old somatic syndrome becomes a "with melancholia" qualifier, one of several that can be attached to an episode (others mark prominent anxiety, panic attacks, a depressive episode that has persisted for at least 2 years, a seasonal pattern and, in bipolar disorders, rapid cycling); and mixed depressive and anxiety disorder moves from the anxiety disorders into the depressive disorders. Table 5.4 compares the three systems.

Table 5.4 Mood disorders in ICD-10, ICD-11 and DSM-5-TR.

Exam trap

In ICD-10, F31 bipolar affective disorder needs at least two episodes; a first and only mania is F30. In ICD-11 and DSM-5-TR, a single mania (or, in ICD-11, a single mixed episode) is already bipolar type I. F34.0 is cyclothymia ("persistent instability of mood" between mild depression and mild elation) and F34.1 is dysthymia (persistent mild depression); an option describing alternating severe depression and mania is bipolar disorder, not either of these. The ICD-10 somatic syndrome corresponds to DSM's melancholic features. Mixed anxiety and depressive disorder is F41.2 in ICD-10, a depressive disorder in ICD-11, and absent from DSM-5-TR. Indian papers still ask ICD-10 codes, so learn F30 to F34 and F41.2.

Scan Your Brain

Fill in the blanks. The answers are below; cover them first.

  2. In grief, unlike a major depressive episode, is usually preserved.
  3. Jules Baillarger called alternating mania and melancholia folie à , and Jean-Pierre Falret called it folie circulaire, both in 1854.
  4. Kraepelin united the recurrent mood illnesses as insanity in 1899.
  5. The distinction between unipolar and bipolar mood disorders was proposed by in 1957.
  6. A major depressive episode needs five of nine symptoms over at least , one of them depressed mood or anhedonia.
  7. A manic episode needs three of the seven DIG FAST symptoms, or if the mood is only irritable.
  8. A hypomanic episode lasts at least consecutive days; if it has psychotic features it is, by definition, .

Answers: (1) affect, (2) self-esteem, (3) double forme, (4) manic-depressive, (5) Karl Leonhard, (6) 2 weeks, (7) four, (8) 4; manic, (9) mixed features, (10) somatic.

5.2 Major Depressive DisorderMedium yield · 8 Qs

Of all the conditions in this book, this is the one you are most likely to meet: in a hospital outpatient department, in a college counselling room, in your own family. The DSM-5-TR calls major depressive disorder (MDD) "the classic condition" among the depressive disorders. It is defined very simply: one or more major depressive episodes, and never a manic or hypomanic episode. Section 5.1 set the depressive episode beside mania and hypomania in a single table. Here we take the episode apart, one symptom at a time, and ask what each symptom looks like when a real person sits in front of you. Then we follow the disorder through a population (who gets it, and how many) and through a lifetime (how it begins, how it ends, and how often it comes back), and finally we look at how it presents in India, where a depressed person very often does not say that he or she is sad at all.

A first episode

Anand was thirty-one, an officer in a nationalised bank, recently promoted and transferred from his home town to a branch in Hubballi. His wife and four-year-old daughter had stayed behind until the school year ended. He came to the psychiatry outpatient department of the district hospital not because he felt sad but because, as he put it, "I am not sleeping and I have no strength." For about seven weeks he had been falling asleep normally and then waking at three in the morning, heart heavy, unable to get back to sleep. Food had lost its taste; he ate because his landlady put the plate in front of him, and his trousers had become loose (he had lost about five kilograms). He used to watch every India match on television; now the cricket was on and he did not know the score. At work he read the same loan file three or four times and still could not decide whether to sanction it. He had started to believe that the promotion was a mistake, that he was a fraud who would soon be found out, and that he had failed his wife by leaving her alone. "Sometimes I think it would be better for everyone if I did not wake up," he said, and then quickly added that he would never do anything to himself, because of his daughter.

The psychologist who saw him noticed other things. Anand sat very still. He answered after long pauses, in a low, flat voice, looking at the floor. When she asked what his daughter liked to do, his eyes filled with tears. Nothing in his history suggested an episode of excitement or reduced need for sleep; there was no alcohol use, and his thyroid tests and blood count, ordered by the physician, were normal.

Anand's story contains every element you will need in this section, so keep it in mind. Notice first that his presenting complaint was insomnia and fatigue, not sadness. The DSM-5-TR warns that this is common, and that a clinician who does not ask about the other symptoms will miss the diagnosis. Notice too that his symptoms clustered: low mood, lost pleasure, poor appetite and weight loss, early waking, slowed movement, exhaustion, worthlessness and guilt, poor concentration and indecision, and a passive wish not to wake up. That is all nine symptoms of a major depressive episode. Notice finally what the clinician checked before she made the diagnosis: that there had never been mania or hypomania, and that no drug or physical illness explained the picture. A depressive disorder marked, above all, by persistent sadness and loss of interest, with no history of mania, is what the examiners mean when they ask which disorder is "characterised by persistent sadness and loss of interest": the answer is depression, or more exactly major depressive disorder. Bipolar disorder can contain exactly the same depressive episodes, but it is defined by the manic or hypomanic episodes that MDD never has (Section 5.4).

The major depressive episode, symptom by symptom

The DSM-5-TR rule for a major depressive episode has three parts, and each part exists to rule something out. The first part is the symptom count. Five or more of nine symptoms must be present during the same two-week period, and at least one of the five must be either (1) depressed mood or (2) markedly diminished interest or pleasure. The second part is impairment: the symptoms must cause clinically significant distress or interfere with social, occupational or other important areas of functioning. The third part is an exclusion: the episode must not be attributable to the physiological effects of a substance (a drug of abuse or a medication) or to another medical condition. Why these rules?

  • Two weeks separates an episode from the ordinary low days that every life contains. A bad week after an exam result or a quarrel is not a disorder; a fortnight in which the low mood has not lifted on most days is a different matter.
  • Five of nine separates a syndrome (a cluster of symptoms that hang together and run a course) from a single complaint. Many people sleep badly or feel tired for two weeks; few have five of these symptoms together.
  • At least one core symptom stops the diagnosis being made from bodily symptoms alone. A man with cancer may lose weight, sleep poorly, feel exhausted and find it hard to concentrate, which is four symptoms, all of which the cancer could produce. Without either depressed mood or loss of pleasure, he does not have a depressive episode.
  • A change from previous functioning, present most of the day, nearly every day, rules out lifelong traits (the person who has always been a light sleeper or a pessimist) and passing bad moments. The ninth symptom is timed differently: thoughts of death and suicidal ideation need only be recurrent, and a suicide attempt or a specific suicide plan needs to have happened only once.
  • Distress or impairment is the line between suffering that is part of life and suffering that disables. In milder episodes, functioning may look normal but only with markedly increased effort.
  • Not due to a substance or medical condition sends depressions caused by alcohol, steroids, hypothyroidism or a stroke to their own diagnoses (Section 5.3).

Two further rules then turn an episode into a disorder. There must never have been a manic or hypomanic episode (if there has, the diagnosis is a bipolar disorder), and the episode must not be better explained by schizoaffective disorder or be superimposed on schizophrenia or another psychotic disorder (Chapter 4). Figure 5.4 shows the whole decision.

Now the symptoms themselves. It helps to sort them into three groups: the two core mood symptoms, four bodily symptoms (older books call them vegetative or neurovegetative, because they concern the basic functions of eating, sleeping, moving and energy), and three cognitive symptoms. The mnemonic you met in Section 5.1, SIG E CAPS (sleep, interest, guilt, energy, concentration, appetite, psychomotor, suicide), plus depressed mood, covers all nine.

Depressed mood. People describe it as feeling sad, empty, hopeless, discouraged or "down in the dumps". Some deny it at first, and it is elicited only when the interviewer gently points out that they look close to tears, as Anand did when his daughter was mentioned. Others say they feel "nothing", or only anxious, and the low mood must be inferred from the face and posture. Many people, especially men, show the mood as irritability: snapping at family, quick anger, blaming others, frustration over trivial things. In children and adolescents the criteria accept an irritable mood in place of a sad one, because a depressed twelve-year-old is often cranky rather than tearful.

Loss of interest or pleasure. This is anhedonia, from the Greek for "without pleasure", and it is nearly always present to some degree. People say they have "stopped caring", or that nothing they used to enjoy gives them anything any more. Families notice it first, as withdrawal from friends and the abandonment of hobbies: the cricket fan who no longer knows the score, the grandmother who stops going to the temple, the student who leaves the college music club. Sexual interest often falls. Anhedonia matters clinically as well as diagnostically: the DSM-5-TR notes that it has a particularly strong association with suicidal ideation.

Appetite and weight. Appetite can go either way. Many depressed people have to force themselves to eat; others eat more and crave sweets and other carbohydrates. A "significant" change means more than 5 percent of body weight in a month (about 3 kilograms for a 60-kilogram person) when the person is not dieting. In children, the equivalent is a failure to make the expected weight gain.

Sleep. Insomnia comes in three forms, named by when in the night it strikes. Difficulty falling asleep is initial insomnia; waking in the night and struggling to get back to sleep is middle insomnia; and terminal insomnia (early morning waking) is waking too early and being unable to sleep again. Depression typically produces middle or terminal insomnia, and Anand's three o'clock waking is the classic pattern; anxiety more often produces initial insomnia. The opposite, hypersomnia, means sleeping too much, either long nights or long daytime naps, and it is more common in young people and in the atypical form of depression (Section 5.3).

Psychomotor change. A slowing of speech, thought and movement is called psychomotor retardation: long pauses before answering, a soft monotonous voice, few words, slow walking, in extreme cases mutism. The opposite, psychomotor agitation, shows as an inability to sit still, pacing, hand-wringing, pulling or rubbing at the skin or clothes. The criterion insists that either must be observable by others; merely feeling slowed down or restless does not count. Psychomotor change is less common than fatigue or poor sleep, but when present it signals a more severe episode, and a person who shows one form often has a history of the other.

Fatigue or loss of energy. The smallest tasks seem to need enormous effort: washing and dressing take twice as long as usual, and the person feels exhausted without having done anything. This symptom accounts for much of the disability of depression, both during the episode and when remission is incomplete.

Worthlessness or guilt. The depressed person evaluates himself or herself unrealistically harshly, ruminates over small past failings, and reads neutral events as proof of personal defects ("my manager did not greet me, so he knows I am useless"). Guilt can reach delusional proportions, as in the person convinced that he is personally responsible for a flood or an epidemic. One detail is a favourite of examiners: blaming oneself for being ill, or for failing at work because of the depression, is very common and does not by itself meet the criterion unless it is delusional.

Poor concentration or indecision. People cannot read a page, follow a television serial or make even minor decisions; students may show a sudden drop in marks. In older adults, memory complaints may be the chief complaint and can be mistaken for early dementia, a picture traditionally called pseudodementia; it usually lifts when the depression is treated (Chapter 10 compares the two).

Thoughts of death or suicide. These range from a passive wish not to wake up, or a belief that others would be better off without one, through recurrent thoughts of suicide, to a specific plan or an attempt. Note the wording: the criterion is thoughts of death, not fear of dying. A person who has put his affairs in order, settled debts, acquired a rope or tablets and chosen a time and place is at very high risk. Sections 5.8 and 5.9 deal with suicide in full.

Table 5.5 gathers what each symptom sounds and looks like at interview.

Table 5.5 The nine symptoms of a major depressive episode as they appear at interview.
SymptomWhat the person says or showsRules and traps
1 Depressed mood (core)"Empty", "hopeless", tearful; may deny sadness at firstIrritable mood counts in children and adolescents
2 Loss of interest or pleasure (core)"Nothing gives me happiness"; drops hobbies, friends, sexStrongly linked to suicidal ideation
3 Appetite or weightFood tasteless, or craving sweets; clothes loose or tightMore than 5 percent of body weight in a month
4 SleepWaking at 3 a.m.; or sleeping 12 hoursMiddle and terminal insomnia are typical
5 Psychomotor changeLong pauses, soft voice; or pacing, hand-wringingMust be observable by others
6 Fatigue"No strength"; dressing takes twice as longCauses much of the disability
7 Worthlessness or guilt"I am a burden", "I am a fraud"Guilt about being ill does not count unless delusional
8 Concentration or decisionsRereads a page; cannot choose; marks fallIn the elderly may mimic dementia
9 Death or suicide"Better if I did not wake up"; a planThoughts of death, not fear of dying; an attempt or plan need occur only once

Depressed people commonly show other features that are not among the nine: tearfulness, brooding, anxiety, phobias, excessive worry about physical health, and pains such as headache, backache and abdominal pain. These are associated features. They are frequent and clinically important, but they do not count towards the five. Elation never counts: an excessively euphoric mood belongs to the opposite pole.

Exam trap

A major depressive episode needs five or more of nine symptoms (not three, four or seven, and not all nine) over at least 2 weeks (not 1 week, which is mania, not 4 days, which is hypomania, and not 1 month or 6 months), and one of the five must be depressed mood or loss of interest or pleasure. Asked which items in a list are symptoms of MDD, choose loss of interest and change in appetite; specific phobias are an anxiety disorder (Chapter 6) and excessively euphoric mood is a feature of mania. Phobias, anxiety, crying and pain are common in depression but are not criterion symptoms.

One more complication deserves mention because it is very common in Indian general hospitals: the depressed person who is also physically ill. Weight loss in untreated diabetes, fatigue in cancer, and sleep changes in pregnancy are identical to criterion symptoms. The DSM-5-TR's guidance is that such symptoms still count unless they are clearly and fully explained by the medical condition, and that the clinician should lean on the symptoms illness does not produce: low mood, anhedonia, guilt or worthlessness, poor concentration and suicidal thoughts. Definitions that use only these non-bodily symptoms pick out almost the same patients as the full criteria.

Severity, recurrence and the ICD-10 view

Once the clinician has decided that a major depressive episode is present, the diagnosis of MDD is described further by four features. The first is whether this is a single episode or recurrent. To count as recurrent, two episodes must be separated by at least two consecutive months in which the criteria for an episode were not met. The second is current severity, judged by the number of symptoms, their intensity and the degree of disability. A mild episode has few if any symptoms beyond the five required, the distress is manageable and the impairment minor; a severe episode has many more than five, the distress is unmanageable and functioning is markedly disrupted; moderate lies between. The third is whether there are psychotic features (delusions or hallucinations), which are coded whatever the severity. The fourth, used when the criteria are no longer fully met, is the state of remission. In partial remission, some symptoms remain, or the symptom-free period has lasted less than two months; in full remission, there have been no significant symptoms for the past two months. A full diagnosis therefore reads something like "major depressive disorder, recurrent episode, severe, with melancholic features". Anand's would be "single episode, severe", unless and until he has another.

The ICD-10, the classification used in most Indian hospital records and in many entrance papers, approaches the episode in a different way. It names three typical symptoms: depressed mood, loss of interest and enjoyment, and reduced energy leading to increased fatiguability and diminished activity. It then lists seven other common symptoms: reduced concentration and attention; reduced self-esteem and self-confidence; ideas of guilt and unworthiness; bleak and pessimistic views of the future; ideas or acts of self-harm or suicide; disturbed sleep; and diminished appetite. Like DSM, it asks for about two weeks. But it grades the episode by counting the two kinds of symptom separately, as Table 5.6 shows.

Table 5.6 How ICD-10 grades a depressive episode (F32). An episode lasts at least about 2 weeks.
EpisodeTypical symptoms (of 3)Other symptoms (of 7)Notes
Mild (F32.0)At least 2At least 2None intense; some difficulty with ordinary work
Moderate (F32.1)At least 2At least 3, preferably 4Considerable difficulty with work and home life
Severe without psychotic symptoms (F32.2)All 3At least 4, some severeLittle social or work activity is possible
Severe with psychotic symptoms (F32.3)All 3At least 4Plus delusions, hallucinations or depressive stupor

ICD-10 adds one more layer. Mild and moderate episodes are coded "with" or "without" the somatic syndrome, a cluster of symptoms with what ICD-10 calls special clinical significance, also known in older books as biological, vital, melancholic or endogenomorphic symptoms. There are eight: marked loss of interest or pleasure in normally enjoyable activities; lack of emotional reactivity to events that would normally cheer the person; waking two hours or more before the usual time; depression worse in the morning; objective psychomotor retardation or agitation noticed by others; marked loss of appetite; weight loss (often taken as 5 percent of body weight in the past month); and marked loss of libido. The syndrome is usually regarded as present when about four of the eight are definitely there. Severe episodes are presumed to have it almost always, so it is not coded separately for them. If the list looks familiar, it should: it is close to the DSM "with melancholic features" specifier described in Section 5.3, and both descend from the old idea of an "endogenous" depression arising from within (Section 5.1). ICD-10 codes a single episode as F32 and recurrent depressive disorder as F33; ICD-11 renames these single episode depressive disorder and recurrent depressive disorder and replaces the somatic syndrome with a "with melancholia" qualifier.

Exam trap

DSM-5-TR has two core symptoms (depressed mood, loss of interest or pleasure) and needs five symptoms in all; ICD-10 has three typical symptoms (adding reduced energy) and a mild episode needs only four symptoms in all (two typical plus two others). ICD-10's somatic syndrome corresponds to DSM's melancholic features, not to "somatic symptom disorder", which is a different chapter altogether (Chapter 7). Recurrent means a gap of at least 2 months between episodes.

Sadness, grief and the bereavement exclusion

Imagine a woman whose husband has died suddenly. For a month she cries most days, sleeps poorly, eats little, cannot concentrate and wonders aloud what the point of living is. Count the symptoms and she may well reach five. Is she ill? For more than thirty years psychiatry answered that question with a rule. From DSM-III (1980) onwards, a depressive syndrome after the death of a loved one was treated as uncomplicated bereavement rather than major depression, and in DSM-IV (1994) and DSM-IV-TR the rule was made precise: a person who met the criteria within two months of the death was not given a diagnosis of major depression, unless the picture included features that ordinary grief does not: marked functional impairment, morbid preoccupation with worthlessness, suicidal ideation, psychotic symptoms or psychomotor retardation. This was the bereavement exclusion.

DSM-5 (2013) removed it, and the reasons are worth knowing because the decision was fiercely debated. The work group, whose case was set out by Sidney Zisook and colleagues in 2012, made three arguments. First, depression is triggered by many losses (a divorce, a job loss, a diagnosis of cancer, a failed crop) and nobody proposed excluding those, so there was no logical reason to single out death. Second, when depressions that followed a bereavement were compared with depressions that followed other stressful events, they looked alike: Kenneth S. Kendler (born 1950), John Myers and Zisook found in 2008 that the two were similar on almost every measure they examined, including symptoms, duration, recurrence and family history. Third, dropping the exclusion was not expected to add large numbers of people to the depressed population. Above all, a full depressive episode after a death carries the same risks, including suicide, as any other, and the exclusion could leave such people untreated.

The critics were just as clear. Allen Frances (born 1942), who had chaired the DSM-IV task force, warned that DSM-5 would turn normal grief into a mental disorder and open the door to antidepressants for the recently bereaved. Jerome C. Wakefield and his colleagues had argued, using data from the American National Comorbidity Survey in 2007, that the logic should run the other way: uncomplicated depressive reactions to other major losses were as benign as uncomplicated grief, so the exclusion should be extended to them rather than abolished. The debate is really about where to draw the line between understandable sadness and disorder (the question Book 2, Section 1.6 asks about all abnormality), and DSM-5-TR settles it by asking for clinical judgement. A note to the criteria says that responses to a significant loss (bereavement, financial ruin, a natural disaster, serious illness) may include intense sadness, rumination, insomnia, poor appetite and weight loss resembling a depressive episode, and that the clinician must decide, using the person's history and the cultural norms for expressing distress, whether a depressive episode is present in addition to the normal response.

How does one decide? The DSM-5-TR gives a set of contrasts, gathered in Table 5.7. The single most useful is the pattern of the pain. Grief comes in waves, the pangs of grief, set off by reminders of the dead person, and between the waves the bereaved can laugh at a memory. The low mood of depression is pervasive and is not tied to any particular thought.

Table 5.7 Grief and a major depressive episode compared (after DSM-5-TR).
