FeatureGriefMajor depressive episode
Main feelingEmptiness and lossPersistent low mood; cannot anticipate pleasure
PatternWaves (pangs) set off by reminders; easing over days to weeksPervasive; not tied to particular thoughts
Positive emotionHumour and warmth possible between pangsPervasive unhappiness and misery
Thought contentPreoccupied with the dead person and memoriesSelf-critical, pessimistic ruminations
Self-esteemGenerally preservedWorthlessness and self-loathing common
Self-blameSpecific failings towards the dead ("I did not visit enough")Global ("I am worthless")
Thoughts of deathOf "joining" the dead personOf ending one's life because one is worthless or cannot bear the pain

Two pointers complete the picture. Grief that is unusually intense and long lasting, with persistent yearning for the dead person for at least twelve months in adults (six months in children), is now its own diagnosis, prolonged grief disorder, added in DSM-5-TR; it is taught in Chapter 7. The ordinary course of grief, and how a counsellor supports a grieving person, is in Book 2, Section 9.5.

Exam trap

The bereavement exclusion was removed in DSM-5 (2013), not introduced. Under DSM-IV it barred an MDD diagnosis for 2 months after a death unless severe features were present. Removing it does not mean that grief is a disorder: DSM-5-TR says bereavement does not usually induce a major depressive episode, and it asks the clinician to judge whether an episode is present in addition to the normal response. Prolonged grief disorder is a separate, newer diagnosis.

How common is depression, and who gets it?

In the 1970s the psychologist Martin E. P. Seligman (born 1942), whose work on learned helplessness you will meet in Section 5.6, called depression the "common cold of psychopathology", and the phrase, usually quoted as the common cold of psychiatry, has stuck. It captures one truth: depression is by far the most common of the serious mental disorders. It hides another. Critics have pointed out that the comparison suggests something frequent but mild, when in fact some depressions are chronic, disabling and fatal. Hold both truths together and you will not go wrong.

How common? The DSM-5-TR puts the 12-month prevalence of MDD in the United States at about 7 percent, three times higher among 18 to 29 year olds than among people aged 60 or more. Lifetime figures are much higher: the best estimates, from the American National Comorbidity Survey Replication (Ronald C. Kessler and colleagues, 2003) and similar studies, are that about 16 percent of people have MDD at some point in their lives, and estimates usually fall between 15 and 20 percent. Rates differ eight- to ninefold between regions of the world, partly because of real differences and partly because people in different cultures report distress differently. The World Health Organization's current fact sheet (September 2026, using figures for 2023) estimates that about 322 million people in the world have depression, 5.2 percent of adults. In 2017 the WHO described depression as the single largest contributor to disability worldwide, a position Christopher J. L. Murray and Alan D. Lopez had foreseen in their Global Burden of Disease study of 1996, which projected that by 2020 depression would be the second leading cause of disease burden in the world.

Is depression becoming more common? The evidence says yes, at least in some places and age groups. In the United States the prevalence rose between 2005 and 2015, and it rose most steeply among the young. Some of the rise may reflect greater willingness to report symptoms and better detection, but the pattern of later-born generations having more depression at earlier ages has been found repeatedly.

Now the most reproducible fact in the whole epidemiology of depression: women are affected more than men. In Western surveys the ratio is about two to one, and the DSM-5-TR notes that the gap is widest between menarche and menopause. In the WHO's worldwide estimate the ratio is smaller, about 1.5 to one, and estimates across studies run from about 1.5 to 3. The gap is not present in childhood, when boys and girls are depressed about equally often (if anything boys slightly more), and it opens dramatically at puberty; in old age it narrows again. Researchers have asked whether the difference is an artefact, the result of women being more willing to admit distress or to seek help, and found that this explains only a small part of it. The real explanations, taken up in Sections 5.5 and 5.6, include reproductive hormones, the much greater exposure of girls and women to sexual abuse and interpersonal violence, social roles with high demands and low control, and a ruminative style of responding to low mood (Susan Nolen-Hoeksema's theory). Men are not protected; they are more likely to show their depression in heavy drinking, risk taking and anger, and to die by suicide.

Who else is at risk? The DSM-5-TR lists a temperament high in negative affectivity (neuroticism), which makes people more likely to become depressed after stressful events; adverse childhood experiences, especially when there are several of different kinds; poverty, limited education and discrimination; chronic or disabling medical illness such as diabetes, obesity and heart disease; and a family history. First-degree relatives of people with MDD have a two- to fourfold higher risk than the general population, and heritability is about 40 percent (Section 5.5 discusses the genetics). Stressful life events are well recognised as triggers of episodes, especially first episodes.

In India, the National Mental Health Survey of 2015 to 2016 (carried out by NIMHANS; its design is described in Book 2, Section 1.10) found that depressive disorder affected 2.7 percent of adults at the time of the survey and 5.2 percent at some time in their lives: nearly 1 in 20. Depression was more common in women, in the 40 to 49 age group and in the urban metros. Nearly two thirds of those with a depressive disorder reported some disability in work, family or social life, and the survey team's own analysis of the depression data (B. A. Arvind and colleagues, 2019) put the treatment gap at 79 percent, which means that about eight in every ten depressed Indians were receiving no treatment. Table 5.8 collects these figures. The Indian ones are lower than the American ones, but the gap may partly be a matter of measurement: a structured interview that asks about sadness will miss people who express depression through the body, which is exactly what many Indian patients do, as the subsection on culture below explains.

Table 5.8 Major depressive disorder in numbers.
MeasureFigureSource
12-month prevalence, United StatesAbout 7 percentDSM-5-TR
Lifetime prevalenceAbout 16 percent (estimates 15 to 20)Kessler and colleagues 2003; Barlow and Durand
Women to menAbout 2 to 1 from adolescence; about 1.5 to 1 worldwideDSM-5-TR; WHO
People with depression worldwideAbout 322 million; 5.2 percent of adultsWHO fact sheet (2023 figures)
India, current and lifetime2.7 percent and 5.2 percent of adultsNational Mental Health Survey 2015 to 2016
India, treatment gapAbout 80 percent (79 percent)National Mental Health Survey 2015 to 2016 (Arvind and colleagues 2019)
Risk in first-degree relativesTwo to four times the general populationDSM-5-TR
Suicide risk in depressive illnessAbout 17 times the general population rateDSM-5-TR
Exam trap

Asked which statement least accurately describes depression, choose "men are more vulnerable to depression than women": the reverse is true, from adolescence onwards. The other usual options are all accurate: depression is widespread and its incidence is increasing (especially among the young), it is often triggered by a stressful event, and its severity is associated with suicide risk. The "common cold of psychiatry" is depression, not schizophrenia (lifetime risk under 1 percent), obsessive-compulsive disorder or hysteria (the old name for conversion and dissociative conditions, Chapter 7).

The course of depression

MDD can begin at any age, from early childhood to late life, but the chance of a first episode rises sharply at puberty, and in the United States new cases peak in the twenties. Onset may be sudden, within days of a stressful event, or gradual over weeks, as with Anand. An untreated episode typically lasts some months: most estimates fall between about four and nine. Recovery is the rule rather than the exception: the DSM-5-TR reports that recovery begins within three months for about 40 percent of people and within a year for about 80 percent. Recent onset is the best predictor of early recovery. Episodes with psychotic features, prominent anxiety, a personality disorder or severe symptoms recover more slowly.

The trouble with depression is not so much that episodes do not end as that they come back. A few terms help here. A relapse is the return of symptoms during the same episode, before full recovery has lasted long enough (usually within months of improvement); a recurrence is a new episode after full recovery. A diagnosis based on a single episode is possible, but MDD is a recurrent illness in the majority of cases. Between 35 and 85 percent of people who have a single episode go on to have a second, depending on the sample and the length of follow-up; one review put the risk of recurrence at about 20 percent in the first year after recovery and about 40 percent by the second. A widely quoted clinical rule of thumb is that after one episode the chance of another is about half, after two about 70 percent and after three about 90 percent. The median lifetime number of episodes among people with recurrent depression is somewhere between four and seven. Recurrence is more likely after a severe episode, in younger people, in those who have already had several episodes, and above all in those whose recovery was incomplete: even mild symptoms that persist during remission are a powerful predictor of the next episode. Why the risk should grow with each episode is the subject of the kindling hypothesis in Section 5.5.

Not every episode ends. About one in five people with a major depressive episode are still depressed two years later. Those who stay at or near the episode level for two years or more, and those who have lived for years in a milder but unremitting low mood, are now diagnosed with persistent depressive disorder (Section 5.3), and chronicity brings a higher chance of underlying personality, anxiety and substance use disorders and a lower chance of full recovery. That is why the DSM-5-TR advises asking every depressed person a simple question: "When was the last time you felt completely well for at least two months?" Figure 5.5 shows the main patterns of course over time, which you can compare with the bipolar life chart in Section 5.4.

Two other possible destinations matter. A substantial proportion of people who seem to have MDD turn out, when a manic or hypomanic episode eventually appears, to have bipolar disorder; many bipolar illnesses begin with one or more depressions. The switch is more likely when depression begins in adolescence, when it has psychotic features, when there is a family history of bipolar disorder, and when the episode has mixed features (Section 5.3). Less often, depression with psychotic features turns out to be the start of schizophrenia, a change that is much more frequent than the reverse. And depression shortens life. Much of the excess mortality is suicide, but not all of it: depression roughly doubles the risk of death in the years after a heart attack, and depressed older adults admitted to nursing homes are much more likely to die in their first year.

Clinical link

Because MDD so often recurs, treatment does not end when the person feels better. Guidelines advise continuing an antidepressant for several months after remission and, for people with repeated episodes, for much longer, and psychological treatments such as mindfulness-based cognitive therapy have been designed specifically to prevent recurrence (Book 2, Section 5.9; drug treatment in Section 5.7). Residual symptoms are not a minor detail to be tolerated: they are the strongest warning that another episode is coming.

Depression in India and across cultures

Saroj, forty-five, was brought by her son to a primary health centre in a village near Mandya. She had "full body pain", a burning sensation in her hands and feet, headaches, "gas" in the stomach and no strength in her limbs. She had seen four doctors in two years, had taken tonics, vitamin injections and painkillers, and had once been taken to a temple healer. When the doctor asked whether she was sad, she said no; she was only "tense", and she could not sleep. When the counsellor asked a different question, whether there was anything she still enjoyed, she was silent for a while and then began to cry. "What is there to enjoy? The crop failed, there is the loan, my husband drinks." Asked more, she described waking before dawn, eating little, feeling that she was a burden on her son, and sometimes wishing that God would take her.

Saroj had a major depressive episode, but she presented it through her body. Clinicians call this somatisation: the expression of psychological distress as physical complaints, with a search for medical help for them. It is not unique to India, and it is not a sign of a "less sophisticated" patient. The DSM-5-TR notes that a syndrome like MDD can be identified across very different cultures, but that the symptoms people lead with vary, and that complaints outside the formal criteria (loneliness, anger, crying and diffuse pain, along with many other bodily complaints) are common everywhere. Every culture also has its own idioms of distress, the words people use to talk about suffering. In South Asia "tension" is both an idiom and an explanation; the DSM-5-TR also lists the Indian idiom ashaktapanna, a feeling of weakness or loss of strength, among the neurasthenia-like syndromes. Among Punjabi migrants in Bedford, England, the anthropologist Inga-Britt Krause described in 1989 the complaint of a "sinking heart": physical sensations in the heart and chest attributed to heat, exhaustion, worry and social failure. The most famous study of this whole question was done in China.

Key study: Kleinman's neurasthenia patients in Hunan (1982)

Arthur Kleinman (born 1941), an American psychiatrist and anthropologist, studied 100 outpatients at the psychiatry clinic of Hunan Medical College who had been diagnosed with shenjing shuairuo, neurasthenia or "weakness of the nervous system", then one of the most common diagnoses in Chinese psychiatric clinics. They complained of headaches, dizziness, weakness, poor sleep and pain, not of sadness. Using a structured interview, Kleinman found that 87 of the 100 met the American DSM-III criteria for major depressive disorder, and 44 had previously undiagnosed chronic pain. When treated with antidepressants, 70 percent of the depressed patients improved substantially in their symptoms, but far fewer stopped seeking help or recovered their social and work functioning, problems that were tied to work, politics and family. Kleinman concluded, in a long paper in Culture, Medicine and Psychiatry (1982), that neurasthenia was best understood as a culturally shaped illness experience, a form of somatisation linked to depression and to locally accepted ways of expressing distress. The criticism is that he applied an American category to Chinese patients, the very "category fallacy" he had warned against in 1977; the lasting lesson is that the same suffering can wear different clothes.

Before the DSM made depression a matter of counting criteria, clinicians often spoke of masked depression, a term much used from the 1970s onwards for depression that presents behind a "mask" of bodily complaints (pain, fatigue, palpitations, digestive trouble) while the person denies or does not recognise low mood. The term is no longer an official diagnosis, and critics point out that the mask is often in the eye of the clinician: patients like Saroj are usually not hiding their sadness, they are leading with what they believe a doctor can treat, and they will speak about mood readily if asked in the right way. But the idea remains useful in Indian primary care, where depressed patients commonly present with aches, weakness, burning sensations, "gas" and sleeplessness, and where they are often given vitamins and painkillers rather than a diagnosis.

The family deserves a word too, because depression in India is rarely a private matter. Patients are usually brought by relatives, relatives often answer for them, and decisions about treatment are taken by the family together. This has costs: stigma, fears about a son's or daughter's marriage prospects, and pressure to keep the illness hidden may delay help for years. It also has strengths: families often provide the supervision, company and practical support that Western patients lack, and they are a natural ally in treatment. The social conditions in Saroj's story, debt, a failed crop and a husband who drinks, are not a background to her depression but part of its cause, a point Vikram Patel's research in Goa has made repeatedly (Section 5.3 describes one of his studies, and Section 5.6 the social theory of depression).

Indian context

In an Indian clinic, a depressed person may open with "body pain", "weakness", "burning", "gas", "tension" or "no sleep". After the physical complaints, always ask directly about mood, enjoyment, guilt, hopelessness and thoughts of death. Questions about enjoyment ("Is there anything you still look forward to?") often open the door that "Are you sad?" keeps shut. The family's account is invaluable, but see the client alone as well: some things, such as a husband's drinking, violence or a wish to die, will not be said in front of relatives.

Depression across the life span, and what travels with it

Depression in children looks different from depression in adults, although the criteria are the same apart from two allowances: an irritable mood can replace a sad one, and failure to make the expected weight gain can replace weight loss. Young children show depression through sad faces, irritability, tantrums, clinginess, problems with eating and sleeping, stomach aches and headaches, separation anxiety and refusal to go to school; school-age children through a fall in marks and withdrawal from friends. Prepubertal depression is relatively uncommon (about 1.5 percent of children aged 2 to 5 in one estimate) and affects boys and girls about equally. Adolescence changes everything: rates rise sharply, approaching adult levels, and the female majority appears. Adolescent depression is serious. In a New Zealand cohort of 1,265 young people, David M. Fergusson and Lianne Woodward (2002) found that the 13 percent who had MDD between 14 and 16 were later at much greater risk of further depression, anxiety disorders, nicotine dependence, substance misuse, suicide attempts, educational underachievement and early parenthood. Onset in adolescence also raises the chance that the illness will prove to be bipolar. Infancy has its own story, the anaclitic depression of infants separated from their mothers, which belongs to Section 5.3.

At the other end of life, full MDD is actually somewhat less common after 65 than in younger adults, but milder depressive syndromes are more common, and depression among older people in nursing homes and hospitals is very common. Late-life depression tends to show more sleep disturbance, bodily complaints, health worries, agitation and psychomotor change (melancholic symptoms become more frequent with age), and memory complaints that may be mistaken for dementia (pseudodementia, Chapter 10). It is often missed, because low mood is mistaken for a natural reaction to illness and loss. Death of a spouse, caring for an ill spouse and loss of independence through illness are among the strongest triggers. The danger is real: older adults, especially older men, have among the highest suicide rates of any group.

Depression rarely travels alone. The DSM-5-TR lists the disorders that most often accompany MDD: substance use disorders, panic disorder, generalised anxiety disorder, post-traumatic stress disorder, obsessive-compulsive disorder, anorexia and bulimia nervosa, and borderline personality disorder. Women more often have comorbid anxiety, bulimia and somatic symptom disorders; men more often have alcohol and drug problems. The partnership with anxiety is so close that some researchers think the two are one family of disorders. Lee Anna Clark and David Watson proposed in 1991 a tripartite model: anxiety and depression share a large general component of negative affect (distress, worry, irritability), while low positive affect (anhedonia, the loss of joy and interest) is specific to depression and physiological hyperarousal (a racing heart, trembling, breathlessness) is specific to anxiety. The model explains why the two so often occur together and also how they can be told apart: ask about pleasure and about bodily arousal. Depression that develops on top of another disorder tends to follow a more stubborn course, and lasting improvement may depend on treating the underlying condition too.

Finally, the conditions a clinician must rule out before settling on MDD, each of which is taught elsewhere in this chapter or book:

  • Bipolar disorder. Any past manic or hypomanic episode changes the diagnosis to bipolar I or II (Section 5.4), so the history must be searched for one, with the family's help.
  • Persistent depressive disorder, premenstrual dysphoric disorder and disruptive mood dysregulation disorder, distinguished by their duration, their timing in the menstrual cycle, and their chronic irritability in childhood respectively (Section 5.3).
  • Depression caused by a substance or a medical condition, such as cocaine withdrawal or hypothyroidism (Section 5.3).
  • Adjustment disorder with depressed mood, in which a stressor is followed by depressive symptoms that do not meet the full criteria for an episode (Chapter 7).
  • Psychotic disorders. In schizoaffective disorder, delusions or hallucinations persist for at least two weeks without a mood episode (Chapter 4).
  • Attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder in children, whose distractibility and low frustration tolerance can mimic depression (Chapter 3).
  • Ordinary sadness and grief, which do not meet the criteria for severity, duration and impairment.
Clinical link

Before an antidepressant is started, ask about past highs: periods of days when the person needed little sleep, talked fast, spent wildly or felt unusually confident. An antidepressant given alone to someone who actually has bipolar disorder can trigger a switch into mania (Section 5.7). Asking a relative is often more revealing than asking the client, who may remember a hypomanic week simply as "the time I was finally well".

Exam trap

In children and adolescents, depressed mood can appear as irritability; that allowance is part of the MDD criteria and does not by itself suggest mania. The sex ratio of depression is about equal before puberty and about two women to one man after it. Pseudodementia is depression mimicking dementia, not a kind of dementia.

5.3 The Other Depressive Disorders and the SpecifiersHigh yield · 15 Qs

Major depressive disorder is the centre of the depressive family, but it has relatives. The DSM-5-TR introduces the whole group with a sentence worth memorising: all the depressive disorders share "sad, empty, or irritable mood, accompanied by related changes that significantly affect the individual's capacity to function", and "what differs among them are issues of duration, timing, or presumed etiology". Hold on to those three words and the chapter organises itself. Persistent depressive disorder differs from MDD in duration: it lasts years. Premenstrual dysphoric disorder differs in timing: it is tied to the menstrual cycle. Substance/medication-induced depressive disorder and depressive disorder due to another medical condition differ in cause. Disruptive mood dysregulation disorder, the newest member, differs in age and in its mood (irritability rather than sadness). After these come the leftover categories, the "hybrid" disorder of mixed anxiety and depression in ICD, and an old idea that every examiner still asks about, anaclitic depression. The second half of the section turns to the specifiers, the labels that describe what kind of episode a person is having and that often decide what treatment will work.

Persistent depressive disorder (dysthymia)

Pooja was twenty-four, a postgraduate student, when she first came for help, and her opening words were "I have always been like this." Since about the age of fourteen she had felt low on most days. She was tired all the time, slept late and long, and comforted herself with food. She thought herself plain, dull and less capable than her classmates, although her marks said otherwise, and she was quietly sure that her life would not get better. She could remember good spells, a holiday, a few weeks after a school prize, but never more than a month or so of feeling truly well. She had never been out of bed for days with excitement or needed less sleep than usual. The year before, after her father's heart attack and a failed attempt at the national eligibility test, things had become much worse for about three months: she lost all interest in everything, could hardly eat, woke at dawn and began to think about taking her mother's sleeping tablets. That had passed with treatment, and she had returned, not to happiness, but to her old grey mood.

Pooja's long, low-grade depression has a long history of names. Kraepelin spoke of a "depressive temperament", and DSM-II (1968) of "depressive neurosis". DSM-III (1980) brought an old word, dysthymia (from Greek roots meaning "ill" and "state of mind"), into official use as dysthymic disorder, a chronic mild depression lasting at least two years. Research then showed that the line between dysthymic disorder and chronic major depression was hard to draw and did not seem to matter: the two did not differ clearly in development, course or family history. DSM-5 (2013) therefore merged them into a single diagnosis, persistent depressive disorder (PDD), which "represents a consolidation of DSM-IV-defined chronic major depressive disorder and dysthymic disorder". The word dysthymia survives in brackets in DSM and as a diagnosis in its own right in ICD.

The criteria fall into four groups.

  • The mood and its duration. Depressed mood for most of the day, for more days than not, for at least 2 years, reported by the person or observed by others. In children and adolescents the mood can be irritable and the duration need only be 1 year, because a year is a large fraction of a child's life and childhood moods change quickly.
  • Two or more of six symptoms while depressed: poor appetite or overeating; insomnia or hypersomnia; low energy or fatigue; low self-esteem; poor concentration or difficulty making decisions; and feelings of hopelessness. A common mnemonic is HE'S 2 SAD: Hopelessness, Energy, Sleep, 2 years, Self-esteem, Appetite, Decisions.
  • Continuity. During the two years (one in the young) the person has never been without the symptoms for more than 2 months at a time. This is what makes the disorder persistent rather than recurrent. Criteria for a major depressive episode may be continuously present for the whole two years; that clause is how the old chronic major depression was absorbed.
  • Exclusions. There has never been a manic or hypomanic episode (that would make it bipolar), and the criteria for cyclothymic disorder have never been met; the symptoms are not better explained by a chronic psychotic disorder and are not caused by a substance or a medical condition such as hypothyroidism; and they cause distress or impairment.

Notice what the list of six leaves out compared with the nine symptoms of a major episode: anhedonia, psychomotor change, guilt and suicidal thoughts. PDD is built around the quiet, wearing symptoms that people learn to live with. That is also why it is so often missed. Because the symptoms have become part of the person's ordinary experience, especially when the onset was early, people may not mention them unless asked directly; they think "this is just my nature", and so do their families. Clinicians once called such people depressive personalities.

The specifiers of PDD describe its history. Onset before age 21 is called early onset, and onset at 21 or later late onset; early onset, like Pooja's, is associated with greater chronicity, a poorer response to treatment, a stronger family history and more personality and substance use disorders. Four further specifiers describe the most recent two years: pure dysthymic syndrome (no full major depressive episode in that time), with persistent major depressive episode (full criteria met throughout), with intermittent major depressive episodes, with current episode, and with intermittent major depressive episodes, without current episode. Severity (mild, moderate, severe) and two of the specifiers described later in this section, anxious distress and atypical features, can also be added.

Pooja's year of severe depression on top of her long-standing low mood has its own name, double depression: a major depressive episode superimposed on dysthymia. When the episode ends, the person returns not to normal but to the dysthymic baseline (Figure 5.5). In DSM-5-TR terms Pooja has persistent depressive disorder, early onset, with intermittent major depressive episodes, and when the episode was present she also met criteria for major depressive disorder, which DSM allows to be diagnosed alongside it.

Key study: "Double depression" (Keller and Shapiro, 1982)

Martin B. Keller and Robert W. Shapiro examined 101 patients who met the criteria for major depressive disorder in a large American collaborative study of depression and asked how many had been depressed before the episode began. Twenty-six percent had an underlying chronic depressive disorder lasting at least two years, on which the acute episode was superimposed. Whether these patients "recovered" depended on how recovery was defined: many recovered from the acute episode, but far fewer recovered from both disorders. And once the acute episode lifted, they relapsed into a new episode at a significantly higher rate than patients without the chronic background. In a two-year follow-up (Keller and colleagues, 1983), 61 percent of patients with double depression had still not recovered from the underlying chronic depression. The study coined a term and established a point that every clinician needs: a patient who is "better" may simply be back at a long-standing low baseline, and that baseline needs treatment too.

How common and how long? The DSM-5-TR puts the 12-month prevalence in the United States at about 0.5 percent for dysthymic disorder and 1.5 percent for chronic major depression, with women about 1.5 and 2 times more often affected respectively; for the merged category, estimates of about 3.5 percent for both lifetime and past-year prevalence have been reported. The near equality of lifetime and past-year figures tells you that the disorder, once present, tends to last. Onset is usually early and insidious, in childhood, adolescence or early adulthood. The median duration in adults is about five years, and some people remain dysthymic for twenty or thirty years. As many as 79 percent of people with dysthymia have a major depressive episode at some point. In a ten-year follow-up of 97 adults with dysthymia, Daniel N. Klein and colleagues (2006) found that 74 percent recovered at some point, but 71 percent of those relapsed, and the group spent about 60 percent of the ten years meeting full criteria for a mood disorder, against 21 percent for a comparison group with non-chronic major depression.

So is PDD a milder illness than MDD or a worse one? Both answers are defensible, and you need to know why. Symptom by symptom it is milder: fewer symptoms are required (two of six, with depressed mood, rather than five of nine), and they are usually less intense. That is the textbook contrast and the answer the examiners want: dysthymia is less severe but lasts longer. Taken as a whole, though, the chronic course makes it at least as damaging. People with PDD have more comorbid anxiety, substance use and personality disorders (borderline personality disorder is a particularly robust risk factor, and early onset is strongly associated with Cluster B and C personality disorders), respond less well to treatment, improve more slowly, carry an elevated risk of suicide, and can be as impaired in work and relationships as people with MDD, or more so.

ICD-10 places dysthymia (F34.1) with cyclothymia (F34.0) under persistent mood (affective) disorders (F34). ICD-10 defines dysthymia as a chronic depression of mood, lasting at least several years, that is not severe enough to meet the criteria for even a mild or moderate recurrent depressive disorder, and cyclothymia as a persistent instability of mood involving numerous periods of mild depression and mild elation. ICD-11 keeps dysthymic disorder as a separate diagnosis rather than merging it as DSM did: the depressed mood must last two years or more, there must have been no two-week period meeting the requirements for a depressive episode during the first two years, and there must be no history of manic, mixed or hypomanic episodes. Table 5.9 sets the three chronic or episodic depressive patterns side by side.

Table 5.9 Major depressive disorder, persistent depressive disorder and cyclothymic disorder (DSM-5-TR).
FeatureMajor depressive disorderPersistent depressive disorderCyclothymic disorder
Minimum duration2 weeks per episode2 years (1 year in the young)2 years (1 year in the young)
Symptoms needed5 of 9, one coreDepressed mood plus 2 of 6Numerous hypomanic and depressive symptoms, never a full episode
Well intervalsOften full recovery between episodesNever symptom-free more than 2 monthsNever symptom-free more than 2 months
PolesDepression onlyDepression onlyBoth, mildly (Section 5.4)
Typical severityModerate to severe episodesMilder symptoms, longer courseMild swings, long course
Exam trap

Dysthymia (PDD) needs depressed mood on more days than not for at least 2 years in adults and 1 year in children and adolescents (not 6 months, 1 year in adults or 5 years), with 2 or more of 6 symptoms and no gap longer than 2 months. Compared with MDD its symptoms are less severe but last longer; it does not involve hallucinations or delusions as a defining feature, and "only women in the winter months" is a garbled version of the seasonal pattern specifier (below), not dysthymia. Two options often set beside dysthymia describe cyclothymia: "persistent instability of mood between mild depression and mild elation" (the ICD-10 wording) and "persistent mild depression and hypomania for more than 2 years". Severe depression alternating with mania is bipolar disorder; bipolar II needs a hypomanic and a major depressive episode; premenstrual dysphoric disorder is tied to the menstrual cycle.

Disruptive mood dysregulation disorder

In the 1990s and early 2000s something strange happened in American child psychiatry. The number of children diagnosed with bipolar disorder exploded. Using a national survey of doctors' office visits, Carmen Moreno and colleagues reported in 2007 that visits by young people aged 19 or under carrying a bipolar diagnosis had risen from 25 per 100,000 of the population in 1994 to 1995 to 1,003 per 100,000 in 2002 to 2003, roughly a fortyfold increase, while the adult rate had less than doubled. Two thirds of the young patients were boys, a third also had ADHD, and nine in ten received psychiatric drugs, often powerful antipsychotics and mood stabilisers.

Most of these children did not have the episodes of elevated mood, grandiosity and reduced sleep that define mania. What they had was severe, chronic, non-episodic irritability: they were angry and touchy almost all the time and exploded into rages several times a week. Some researchers argued that this was what mania looked like in children. Others were not convinced. At the US National Institute of Mental Health, Ellen Leibenluft and colleagues proposed in 2003 a set of phenotypes of juvenile mania; the broadest, a chronic non-episodic illness of severe irritability and hyperarousal, they went on to study under the name severe mood dysregulation. When such children were followed up, they did not grow into bipolar adults: they developed unipolar depression and anxiety disorders, and their families did not carry the high rates of bipolar disorder that a true bipolar illness would predict. DSM-5 (2013) gave these children their own diagnosis, disruptive mood dysregulation disorder (DMDD), and placed it among the depressive disorders, precisely because of where the children end up.

Picture a nine-year-old boy in Class 4 whose mother says she is "walking on eggshells". Told to switch off the television, he screams, kicks the door and throws his school bag; at school he has torn up a classmate's notebook and hit a teacher's assistant; even on calm days he is sullen, snappish and easily set off, and his teachers describe him as "always angry". This has been going on since he was about six. The DMDD criteria capture exactly this picture, and they fall into five groups.

  • The outbursts. Severe, recurrent temper outbursts, verbal (rages) or behavioural (aggression towards people or property), grossly out of proportion in intensity or duration to the provocation, inconsistent with the child's developmental level, and occurring on average three or more times a week.
  • The mood between outbursts. Persistently irritable or angry most of the day, nearly every day, and observable by others: parents, teachers, peers. This is the feature that separates DMDD from a child who is fine between tantrums.
  • Time. All of this for 12 months or more, with no period of 3 or more consecutive months free of it.
  • Place. Outbursts and irritable mood in at least two of three settings (home, school, with peers) and severe in at least one, so that the problem belongs to the child and not to one relationship or classroom.
  • Age. Onset before age 10, and the diagnosis made for the first time no earlier than 6 and no later than 18. The lower limit exists because tantrums are developmentally normal in toddlers; the upper limit because the disorder's validity has only been established in children and adolescents.

Then come the exclusions, and these are what make DMDD tricky in multiple-choice questions. There must never have been a distinct period of more than a day in which the full symptoms of a manic or hypomanic episode (apart from duration) were present: DMDD is chronic, mania is episodic. The behaviour must not occur only during a major depressive episode, and must not be better explained by autism (when routines are disturbed), post-traumatic stress disorder, separation anxiety or PDD. DMDD cannot coexist with oppositional defiant disorder, intermittent explosive disorder or bipolar disorder. If a child meets the criteria for both DMDD and ODD, only DMDD is diagnosed; if a child has ever had mania or hypomania, DMDD is ruled out. It can coexist with ADHD, MDD, conduct disorder and substance use disorders. The overlap with ODD is lopsided: most children with DMDD also meet the criteria for ODD, but only about 15 percent of children with ODD meet the criteria for DMDD, because DMDD demands both severe outbursts and a persistently disturbed mood. Table 5.10 compares the conditions it must be told apart from, and Figure 5.6 shows the age rules that examiners test most often.

Table 5.10 Disruptive mood dysregulation disorder and its neighbours.
FeatureDMDDOppositional defiant disorderIntermittent explosive disorderBipolar disorder in a child
Mood between outburstsPersistently irritable or angryAngry or irritable mood is one possible featureNormalUsual self between episodes
CourseChronic, non-episodicChronicRecurrent outburstsDistinct episodes
Minimum duration12 months6 months3 months (for frequent minor outbursts)Manic episode 1 week
Elation, grandiosityAbsentAbsentAbsentCharacteristic
With DMDD?Not applicableNever both: diagnose DMDDNever bothNever both: diagnose bipolar

How common is it? Community estimates are uncertain: 2.5 percent of 11-year-olds in a Brazilian birth cohort, and 8.2 percent of 6-year-olds in one American sample. Clinic samples are mostly boys. About half of children with DMDD still meet the criteria a year later, and those who do not usually remain irritable. Conversion to bipolar disorder is very rare; the risk instead is adult depression and anxiety. The diagnosis has its critics. Allen Frances counted it among DSM-5's worst decisions, warning that it would turn ordinary temper tantrums into a mental disorder and simply move children from one drug-prescribing label to another. The ICD-11 chose a different route: it has no DMDD, and instead allows oppositional defiant disorder to be specified as with chronic irritability-anger.

Exam trap

A 12-year-old boy with new temper outbursts out of proportion to the situation cannot have DMDD, because onset must be before age 10. Bipolar disorder (if there are distinct episodes), conduct disorder and ADHD remain possible (Chapters 3 and 11). Do not confuse the three ages: onset before 10; first diagnosis not before 6 and not after 18. The number 12 appears in some descriptions because DSM's introduction speaks of children "up to 12 years of age", but it is not the onset rule. And DMDD is not a precursor of bipolar disorder, whatever some coaching notes say: it predicts later depression and anxiety, which is why it sits among the depressive disorders.

Premenstrual dysphoric disorder

Many women notice some change in the days before a period: bloating, breast tenderness, food cravings, a shorter temper. About 20 percent have enough of these to be described as having premenstrual syndrome (PMS), which needs no particular number of symptoms and no mood symptom. A much smaller group has something different in kind: for a week or so before each period they become markedly depressed, anxious, tearful or irritable, quarrel with partners and children, cannot work, and then, within a few days of the period starting, return to themselves. This is premenstrual dysphoric disorder (PMDD).

PMDD has had a long and contested road into the manual. It first appeared in the appendix of DSM-III-R (1987) as "late luteal phase dysphoric disorder", the name referring to the luteal phase, the second half of the menstrual cycle, between ovulation and menstruation. DSM-IV (1994) renamed it premenstrual dysphoric disorder but kept it in an appendix of criteria sets for further study. Critics, many of them feminist psychologists, objected that it risked labelling a normal biological process as illness and stigmatising women in general. After almost twenty more years of research had confirmed a specific, treatment-responsive condition that begins after ovulation, remits within a few days of menses and markedly impairs functioning, DSM-5 (2013) moved PMDD into the main text as a depressive disorder.

The criteria are built to answer the critics. In most menstrual cycles of the past year, at least five symptoms must be present in the final week before menses, start to improve within a few days after menses begin, and become minimal or absent in the week after. At least one of the five must be one of four affective symptoms: marked mood swings (sudden sadness, tearfulness or sensitivity to rejection), marked irritability or anger, marked depressed mood or hopelessness, or marked anxiety and tension. The remainder can come from a further seven: decreased interest, poor concentration, lethargy, appetite change or food cravings, sleeping too much or too little, feeling overwhelmed or out of control, and physical symptoms such as breast tenderness, joint or muscle pain, bloating or weight gain. Physical symptoms alone, however troublesome, do not make PMDD. The symptoms must cause clinically significant distress or interference, must not be merely a premenstrual worsening of another disorder such as MDD, panic disorder, PDD or a personality disorder (though PMDD can co-occur with any of them), and must not be due to a substance or medical condition.

One criterion is unique: the pattern must be confirmed by prospective daily ratings during at least two symptomatic cycles, and until then the diagnosis is recorded as provisional. Why insist on a diary? Because memory is unreliable here. The start of a period is a memorable event, and many women with MDD, PDD or bipolar disorder believe their symptoms are premenstrual, only to find, when they chart them day by day, that the symptoms do not follow the cycle at all. (Rating forms such as the Daily Record of Severity of Problems are described in the Assessment book.)

The 12-month prevalence has been estimated at 5.8 percent in a large German community study, while the most rigorous American estimate, using daily ratings over two cycles and requiring impairment and no other mental disorder, was 1.3 percent. Retrospective reports always give higher figures than prospective ones. PMDD can begin at any time after menarche and ends at menopause, although cyclical hormone replacement can bring it back. It has been described in the United States, Europe, India, Nigeria, Brazil and elsewhere in Asia. Three conditions must be told apart: PMS (fewer symptoms, no required mood symptom, milder); dysmenorrhoea (painful periods, whose symptoms begin with menses rather than before them); and symptoms that began with hormonal contraceptives and disappear when the hormones are stopped, which make a substance/medication-induced depressive disorder. Selective serotonin reuptake inhibitors are an effective treatment, and unusually they can work when taken only in the luteal half of the cycle (Section 5.7 describes the drugs). ICD-11 lists premenstrual dysphoric disorder in its chapter on diseases of the genitourinary system, with a cross-reference from the depressive disorders.

Exam trap

PMDD was moved from the appendix into the main text in DSM-5; it is a depressive disorder. It needs five symptoms, at least one of them affective (lability, irritability, depressed mood or anxiety), in the final week before menses, improving within a few days of onset and minimal in the week after, confirmed by prospective daily ratings over two cycles. Premenstrual syndrome is not PMDD, and "symptoms only in winter" is the seasonal pattern, not PMDD.

Depression with a known cause: substances and medical conditions

Sometimes the cause of a depression can be named. A substance/medication-induced depressive disorder is diagnosed when a prominent and persistent depressed mood or loss of interest develops during or soon after intoxication with, or withdrawal from, a substance, or after starting or stopping a medication, and the substance is capable of producing it. Two families of drugs of abuse are most often responsible: depressants during intoxication (alcohol, benzodiazepines and other sedatives) and stimulants during withdrawal (the "crash" after cocaine or amphetamines). Among medications, steroids, the antihypertensives clonidine, methyldopa and reserpine, interferon and L-dopa are especially likely to cause depression, and many others have been implicated, including isotretinoin for acne, oral contraceptives and other hormonal agents, some anticonvulsants, the HIV drug efavirenz and the smoking-cessation drug varenicline. Reserpine has a special place in the history of psychiatry: its tendency to cause depression helped launch the monoamine theory (Section 5.5).

The difficult question is always whether the substance caused the depression or merely accompanied an independent one. The DSM-5-TR gives three clues that point to an independent depressive disorder: the depression began before the substance use; it persists for a substantial period, about a month, after the end of acute withdrawal or severe intoxication; or there is other evidence, such as a history of depressions unrelated to substances. In people with alcohol and stimulant use disorders, substance-induced depressive episodes are very common (40 percent or more over a lifetime), so the question arises daily in de-addiction wards; Chapter 10 discusses it further.

A depressive disorder due to another medical condition is diagnosed when the history, examination or laboratory findings show that the depression is the direct physiological consequence of an illness, not a psychological reaction to it (a person who becomes depressed on learning she has cancer has an adjustment disorder or MDD, not this diagnosis). The best-supported causes are stroke, Huntington's disease, Parkinson's disease, traumatic brain injury and other focal brain lesions, hypothyroidism, Cushing's syndrome and pancreatic cancer; systemic lupus erythematosus, vitamin B12 deficiency and multiple sclerosis are also linked. Some details are examinable. After a stroke, depression often begins within days, lasts nine to eleven months on average, and is most likely after left frontal lesions. In Huntington's and Parkinson's disease, depression often precedes the movement and cognitive symptoms; in Huntington's it is considered the first neuropsychiatric symptom. In hypothyroidism, Cushing's syndrome and pancreatic cancer, depression can be the first sign of the disease. The diagnosis is specified as with depressive features, with major depressive-like episode, or with mixed features.

Clinical link

This is why a careful work-up of a first depression includes a medical history, a list of current medicines, questions about alcohol and drugs, and simple tests such as thyroid function and a blood count, as Anand's physician ordered. A psychologist who finds a new depression in a middle-aged person with tremor, weight gain and cold intolerance, or a sudden depression after starting a blood-pressure tablet, should refer for medical review before starting a course of therapy.

The remaining categories and the "hybrid" disorder

Some depressions are real and impairing but do not fit any of the named disorders. DSM-5-TR offers other specified depressive disorder, in which the clinician records the reason, and gives four examples. The first, recurrent brief depression, described by Jules Angst and colleagues in the Zurich cohort study (1990), is depressed mood with at least four other depressive symptoms lasting 2 to 13 days, at least once a month (not tied to the menstrual cycle), for at least 12 consecutive months. A short-duration depressive episode is depressed mood with at least four other symptoms lasting 4 to 13 days, short of the two weeks a major episode needs. A depressive episode with insufficient symptoms is depressed mood plus at least one other symptom for two weeks. And a major depressive episode superimposed on schizophrenia or another psychotic disorder is also recorded here. Unspecified depressive disorder is used when the clinician cannot or chooses not to say why the criteria are not met, as in an emergency department. DSM-5-TR also lists an unspecified mood disorder for presentations, such as acute agitation, in which even the choice between a bipolar and a depressive disorder cannot yet be made.

Now the "hybrid". Primary care clinicians all over the world see people who are a little anxious and a little depressed, tense, worried, low and tired, with palpitations and poor sleep, but who meet the full criteria for neither an anxiety disorder nor a depressive episode. The ICD-10 gave them a diagnosis, mixed anxiety and depressive disorder (F41.2), placed among the "other anxiety disorders": symptoms of both anxiety and depression are present, neither clearly predominates, and neither is severe enough to justify a separate diagnosis; some autonomic symptoms (tremor, palpitations, dry mouth, churning stomach) must be present. Because it combines two diagnostic families in one category, it is the disorder textbooks and examiners call the hybrid disorder. DSM-IV placed a version in its appendix for further study; DSM-5 considered it during development and left it out, partly because clinicians could not diagnose it reliably in the field trials, and instead introduced the "with anxious distress" specifier (below). ICD-11 kept it, renamed mixed depressive and anxiety disorder and moved it into the depressive disorders, which, as you saw, is where the shared negative affect of the tripartite model would suggest it belongs.

Exam trap

The "hybrid disorder" is mixed anxiety and depressive disorder: ICD-10 F41.2 (an anxiety disorder there), ICD-11 mixed depressive and anxiety disorder (a depressive disorder there), absent from DSM-5-TR. The usual distractors are other conditions: multiple personality disorder is the old name for dissociative identity disorder (Chapter 7), and borderline and schizoid personality disorders belong to Chapter 11.

Anaclitic depression and hospitalism

Can a baby be depressed? The first person to argue convincingly that one can was René Árpád Spitz (1887 to 1974), a Viennese-born psychoanalyst who emigrated to the United States and filmed and observed infants in institutions. In 1945 he described hospitalism, the devastating decline of infants raised in a foundling home where food, hygiene and medical care were adequate but each nurse looked after many babies and there was almost no individual mothering. The infants became withdrawn and passive, their development fell steadily behind, they were unusually vulnerable to infection, and many died. In 1946, working with Katherine Wolf, he described a related but reversible condition in a nursery attached to a women's prison, where mothers ordinarily cared for their own babies. Infants who had enjoyed a good relationship with their mothers and were separated from them for some months in the second half of the first year became weepy and demanding, then withdrawn; they lost weight, slept poorly, stopped developing and lay with a frozen, far-away expression. If the mother returned within a few months the babies recovered, often strikingly; if the separation went on, the picture slid towards hospitalism. Spitz called this anaclitic depression, from the Greek for "leaning upon", because the depression followed the loss of the person on whom the infant leaned.

Spitz's observations, along with those of John Bowlby (Section 5.6), changed the care of children in hospitals and orphanages, and they show the theme of loss that runs through the psychological theories of depression. Today the long-term effects of severe early deprivation are described by the attachment and trauma diagnoses (Chapter 7) and by developmental psychology, but "anaclitic depression" and "hospitalism" remain standard examination terms.

Exam trap

Anaclitic depression (1946) and hospitalism (1945) are René Spitz's terms. The usual distractors are known for other things: Jean Piaget for the stages of cognitive development, B. F. Skinner for operant conditioning, and Hans Eysenck for his dimensions of personality (extraversion, neuroticism, psychoticism). Do not confuse Spitz's infant syndrome with Sidney Blatt's later (1974) use of "anaclitic" for an adult depression centred on dependency and loss, which he contrasted with "introjective" depression centred on self-criticism.

The specifiers: anxious distress and mixed features

Two people can both have "major depressive disorder, recurrent, moderate" and yet look, and respond to treatment, quite differently. One sleeps twelve hours a day and craves sweets; the other wakes at three and cannot eat. One is calm and slowed; the other paces the ward, terrified that something awful is about to happen. One hears a voice calling her a sinner; one became ill three weeks after giving birth; one is ill every winter. The DSM handles these differences with specifiers. A specifier is a label added to a diagnosis that describes the current or most recent episode, or the pattern of episodes over time, without creating a new disorder. Most of them were chosen because they predict something useful, the course, the suicide risk or the treatment most likely to work. Table 5.11 lists them all; the rest of this section explains each in turn.

Table 5.11 The specifiers for depressive and bipolar disorders (DSM-5-TR).
SpecifierDefining featuresApplies toWhat it predicts or changes
With anxious distress2 or more of 5: keyed up, restless, worry spoils concentration, fear of something awful, fear of losing controlMDD, PDD, bipolar I and II, cyclothymiaHigher suicide risk, longer illness, poorer response
With mixed features3 or more symptoms of the opposite pole during most days of the episodeDepressive, manic or hypomanic episodesIn depression, raised risk of later bipolar disorder; care with antidepressants
With melancholic featuresNo pleasure or no mood reactivity, plus 3 of 6 (despair, worse in morning, early waking, psychomotor change, weight loss, guilt)Major depressive episodesSevere, often inpatient; good response to physical treatments
With atypical featuresMood reactivity plus 2 of 4 (weight gain, hypersomnia, leaden paralysis, rejection sensitivity)Major depressive episodes, PDDEarlier onset, more women; historically better response to MAOIs
With psychotic featuresDelusions or hallucinations, mood-congruent or mood-incongruentMajor depressive and manic episodesSevere; antidepressant plus antipsychotic, or ECT
With catatoniaCatatonic features for most of the episode (Chapter 4)Major depressive and manic episodesResponds to benzodiazepines and ECT
With peripartum onsetOnset in pregnancy or within 4 weeks after deliveryDepressive, manic or hypomanic episodesRisk to mother and infant; recurrence with later births
With seasonal patternOnset and remission at set times of year for 2 years, outnumbering other episodesRecurrent MDD; bipolar I and IILight therapy; anticipate the next season
With rapid cycling4 or more mood episodes in 12 monthsBipolar I and II onlyHarder to treat; avoid antidepressants (Section 5.4)

The specifier with anxious distress is applied when at least two of five symptoms are present on most days of the episode: feeling keyed up or tense; feeling unusually restless; difficulty concentrating because of worry; fear that something awful may happen; and a feeling that one might lose control. Severity is graded by the count: two symptoms is mild, three moderate, four or five moderate-severe, and four or five with motor agitation severe. The specifier can be added to MDD, PDD, every kind of episode in bipolar I and II, and cyclothymic disorder. It is one of DSM-5's most important additions, because anxiety in a mood disorder is common in both primary care and specialist settings and it matters: high levels of anxiety go with a higher suicide risk, a longer illness and a greater chance that treatment will fail. It is also DSM's answer to the question that ICD answers with mixed anxiety and depressive disorder: rather than a separate hybrid diagnosis, DSM marks the anxiety on the mood diagnosis.

The specifier with mixed features describes an episode of one pole that carries several symptoms of the other. In a major depressive episode with mixed features, at least three manic or hypomanic symptoms are present on most days: elevated or expansive mood; inflated self-esteem or grandiosity; being more talkative than usual; flight of ideas or racing thoughts; increased energy or goal-directed activity; risky activities such as buying sprees, sexual indiscretions or foolish investments; and decreased need for sleep (feeling rested despite sleeping less, which is quite different from the exhausted wakefulness of insomnia). In a manic or hypomanic episode with mixed features, at least three depressive symptoms are present: prominent dysphoria or depressed mood, loss of interest or pleasure, psychomotor retardation, fatigue, worthlessness or guilt, and recurrent thoughts of death or suicide. In both cases the mixed symptoms must be observable by others and not caused by a substance.

Look closely at those two lists and you will notice what is missing: distractibility, irritability, psychomotor agitation, insomnia, poor concentration and appetite change. They were left out on purpose, because they occur at both poles, and counting them would label almost every agitated depression as "mixed". When a person meets the full criteria for mania and for a major depressive episode at the same time, DSM-5-TR calls it a manic episode with mixed features, because full mania carries the greater impairment and danger. This replaced DSM-IV's "mixed episode", which required full criteria for both nearly every day for a week; ICD-11 has kept a mixed episode of its own (Section 5.1). The specifier matters for prognosis and treatment. Mixed features in a depressive episode are a significant risk factor for later bipolar I or II disorder, so such a person should be watched for a switch and treated cautiously with antidepressants; mixed states are also periods of high suicide risk, when despair meets restless energy.

Exam trap

"With mixed features" in a manic or hypomanic episode means concurrent depressive symptoms (at least three); in a depressive episode it means concurrent manic or hypomanic symptoms. It does not mean psychosis, anxiety (that is anxious distress) or substance withdrawal. Anxious distress, mixed features and psychotic features are all genuine specifiers of a manic episode, so in a list of specifiers for mania the odd one out is "with limited prosocial emotions", which belongs to conduct disorder (Chapter 11).

Melancholic and atypical features

Two old descriptions of depression, opposite in almost every bodily respect, survive as specifiers. The specifier with melancholic features captures the classic severe depression of the old psychiatric wards, the "endogenous" depression that seemed to come from within. It needs one of two signs of a near-complete absence of pleasure during the worst part of the episode: loss of pleasure in all or almost all activities, or lack of reactivity to usually pleasurable events, so that even very welcome news brightens the mood not at all or only a little (perhaps to a fifth or two fifths of normal, for minutes). Then three or more of six further features are needed: a distinct quality of mood, a profound despondency, despair or "empty" mood that the person experiences as different in kind from ordinary sadness and not merely as more of it; diurnal variation with depression regularly worse in the morning; early-morning waking at least two hours before the usual time; marked psychomotor retardation or agitation; significant loss of appetite or weight; and excessive or inappropriate guilt. Anand, waking at three, unable to enjoy even his daughter, slowed and guilt-ridden, worst in the mornings, would qualify. Melancholic features are more common in inpatients, in severe episodes, in older people and in those with psychotic features; they are associated with overactivity of the hypothalamic-pituitary-adrenal stress axis (Section 5.5); and they show only a modest tendency to recur in later episodes. Clinically, melancholic depression has traditionally been regarded as responding well to physical treatments such as tricyclic antidepressants and ECT, and less well to placebo.

The specifier with atypical features describes nearly the reverse. Its defining feature is mood reactivity: the mood brightens, sometimes for long periods, when something good happens, such as a visit from friends or a compliment. Two or more of four further features are needed: significant weight gain or increased appetite; hypersomnia (at least ten hours of sleep a day, or at least two hours more than when not depressed); leaden paralysis, a heavy, weighted-down feeling in the arms or legs lasting at least an hour a day; and a long-standing pattern of rejection sensitivity, an exaggerated sensitivity to perceived rejection by others, present even when the person is not depressed, that impairs social or work life. The specifier cannot be used if the same episode meets the criteria for melancholic features or catatonia. The word "atypical" is a historical accident, not a statement about frequency. The label was first used in Britain in the late 1950s, by E. D. West and P. J. Dally (1959), for depressed outpatients who did well on the new monoamine oxidase inhibitor (MAOI) iproniazid, and who looked unlike the "typical" agitated, sleepless, endogenous inpatients of the day. It is in fact common, and more so in women, in younger people and in those with an early onset; it carries more comorbid anxiety and alcohol misuse, more suicide attempts, and, because of the weight gain, a raised risk of obesity and metabolic problems. The treatment finding that kept the subtype alive came from Columbia University: in a six-week trial of 119 patients with atypical depression, Michael R. Liebowitz, Frederic M. Quitkin and colleagues (1988) found response rates of 71 percent on the MAOI phenelzine, 50 percent on the tricyclic imipramine and 28 percent on placebo. Table 5.12 sets the two specifiers side by side.

Table 5.12 Melancholic and atypical features compared.
FeatureMelancholicAtypical
Mood reactivityAbsent: good news hardly helpsPresent: required
SleepEarly-morning wakingHypersomnia
Appetite and weightLossGain, carbohydrate craving
Time of dayWorse in the morningNo fixed pattern; often worse in the evening
BodyMarked psychomotor retardation or agitationLeaden paralysis
InterpersonalExcessive guiltLifelong rejection sensitivity
WhoSevere, older, inpatientYounger, more women, early onset
Treatment clueTricyclics, ECTMAOIs historically better than tricyclics
Exam trap

The one feature that separates the two is mood reactivity: absent in melancholic depression, required in atypical depression. Weight gain, hypersomnia, leaden paralysis and rejection sensitivity are atypical; worse in the morning, early waking, weight loss and excessive guilt are melancholic. "Atypical" does not mean rare. ICD-10's somatic syndrome is the counterpart of melancholic, not atypical, features.

Psychotic features and catatonia

An elderly retired schoolmaster is brought to hospital by his sons. He has stopped eating because, he says, his stomach and intestines have rotted away and food has nowhere to go; he is a sinner who has ruined his family and deserves to be punished; at night he hears a voice saying he will be taken away. These are delusions and hallucinations, so his major depressive episode is specified with psychotic features. Notice their content. Each fits the depressed mood: personal inadequacy, guilt, disease, death, nihilism and deserved punishment are the themes the DSM-5-TR lists, and psychotic features with such content are called mood-congruent. His belief that his organs no longer exist is a nihilistic delusion, which at its extreme (the belief that one is dead or does not exist) is known as Cotard's syndrome, after the French neurologist Jules Cotard (1840 to 1889), who first described it in 1880 (Chapter 2 describes the delusions). In mania, mood-congruent themes are grandiosity and invulnerability, sometimes with suspicion of people who doubt the person's great abilities. Psychotic features are mood-incongruent when their content is unrelated to the mood, such as persecutory delusions with no theme of guilt or deserved punishment, thought insertion or voices commenting neutrally, or a mixture of congruent and incongruent themes.

Psychotic features occur in roughly 5 to 20 percent of identified cases of depression, more often in severe, melancholic, older and hospitalised patients. They signal a poorer outcome: slower recovery, greater impairment, a higher suicide risk, and a greater chance that the illness will later prove to be bipolar. They also change treatment, because an antidepressant alone works poorly: the usual choices are an antidepressant combined with an antipsychotic, or electroconvulsive therapy, which is often the most effective treatment of all for psychotic depression (Section 5.7). Psychotic features are coded whatever the severity of the episode. One rule to remember from Section 5.1: a hypomanic episode can never have psychotic features; if they appear, the episode is by definition manic.

How then do you tell a mood disorder with psychotic features from a primary psychotic disorder such as schizophrenia or schizoaffective disorder (Chapter 4)? Two features do most of the work. The first is timing: in a mood disorder the psychosis occurs only during mood episodes and clears when the mood recovers, whereas in schizoaffective disorder delusions or hallucinations persist for at least two weeks with no major mood episode, and in schizophrenia psychosis dominates the illness. The second is content: psychosis in mood disorders is usually mood-congruent, while the bizarre and mood-incongruent symptoms of schizophrenia have no connection with mood. Mood-incongruent features can occur in mood disorders, and when they do they suggest a poorer prognosis, but mood congruence remains the classic distinguishing clue. The mere presence of delusions, the length of the depressive episodes or the presence of anxiety does not settle the question.

With catatonia is applied to a major depressive or manic episode when catatonic features (stupor, mutism, negativism, posturing, catalepsy and waxy flexibility, stereotypies, echolalia and the others listed in Chapter 4) are present for most of the episode. Catatonia was long thought of as a feature of schizophrenia, but it is at least as common in severe mood disorders. It is a medical emergency when the person stops eating and drinking, and it usually responds well to benzodiazepines such as lorazepam and to ECT.

Exam trap

What best distinguishes a primary psychotic disorder from a mood disorder with psychotic features? The mood congruence of the psychotic symptoms (together with their confinement to mood episodes). Delusions as such, the duration of depressive episodes and comorbid anxiety are not discriminating. Depressive mood-congruent themes: guilt, inadequacy, disease, death, nihilism, deserved punishment; manic ones: grandiosity, special powers, invulnerability.

Peripartum onset: from maternity blues to postpartum psychosis

Uma, twenty-eight, had her first baby in a Bengaluru hospital after a long labour. On the third day she found herself crying when the nurse brought the baby, then laughing at herself, then crying again; she slept badly and felt overwhelmed. Her mother told her this was normal, and by the tenth day it had passed. Then, in the third week, something different began. She could not sleep even when the baby slept. She took no pleasure in the child and felt nothing when he smiled, which made her think she was a terrible mother; she believed her husband and mother-in-law were disappointed in her; she stopped eating and spent the afternoons lying in a darkened room; and she had frightening images of something happening to the baby. Her mother-in-law said she was "weak" and needed rest and special food. Uma's first experience, in the first days, was maternity blues. The second was a major depressive episode with peripartum onset.

DSM-5-TR applies the specifier with peripartum onset to a depressive, manic or hypomanic episode when the mood symptoms begin during pregnancy or in the 4 weeks following delivery. The older term "postpartum" was widened to "peripartum" because about half of so-called postpartum major depressive episodes actually begin before the birth. About 9 percent of women have a major depressive episode between conception and birth, and the best estimate for a major depressive episode in the year after birth is just under 7 percent; studies that count all clinically significant depression give higher figures, around 13 to 19 percent. Clinicians and researchers usually look well beyond four weeks: the first year after birth is a period of raised risk, and the ICD-11 groups its own mental disorders associated with pregnancy, childbirth and the puerperium (with and without psychotic symptoms) by onset within about six weeks of delivery. Fathers are not immune: in one large British study (Paul Ramchandani and colleagues, 2005), about 4 percent of fathers, against 10 percent of mothers, had high levels of depressive symptoms eight weeks after the birth.

Indian figures are higher still. Vikram Patel (born 1964) and colleagues followed women from the last three months of pregnancy in a district hospital in Goa and found in 2002 that 23 percent had a depressive disorder six to eight weeks after delivery, that most of them had already been distressed during pregnancy, and that more than half were still ill six months later. Economic deprivation and a poor marital relationship predicted both onset and chronicity, and the sex of the baby mattered: the birth of a girl to a family that wanted a boy increased the risk and amplified the effects of marital violence and hunger. A meta-analysis of 38 Indian studies with 20,043 women (Upadhyay and colleagues, 2017) put the pooled prevalence of postpartum depression at 22 percent, with financial difficulty, domestic violence, a past psychiatric history, marital conflict, lack of support from the husband and the birth of a girl as recurring risk factors. Traditional practices, such as the period of rest at the mother's home after delivery, can protect when they bring support; they do not protect against poverty, violence or a family's disappointment.

Three conditions follow childbirth and must never be confused, because they need completely different responses. Table 5.13 compares them. The maternity blues (baby blues) affect somewhere between 40 and 80 percent of women, begin in the first few days after delivery, and bring sudden tearfulness, mood swings, irritability, poor sleep and sometimes mild confusion; they cause no real impairment and fade within about a week (two at most) without treatment, and the DSM-5-TR does not regard them as a mental disorder at all. By contrast, postpartum depression is a full major depressive episode with peripartum onset, as Uma had; it impairs the mother's functioning and her care of the baby, carries a risk of suicide, and needs treatment. Finally, postpartum psychosis is rare, occurring in about 1 in 500 to 1 in 1,000 deliveries, more often in first-time mothers. It usually begins abruptly within the first two weeks, with confusion, rapidly changing mood, severe insomnia, delusions and hallucinations, and it is strongly linked to bipolar disorder: the risk is highest in women with a past postpartum psychotic episode, a history of bipolar I disorder, or a family history of bipolar illness. It is a psychiatric emergency, because the rare tragedies of infanticide are most often associated with command hallucinations to kill the baby or delusions that the baby is possessed. Once a woman has had a postpartum psychotic episode, the risk of another with each later delivery is 30 to 50 percent. Two medical look-alikes must be excluded: delirium in the puerperium, recognised by a fluctuating level of awareness and attention, and thyroid disease, which is common after delivery and would make the diagnosis a depressive disorder due to another medical condition.

Table 5.13 Maternity blues, postpartum depression and postpartum psychosis.
FeatureMaternity bluesPostpartum depressionPostpartum psychosis
How commonAbout 40 to 80 percent of mothersAbout 1 in 8 to 1 in 5 (higher in Indian studies)About 1 to 2 per 1,000 deliveries
OnsetFirst few days after birthPregnancy or the first weeks to months after birthAbrupt, usually within the first 2 weeks
Main featuresTearfulness, labile mood, poor sleep; no impairmentFull major depressive episode: anhedonia, guilt, insomnia, hopelessnessConfusion, mood swings, delusions, hallucinations
CoursePasses within about a week without treatmentMonths if untreated; may become chronicRecovers with treatment; 30 to 50 percent recur with later births
Main risksNone; may herald depression if it persistsSuicide; harm to bonding and child developmentSuicide and infanticide; strongly linked to bipolar disorder
ResponseReassurance and supportPsychological and drug treatmentEmergency admission
Exam trap

DSM-5-TR's peripartum specifier covers onset during pregnancy or within 4 weeks of delivery (ICD's puerperal categories use about 6 weeks). The blues are common, brief and not a disorder; postpartum depression is a major depressive episode; postpartum psychosis is rare, abrupt, usually bipolar in nature, and an emergency. Peripartum onset is a specifier, not a separate disorder.

Seasonal pattern and rapid cycling

In the early 1980s a South African psychiatrist working at the US National Institute of Mental Health, Norman E. Rosenthal (born 1950), who had himself noticed that his energy and mood sank in the dark winters after moving north, began to collect patients who became depressed at the same time every year.

Key study: Seasonal affective disorder (Rosenthal and colleagues, 1984)

Rosenthal and eight colleagues described 29 patients whose depressions recurred every autumn or winter and lifted in spring and summer. Most had a bipolar disorder, especially bipolar II, with hypomanic spells in summer. Their winter depressions were marked by oversleeping, overeating and craving for carbohydrates, and they seemed to respond to changes in climate and latitude. Sleep recordings in nine of them confirmed the oversleeping. In a preliminary trial in 11 patients, lengthening the day with bright artificial light had an antidepressant effect. The paper, in the Archives of General Psychiatry, named the syndrome seasonal affective disorder (SAD) and launched light therapy. Its limits were those of any first report: a small, self-selected sample (many had answered a newspaper article) and an uncontrolled light trial.

In DSM-5-TR, SAD is not a separate disorder. It is the specifier with seasonal pattern, which applies to recurrent major depressive disorder and to bipolar I and bipolar II disorder. Its rules are designed to make sure the season, not something that happens in a season, is the driver. There must be a regular relationship between the onset of episodes and a particular time of year (usually autumn or winter), and full remissions (or, in bipolar disorder, a switch to the other pole) must also occur at a characteristic time (usually spring). In the last two years, two episodes must have shown this pattern with no non-seasonal episodes of that pole, and over the lifetime the seasonal episodes must substantially outnumber the non-seasonal ones. Episodes explained by seasonal stressors do not count: a farm labourer who is depressed every lean season because there is no work, or a student who sinks every February and March as the board examinations approach, has a seasonal stressor, not a seasonal pattern.

Winter depressions look like atypical depressions: loss of energy, hypersomnia, overeating, weight gain and carbohydrate craving. A less common summer pattern also exists.
