Prevalence rises with latitude: in one American comparison it was under 2 percent in Florida and nearly 10 percent in New Hampshire, and in Fairbanks, Alaska, about 9 percent met criteria; winter depression is more common in women and in younger people, but it is not confined to women. In a country lying as close to the equator as most of India, winter depression would be expected to be uncommon. The mechanism probably involves the body clock and the hormone melatonin, which is secreted in darkness (Section 5.5). The treatment, light therapy (phototherapy), consists of sitting each morning in front of a bright light box, typically about 10,000 lux for around half an hour (early protocols used 2,500 lux for two hours). Mood often begins to lift within three or four days, and a winter depression may remit within one to two weeks; morning light works better than evening light. Side effects include headache, eyestrain and feeling "wired", and in bipolar patients light can trigger hypomania.

The last specifier, with rapid cycling, belongs to the bipolar disorders only. It requires at least four mood episodes in the previous 12 months (manic, hypomanic or major depressive in bipolar I; hypomanic or major depressive in bipolar II), each separated by a remission of at least two months or by a switch to the opposite pole. It cannot be applied to major depressive disorder, however many episodes a person has. Its causes, its link with antidepressants and thyroid disease, and its higher frequency in women are explained with the bipolar disorders in Section 5.4.

Exam trap

Seasonal affective disorder is coded in DSM-5 as the "with seasonal pattern" specifier of recurrent major depressive disorder or of bipolar I or II disorder. It is not a separate disorder, not an anxiety or personality disorder, and not limited to women (though commoner in them). Rosenthal's original 1984 patients were mostly bipolar. Rapid cycling (four or more episodes in 12 months) applies to bipolar disorders only. And the specifier "with limited prosocial emotions" belongs to conduct disorder, not to any mood disorder.

Scan Your Brain

Fill in the blanks. The answers are below; cover them first.

  1. A major depressive episode needs five or more of nine symptoms, one of them depressed mood or loss of interest, lasting at least weeks.
  2. Waking two or more hours too early and being unable to sleep again is called insomnia.
  3. The rule that barred a diagnosis of major depression for two months after a death, removed in DSM-5, was the exclusion.
  4. Arthur Kleinman found that 87 of 100 Chinese patients diagnosed with met DSM-III criteria for major depressive disorder.
  5. A major depressive episode superimposed on dysthymia is called depression.
  6. In disruptive mood dysregulation disorder the symptoms must begin before age .
  7. Premenstrual dysphoric disorder must be confirmed by prospective daily ratings over at least symptomatic cycles.
  8. The withdrawal and deterioration of infants separated from their mothers, described by René Spitz in 1946, is called depression.
  9. Mood reactivity together with weight gain, hypersomnia, leaden paralysis or rejection sensitivity defines the "with features" specifier.
  10. The ICD-10 "hybrid" category in which neither anxiety nor depression is severe enough for its own diagnosis is mixed anxiety and disorder.

Answers: (1) 2, (2) terminal (early morning waking), (3) bereavement, (4) neurasthenia, (5) double, (6) 10, (7) two, (8) anaclitic, (9) atypical, (10) depressive.

5.4 Bipolar and Related DisordersHigh yield · 10 Qs

Most people who become depressed never travel to the opposite pole. A smaller group do. At some point their mood, energy and confidence rise far above anything that is ordinary for them; they need hardly any sleep, their thoughts race ahead of their speech, and their judgement fails at exactly the moment they feel most certain of it. These are the bipolar disorders, the conditions Emil Kraepelin gathered under the name manic-depressive insanity (Section 5.1 tells that history). DSM-5 (2013) took them out of the old "mood disorders" class and gave them a chapter of their own, placed between the schizophrenia spectrum and the depressive disorders as a bridge between the two in symptoms, family history and genetics. Everything in this chapter of the classification is defined by two kinds of elevated episode, mania and hypomania, so those come first. The diagnoses built from them follow, then the states that only look bipolar, the patterns the illness takes over a lifetime, and finally the reasons bipolar disorder is so often missed, mistaken for something else or, lately, stretched too far.

The manic episode up close

Rahul, a 24-year-old software engineer in Pune, was brought to the psychiatry emergency of a general hospital by his father and elder brother at eleven at night. For nine days he had slept two or three hours a night and woken feeling fresh. He had resigned from his job by email, copying in the company's chief executive, to explain that he was "too big for a ticket-closing role" and would now build a company to bring free solar power to every village in India. In one week he had booked two cars on loan, transferred most of his savings to a man who had promised him a "franchise", and sent dozens of messages to a woman colleague he barely knew. He had not stopped talking since he arrived. Asked how he was, he answered without a pause:

"Never better, doctor. Better, butter, bitter, the bitter truth is that I am ten years ahead of everyone in that office. Office, officer, I could be an IAS officer, I never sat the exam but I could clear it tomorrow. Is that a Parker pen? I will order fifty for your whole staff. My company is called Surya Shakti, the goddess showed me the name, she has chosen me to light up the country, and the Prime Minister's office will call me this week."

When the doctor tried to bring him back to the question of sleep, Rahul's smile vanished: "You are jealous, exactly like my manager." A minute later he was laughing and offering the doctor a job. He saw no reason to be in a hospital; it was his family, he said, who needed treatment. His father mentioned, almost in passing, that five years earlier, in the second year of his engineering course, Rahul had spent three months "completely low", hardly leaving his room. Rahul was in a manic episode, and nearly every feature of mania is visible in those few minutes.

The core of the episode, its Criterion A in DSM-5-TR, has two parts that must both be present. The first is a distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive or irritable mood. Elevated mood is euphoria, the feeling of being on top of the world, and in mania it often has an infectious quality that others at first find charming. Expansive mood is unceasing, indiscriminate enthusiasm: striking up long conversations with strangers on the bus, embracing every plan and every person. Irritable mood appears when the person is crossed, and in many episodes it is the main mood; Rahul's instant anger when interrupted is typical. The mood is also labile, swinging between euphoria, irritability and tears within minutes. The second part, made an equal requirement in DSM-5, is abnormally and persistently increased activity or energy. Requiring both was meant to cut down false positives: a happy or irritable week without a surge of energy is not mania.

The disturbance must last at least one week and be present most of the day, nearly every day, or any length of time if the person has to be admitted to hospital. The exception exists for a practical reason. Mania is often treated within days of admission, and it would be absurd to deny the diagnosis because effective treatment cut the episode short.

During that period at least three of seven further symptoms must be present to a significant degree and must represent a clear change from the person's usual behaviour. If the mood is only irritable, four are needed, because irritability on its own is so common (in depression, anxiety, intoxication, personality disorders and plain bad days) that the diagnosis needs more evidence. The seven are summarised by the DIG FAST mnemonic of Section 5.1; here is what each looks like at interview.

  • Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity. This runs from uncritical self-confidence to frank delusion. The person may start to write a novel, launch an invention or advise a government without any relevant skill, or believe that he has a special relationship with a famous person. Rahul's chosen mission and the call from the Prime Minister's office are grandiose delusions.
  • Decreased need for sleep. The person sleeps three hours and feels rested. This is not insomnia: the person with insomnia wants to sleep and cannot, and feels wretched the next day, whereas the manic person does not want or need to sleep. It is often the very first sign of a coming episode, which is why families of people with bipolar disorder are taught to watch for it.
  • More talkative than usual, or pressure of speech. Pressure of speech is rapid, loud, insistent talk that is hard to interrupt, full of jokes, puns, rhymes and theatrical flourishes. When the mood is irritable, the same pressure comes out as complaints and angry tirades.
  • Flight of ideas, or the subjective experience that thoughts are racing. Flight of ideas is the observable sign: a nearly continuous flow of accelerated speech that jumps from topic to topic on the strength of chance associations. Rahul's "better, butter, bitter" is a clang association, a link made by sound rather than meaning, and "office, officer" is a link by word play. Unlike the loosened associations of schizophrenia (Chapter 2), the connections in flight of ideas can usually be followed if you listen carefully. Racing thoughts are the inner experience of the same acceleration, and the person may say that his thoughts are moving faster than he can speak.
  • Distractibility. Attention is pulled away by trivial things in the room: the interviewer's pen, a noise in the corridor, the colour of a curtain. It is often what makes a rational conversation impossible.
  • Increase in goal-directed activity, or psychomotor agitation. The person takes on many overlapping projects, becomes intensely sociable (calling old friends and strangers at all hours), becomes more interested in sex, or writes floods of letters, emails and messages to friends, officials and the media. When the activity has no goal at all (pacing, fidgeting, holding several conversations at once) it is psychomotor agitation.
  • Excessive involvement in activities with painful consequences. Unrestrained spending, giving away money and possessions, foolish business investments, reckless driving, gambling and sexual indiscretions. The person can foresee none of the consequences, because the expansive mood makes every plan seem certain to succeed. Rahul's car loans and the "franchise" payment belong here.

Criterion C sets the severity that separates mania from hypomania. The episode must cause marked impairment in social or occupational functioning, or require hospitalisation to prevent harm to self or others, or include psychotic features. Any one of the three is enough. The impairment is often disastrous: lost jobs, debts, broken marriages, police cases, physical exhaustion. Criterion D excludes episodes caused by the physiological effects of a substance or another medical condition, with one important exception that comes up again below: a full manic episode that begins during antidepressant treatment, whether a drug or electroconvulsive therapy, and continues at full strength after the treatment's direct effect has worn off counts as a genuine manic episode, and therefore as bipolar I disorder.

Several associated features complete the picture. Insight is usually lost, so the person vehemently resists treatment, and it is almost always the family who brings him; Rahul's view that his family needed treatment is characteristic. Dress and make-up may become flamboyant or sexually provocative. Some people report that colours and sounds seem sharper. Hostility can become threatening or violent, especially when the person is delusional or is being restrained. And in about 35 percent of manic episodes depressive symptoms are present at the same time, a mixture you will meet under mixed features below.

Psychotic features are common in severe mania. Delusions are usually grandiose (a special mission, a secret identity, extraordinary powers or wealth) and often religious, as with the goddess who chose Rahul. They may also be persecutory, but in a way that grows out of the grandiosity: others are plotting against him because they envy his gifts or doubt his achievements. Hallucinations, when they occur, tend to be voices that praise or instruct. DSM-5-TR calls such content mood-congruent psychotic features, because it fits the manic themes of grandiosity and invulnerability. Mood-incongruent psychotic features are delusions or hallucinations with no connection to the mood, such as the belief that thoughts are being inserted by a neighbour's machine. Two rules follow, and the second is examined directly. First, in a mood disorder the psychosis appears only during mood episodes, never in the well intervals. Second, the content usually fits the mood. Mood congruence, together with that timing, is what best distinguishes a mood disorder with psychotic features from a primary psychotic disorder such as schizophrenia or schizoaffective disorder (Chapter 4). The mere presence of delusions does not, since both have them. Harrison Pope and Joseph Lipinski, reviewing the evidence in 1978, showed that even Kurt Schneider's first-rank symptoms, long treated as proof of schizophrenia, occur in a sizeable minority of manic patients, which is one reason manic patients were so often misdiagnosed.

Key study: The stages of mania (Carlson and Goodwin, 1973)

Gabrielle A. Carlson and Frederick K. Goodwin, at the National Institute of Mental Health, went back through the detailed daily ward records of 20 patients with bipolar disorder, tracing each one through a full manic episode, in a study published in 1973 in the Archives of General Psychiatry. They found that mania unfolds in stages. In stage I the patient is euphoric, expansive, grandiose and overconfident, talking and moving more, and still in partial control. In stage II speech and activity accelerate further, euphoria gives way more often to irritability, hostility and dysphoria, and delusions appear. In stage III, reached by some patients, the person is desperate, panic-stricken and hopeless, with frenzied, bizarre behaviour, loose and incoherent thinking, hallucinations and sometimes disorientation. The point of the study was diagnostic: a patient seen only at stage III can look exactly like an acute schizophrenic patient, and only the longitudinal view, how the episode began and how it resolves, shows that it is mania. The most extreme form, delirious mania, was described by the American asylum physician Luther V. Bell in 1849 and is still sometimes called Bell's mania; it is a medical emergency and overlaps with catatonia (Chapter 4).

Exam trap

Asked for the two key symptoms of a manic episode, choose extremely elevated mood and grandiose ideas. Flight of ideas and distractibility are genuine manic symptoms, but they are paired in the distractors with things that are not: hallucinations are not among the seven Criterion B symptoms (they can occur, but only as psychotic features), social phobia belongs to the anxiety disorders (Chapter 6), and intense fear of weight gain is anorexia nervosa (Chapter 8). Remember the numbers: one week, or any duration if hospitalised; three symptoms, or four if the mood is only irritable. A decreased need for sleep is not insomnia. In mood disorders psychotic symptoms are confined to the episodes and are usually mood-congruent, which is the best way to tell them from a primary psychotic disorder.

Hypomania: the same symptoms, a different threshold

A hypomanic episode (from the Greek hypo, under) is built from exactly the same materials as mania. There is the same distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive or irritable mood with increased activity or energy, and the same list of seven symptoms, three of which must be present, or four if the mood is only irritable. Students often assume that hypomania is mania with fewer symptoms. It is not. The symptom count is identical. What differs is how long the state lasts and how much damage it does.

Four rules set hypomania apart. It must last at least four consecutive days, most of the day, nearly every day, rather than a week. It must bring an unequivocal change in functioning that is uncharacteristic of the person when well. That change must be observable by others, not merely reported by the person. And it must not be severe enough to cause marked impairment in social or occupational functioning or to require hospitalisation. A fifth rule is written into the criteria as a definition: if there are psychotic features, the episode is, by definition, manic. Hypomania can never be psychotic, and a hypomanic person never needs admission; if either happens, you are dealing with mania.

Each rule exists for a reason. The "observable by others" requirement guards against a real danger in diagnosis: hypomania is usually pleasant, and people looking back on their lives can easily relabel an ordinary good week as a "high". Insisting that a spouse, a friend or a colleague noticed the change keeps the diagnosis honest. DSM-5-TR adds two cautions in the same spirit. The several days of restored energy and relief that often follow the lifting of a depression are not hypomania, and one or two symptoms of edginess, irritability or agitation after starting an antidepressant are not hypomania either. A full hypomanic episode that begins during antidepressant treatment and continues at full strength after the drug's direct effect has worn off does count, just as with mania.

What is hypomania like from the inside? Many people describe it as being "the best version of myself": sharp, witty, sociable, productive and confident, needing five hours of sleep instead of eight. The state is ego-syntonic, felt as part of the self rather than as illness, and people seldom seek help for it or mention it unless they are asked. Others notice something different: the person talks more and over everybody, starts several projects at once, spends more freely, flirts, and becomes short-tempered when slowed down. Because the impairment criterion excludes marked damage, hypomania on its own is rarely a disaster. Its importance is what it predicts. A person who has had a hypomanic episode and a major depressive episode has bipolar II disorder, not unipolar depression, and that changes treatment (Section 5.7). ICD-10's research criteria use the same four-day minimum for hypomania, and ICD-11 keeps hypomania as a distinct episode lasting at least several days (Section 5.1 compares the two systems). Table 5.14 sets the two elevated episodes side by side.

Table 5.14 The manic and the hypomanic episode compared (DSM-5-TR).
FeatureManic episodeHypomanic episode
Mood and energyAbnormally and persistently elevated, expansive or irritable, with increased energyThe same
Minimum duration1 week, or any duration if hospitalised4 consecutive days
Symptoms needed3 of 7 (4 if mood is only irritable)The same 3 of 7 (4 if only irritable)
FunctioningMarked impairmentA clear, uncharacteristic change but no marked impairment
HospitalisationOften neededNever (if needed, the episode is manic)
Psychotic featuresMay be presentNever (psychosis makes it mania)
Who noticesEveryone; insight usually lostMust be observable by others; the person may feel simply well
Diagnosis it definesBipolar I disorderBipolar II disorder (with a major depressive episode)
Exam trap

The question "what distinguishes mania from hypomania?" has one right answer in many costumes: mania involves marked impairment, hospitalisation or psychotic features and lasts at least a week; hypomania lasts at least four days with an observable change but none of those three. Reject the distractors one by one. Hypomania does have elevated mood. It never requires, or even permits, hallucinations; "only hypomania can include psychotic features" is exactly backwards. Mania does not need depressive symptoms, and it need not be preceded by a depression. Hypomania does not last longer than mania as a rule (its minimum is shorter), and it does require an observable change from baseline. The number of symptoms is the same for both, so "hypomania has fewer symptoms" is also wrong.

Bipolar I disorder

With the two elevated episodes defined, the diagnoses are simple to state. Bipolar I disorder requires one thing: at least one lifetime manic episode that is not better explained by schizoaffective disorder and is not superimposed on schizophrenia, schizophreniform disorder, delusional disorder or another psychotic disorder. That is all. Major depressive episodes are common in bipolar I disorder but are not required, hypomanic episodes are common but not required, and periods of depressive symptoms too mild to count as a major depressive episode are perfectly compatible with it. DSM-5-TR describes bipolar I disorder as the modern form of the classic manic-depressive illness of the nineteenth century, differing from it only in that neither psychosis nor a depressive episode has to be present.

Why should a single manic episode be enough, when a single depressive episode is not enough for anything but a single-episode diagnosis? Because of what follows it. More than 90 percent of people who have one manic episode go on to have further mood episodes, and the great majority of people who meet criteria for mania also have major depressive episodes at some point in their lives. Rahul, with one depression at 19 and one mania at 24, has bipolar I disorder; so would a woman whose only episode so far was a manic one. A few people have repeated manias and never a depression. They are still diagnosed with bipolar I disorder, though clinicians call the pattern unipolar mania. Indian clinicians have taken a particular interest in it: studies from Indian hospitals have reported it in anything from about 5 percent to nearly half of patients with bipolar I disorder, the spread depending mainly on how strictly it is defined and on whether the sample was drawn from inpatients.

The diagnosis is recorded by the type of the current or most recent episode (manic, hypomanic, depressed or unspecified), its severity (mild, moderate or severe), whether psychotic features are present, and, between episodes, whether the disorder is in partial or full remission. The specifiers of Section 5.3 are then added as they apply: with anxious distress, with mixed features, with rapid cycling, with melancholic or atypical features, with mood-congruent or mood-incongruent psychotic features, with catatonia, with peripartum onset and with seasonal pattern. Separate episodes are counted only when they are divided by at least two months of partial or full remission, or by a switch to the opposite pole; otherwise one episode is treated as a continuation of the last. So a full description of Rahul at admission reads: bipolar I disorder, current episode manic, severe, with mood-congruent psychotic features.

How common is it? DSM-5-TR reports a 12-month prevalence of 1.5 percent for DSM-5 bipolar I disorder in a nationally representative sample of American adults, with no difference between men (1.6 percent) and women (1.5 percent). Older figures were lower: the original DSM-5 (2013) quoted 0.6 percent for the United States, a figure for DSM-IV bipolar I disorder from the National Comorbidity Survey Replication, and DSM-5-TR still reports that across eleven countries the 12-month prevalence of DSM-IV bipolar I disorder ranged from 0.0 to 0.6 percent, higher in high-income countries than in low- and middle-income ones (Japan, at 0.01 percent, was the exception). The lifetime prevalence of bipolar disorder is usually given as about 1 percent. In India the National Mental Health Survey of 2015 to 2016 found a current prevalence of bipolar affective disorder of 0.3 percent and a lifetime prevalence of 0.5 percent among adults. The sex ratio is the striking contrast with depression. Women are about twice as likely as men to have major depressive disorder, but bipolar I disorder affects the sexes almost equally (a lifetime ratio of about 1.1 men to 1 woman). The sexes differ in the shape of the illness instead: women with bipolar disorder spend more time depressed, have rapid cycling and mixed states more often, and are at high risk of episodes after childbirth.

Onset is early. The peak age at onset across studies lies between 20 and 30 years; the mean age at onset in the United States is 22, slightly younger in women than in men, and Barlow and Durand, drawing on other studies, put the average onset of bipolar I disorder younger still, at 15 to 18 years (and of bipolar II disorder at 19 to 22). A first manic episode after about the age of 40 or 50 is unusual enough that it should prompt a search for a medical cause or a drug (below). Bipolar I disorder rarely travels alone. Most people with it have a history of three or more mental disorders, most often anxiety disorders, alcohol and other substance use disorders and attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder; nearly half have an alcohol use disorder at some time. They also carry high rates of heart disease, metabolic syndrome, obesity, migraine and autoimmune disease, which largely explain why people with bipolar disorder die younger than others.

Indian context

In Indian clinics the elevated pole often brings people to care, because mania disrupts a household in a way that depression does not: the family arrives with a young man who has stopped sleeping, spent the family's savings or quarrelled with the neighbours. Grandiose content is often religious (a divine mission, a special relationship with a deity) and families may first take the person to a temple, a dargah or a faith healer. Depressive episodes in the same person may never be treated at all. The National Mental Health Survey of 2015 to 2016 found a lifetime prevalence of bipolar affective disorder of 0.5 percent, and, as for most severe mental illnesses, a large treatment gap. Admission of a person in mania who lacks the capacity to decide about treatment is governed by the Mental Healthcare Act 2017 (Book 2, Section 1.10).

Exam trap

"Which statement about bipolar disorder is INCORRECT?" The incorrect one is "bipolar I disorder includes hypomania" when it is offered as the defining feature: bipolar I is defined by mania; hypomania defines bipolar II and is merely allowed in bipolar I. The true statements are that bipolar I includes mania, bipolar II includes hypomania, and bipolar I may include periods of depression even when they fall short of a major depressive episode. A disorder in which a person swings from one mood extreme to the other, or has alternating episodes of mania and depression, is bipolar disorder, not unipolar, depressive or "manic" disorder. And do not trust old notes that say mania is "more common in men" or begins "after 30": bipolar I has an equal sex ratio and usually begins in the late teens or twenties.

Bipolar II disorder

Geeta, a 34-year-old government school teacher in Mysuru, came to a psychologist in her third depression in six years. She was tearful, slept ten hours a night and still woke exhausted, craved sweets, described her arms and legs as "heavy like stones", and was tormented by guilt about neglecting her children. Her two earlier depressions had been treated by her family doctor with antidepressants. Asked whether she had ever felt the opposite of depressed, she said no. Her husband, seen separately, told a different story. About once a year, for five or six days, Geeta became "a different person": she slept four hours, rearranged the whole house at two in the morning, talked over everyone at family functions, enrolled in three online courses in one evening, bought sarees on credit she could not afford, and snapped at the children if they slowed her down. She never missed a day of school during these spells, and nobody, Geeta least of all, had thought of them as illness. They were hypomanic episodes. Together with her major depressive episodes they make the diagnosis bipolar II disorder.

Bipolar II disorder requires at least one hypomanic episode and at least one major depressive episode, and there must never have been a manic episode. As in bipolar I, the episodes must not be better explained by schizoaffective disorder or superimposed on a psychotic disorder. A final criterion makes the source of the suffering explicit: the depression, or the unpredictability caused by frequent alternation between depression and hypomania, must cause clinically significant distress or impairment. That criterion is needed because, by definition, hypomania itself does not cause marked impairment. The impairment in bipolar II disorder comes from the depressions and from the erratic, unreliable pattern of functioning that the swings create.

Geeta's story shows how the diagnosis is usually reached. People with bipolar II disorder almost always present during a depression. They seldom mention hypomania, either because they do not recognise it or because they regard it as their best self, so the diagnosis can only be made by asking specifically about past elevated periods and, wherever possible, asking someone who knows the person well. The illness most often begins with a depressive episode and is not recognised as bipolar until a hypomanic episode appears; this happens in about 12 percent of people first diagnosed with major depressive disorder, and the average lag between the start of the illness and a bipolar diagnosis is more than ten years. Once a hypomanic episode has occurred the diagnosis becomes bipolar II and never reverts to major depressive disorder, however long the person then stays depressed. If a full manic episode ever occurs, as it does in about 5 to 15 percent of people with bipolar II disorder, the diagnosis changes to bipolar I permanently.

The most important fact about bipolar II disorder is the one most often got wrong: it is not a milder form of bipolar I disorder. Its hypomanias are milder than manias, but its depressions are not milder than anyone's. Compared with people with bipolar I disorder, people with bipolar II disorder have a more chronic illness, more episodes over a lifetime and, on average, spend more of their lives in the depressive phase, which can be severe and disabling. About a third report a lifetime suicide attempt; rates of attempts and of suicide deaths appear to be about the same as in bipolar I disorder, and the attempts may even be more lethal. Atypical depressive features of the kind Geeta showed (oversleeping, overeating, leaden heaviness of the limbs) are common, as is a seasonal pattern. About three-quarters have an anxiety disorder, and alcohol and cannabis use disorders are frequent. About 5 to 15 percent have four or more episodes within a year (rapid cycling, below), more often women, who are also more likely than men to have hypomania with mixed depressive features. Childbirth can trigger hypomania, and postpartum "highs" are often followed by a depression.

DSM-5-TR gives a 12-month prevalence for bipolar II disorder of 0.8 percent in the United States and 0.3 percent internationally. The average age at onset is the mid-20s, a little later than bipolar I disorder and earlier than major depressive disorder. Findings on the sex ratio are mixed: community studies show little difference, whereas some clinical samples contain more women, perhaps because women seek treatment more readily. Hypomanic episodes are never psychotic, but the depressive episodes of bipolar II disorder can be, although psychotic features appear less often than in bipolar I depressions. Table 5.15 compares the three main bipolar disorders, and Figure 5.7 shows the shape of each over time.

Table 5.15 Bipolar I disorder, bipolar II disorder and cyclothymic disorder compared (DSM-5-TR).
FeatureBipolar IBipolar IICyclothymic disorder
Defining requirementAt least one manic episodeAt least one hypomanic and one major depressive episode2 years (1 in youth) of numerous highs and lows below episode level
Manic episodeRequiredNeverNever
Hypomanic episodeCommon, not requiredRequiredNever a full episode, only symptoms
Major depressive episodeCommon, not requiredRequiredNever a full episode, only symptoms
Psychotic featuresPossible in mania or depressionPossible only in depressionNone
Prevalence12-month 1.5% (US)12-month 0.8% (US), 0.3% worldwideLifetime 0.4% to 2.5%
Typical onsetLate teens to 20s (US mean 22)Mid-20sAdolescence or early adulthood
Sex ratioEqualMixed findingsEqual in the community
Exam trap

The key distinction between bipolar I and bipolar II is a single fact: bipolar I requires at least one full manic episode (a week, or any duration if hospitalised); bipolar II requires at least one hypomanic episode plus at least one major depressive episode, and never a manic episode. Reject the distractors: bipolar II is not "always more severe" (nor is it simply milder; its burden is depression); bipolar I can and usually does have depressive episodes; bipolar II requires no psychotic features (its hypomanias never have them, though a depressive episode can); bipolar II has no manic episodes at all, so it cannot have "longer manic episodes"; rapid cycling occurs in both; and the presence of a major depressive episode does not separate them, because depression is required in II and allowed in I.

Cyclothymic disorder

Amir, a 20-year-old commerce student in Lucknow, was sent to the college counsellor after failing two papers. Since he was about 14, he said, his mood had "run on its own timetable". For a week or two at a time he was the life of the hostel: sleeping five hours, flooding the group chats, planning businesses with friends, spending a month's allowance in three days. Then, with no obvious reason, he would sink for a few weeks into flatness, sleeping late, skipping classes and feeling that he was a failure who would never amount to anything. Normal stretches seldom lasted more than a few weeks. He had never stopped functioning altogether, never needed admission, and when the counsellor went through the criteria carefully, none of his highs had amounted to a hypomanic episode and none of his lows to a major depressive episode. His friends called him moody; his parents called it his nature. After six years of this pattern, it was cyclothymic disorder.

The name is old. The German psychiatrist Karl Ludwig Kahlbaum (1828 to 1899), better remembered for naming catatonia, introduced the term cyclothymia in 1882 for a mild, cyclical mood illness that did not end in deterioration, and his collaborator Ewald Hecker (1843 to 1909) described it more fully in 1898. DSM-5-TR's criteria are built around three ideas: chronicity, subthreshold swings, and the absence of any full episode.

  • For at least 2 years (at least 1 year in children and adolescents) there have been numerous periods with hypomanic symptoms that do not meet criteria for a hypomanic episode and numerous periods with depressive symptoms that do not meet criteria for a major depressive episode.
  • During that time the symptoms have been present for at least half the time, and the person has never been without them for more than 2 months at a stretch.
  • Criteria for a major depressive, manic or hypomanic episode have never been met.
  • The picture is not better explained by a psychotic disorder, is not caused by a substance or a medical condition such as hyperthyroidism, and causes clinically significant distress or impairment.

The two-month rule is the same one used for persistent depressive disorder (Section 5.3), and for the same reason: it separates a chronic condition from a series of separate short episodes. The "never met full criteria" rule keeps cyclothymia distinct from bipolar I and II. If, after the first two years, a full episode does occur, cyclothymic disorder is dropped and the diagnosis changes to major depressive disorder (after a major depressive episode), bipolar I disorder (after a manic episode) or other specified bipolar and related disorder (after a hypomanic episode with no prior depression).

ICD-10, in which cyclothymia is coded F34.0 among the persistent mood disorders beside dysthymia (F34.1), describes it as a persistent instability of mood involving numerous periods of mild depression and mild elation, none of them severe or prolonged enough to meet the criteria for bipolar affective disorder or recurrent depressive disorder. ICD-11 moves cyclothymic disorder into its bipolar grouping. The lifetime prevalence in the United States and Europe is about 0.4 to 2.5 percent, and 3 to 5 percent of patients in mood disorder clinics. It is equally common in men and women in the community, though women are more likely to seek treatment. It usually begins in adolescence or early adult life, insidiously, and runs a persistent course; most young people with it report mood symptoms that began before the age of 10. Its importance lies partly in where it can lead: DSM-5-TR puts the risk of later bipolar I or bipolar II disorder at 15 to 50 percent, higher in young people than in adults. Hagop Akiskal, whose ideas appear again below, regarded cyclothymia as a temperament on which fuller bipolar illness can be built, and the relatives of people with cyclothymic disorder have raised rates of depressive and bipolar disorders.

Three conditions are easily confused with it. In rapid-cycling bipolar disorder the mood also shifts often, but the shifts are full episodes, whereas in cyclothymia they never are. In borderline personality disorder (Chapter 11) mood shifts are brief, often lasting hours, are usually set off by events in relationships, and move among anxiety, irritability, anger and sadness; sustained elation, euphoria and increased energy are not part of borderline personality disorder. If criteria for both are met, both may be diagnosed. And stimulant use can produce swings that look cyclothymic but settle when the drug is stopped.

Exam trap

Cyclothymia is best defined as at least 2 years of fluctuating hypomanic and depressive symptoms that never meet the criteria for a hypomanic, manic or major depressive episode, or in ICD-10's words, a persistent instability of mood between mild depression and mild elation. It is not alternating manic and depressive episodes of equal severity (that is bipolar I), not a single hypomanic episode, and not "recurrent dysthymic episodes lasting 6 months". When the same options appear under "What is dysthymia?", remember that dysthymia is persistent mild depression with no highs at all; "persistent instability of mood between mild depression and mild elation" describes cyclothymia, and "persistent mild depression and hypomania for more than 2 years" describes neither, because dysthymia allows no hypomania and cyclothymia allows no full hypomanic episode. The 2-year rule shrinks to 1 year in children and adolescents.

When drugs or illness cause the high, and the other specified category

A manic state can be manufactured. In 1978 two American psychiatrists, Charles Krauthammer (later better known as a newspaper columnist) and Gerald L. Klerman, collected cases of what they called secondary mania: manic syndromes that appeared after a physical illness or a drug in people with no history of mood disorder. DSM-5-TR keeps their insight in two diagnoses whose names say what they are.

Substance/medication-induced bipolar and related disorder is a prominent and persistent elevated, expansive or irritable mood with increased energy that develops during or soon after intoxication with, or withdrawal from, a substance capable of producing it, or after exposure to a medication. The usual culprits are stimulants (amphetamines, methylphenidate, cocaine), phencyclidine and corticosteroids, and newer synthetic stimulants keep being added to the list. The timing is characteristic of each. Stimulant-induced states begin within minutes to an hour of a dose and usually resolve in one or two days; mania from corticosteroids typically follows several days of treatment and is more likely at high doses; phencyclidine may produce a delirium first and a manic or mixed state afterwards, usually within hours of the dose. The diagnosis is not made if there is evidence of an independent bipolar disorder: if the mood symptoms began before the substance use, if they persist for a substantial period (about a month) after intoxication or withdrawal has ended, or if there is a history of episodes unconnected with any substance. Because people in a manic episode often use more alcohol and drugs, the clinician has to work out which came first.

Antidepressants, electroconvulsive therapy and even bright light therapy can tip a depressed person into hypomania or mania, the so-called antidepressant switch. DSM-5-TR treats the switch with care. A brief rise in edginess, irritability or agitation after starting an antidepressant is a side effect, not a bipolar diagnosis. But a full manic or hypomanic episode that begins during treatment and persists at full strength after the drug has left the body is taken as evidence of a true bipolar disorder, not a substance-induced one; the treatment is thought to have unmasked an underlying bipolar diathesis. This is one reason an antidepressant on its own is risky in a person who may be bipolar, and why fluoxetine is not a treatment for mania (Section 5.7).

Bipolar and related disorder due to another medical condition is diagnosed when the manic or hypomanic picture is the direct physiological consequence of an illness, judged from the history, examination and laboratory tests. The best-known causes are Cushing's disease, multiple sclerosis, stroke and traumatic brain injury; hyperthyroidism is a classic cause of hypomanic-like states, and anti-NMDA receptor encephalitis, an autoimmune inflammation of the brain, can present with manic or mixed mood and psychosis. The mood change usually appears within the first weeks or month of the medical illness. In the nineteenth-century asylum the great imitator of mania was general paresis, the late brain stage of syphilis, whose patients often had extravagant grandiose delusions. The modern lesson is the same: a first manic episode in late middle age or old age, especially with sexual or social disinhibition, should prompt a search for a medical condition such as frontotemporal neurocognitive disorder (Chapter 10) or for a drug.

Finally, some people clearly have bipolar-type symptoms that fit none of the named disorders. DSM-5-TR offers other specified bipolar and related disorder, recorded with the reason, and gives five examples: short-duration hypomanic episodes of only 2 to 3 days together with major depressive episodes; hypomanic episodes with too few symptoms together with major depressive episodes; a hypomanic episode with no prior major depressive episode; short-duration cyclothymia lasting less than 24 months (less than 12 in young people); and a manic episode superimposed on schizophrenia or another psychotic disorder. When the clinician chooses not to give a reason, or lacks the information (in an emergency room, for instance), the diagnosis is unspecified bipolar and related disorder. DSM-5-TR also restored an unspecified mood disorder (F39) for presentations, such as acute agitation, in which it is not yet possible to choose between the bipolar and the depressive chapters.

Mixed features and rapid cycling

The textbook picture of bipolar disorder, separate episodes of pure mania and pure depression with health in between, is the exception rather than the rule. Two departures from it are common enough to have their own specifiers. The first concerns what happens within an episode, the second how often episodes come.

Kraepelin had already noticed that many patients were neither purely manic nor purely depressed, and his pupil Wilhelm Weygandt published a monograph on these mixed states in 1899. Imagine a person whose thoughts race and who has not slept for days, yet who is in despair, crying, convinced he is worthless and thinking of suicide. DSM-IV required a "mixed episode" to meet the full criteria for both a manic episode and a major depressive episode nearly every day for at least a week, a bar so high that few patients ever reached it. DSM-5 replaced the mixed episode with the specifier with mixed features, which can be attached to a manic, hypomanic or major depressive episode. A manic or hypomanic episode has mixed features when full criteria for the episode are met and at least three depressive symptoms are present on most days: prominent dysphoria or depressed mood, loss of interest or pleasure, psychomotor retardation, fatigue, feelings of worthlessness or excessive guilt, and recurrent thoughts of death or suicide. A major depressive episode has mixed features when at least three manic or hypomanic symptoms are present: elevated mood, grandiosity, pressured speech, flight of ideas or racing thoughts, increased energy or goal-directed activity, risky pleasurable activities, and decreased need for sleep. In both cases the mixed symptoms must be observable by others and must not be caused by a substance.

Look closely at those two lists and you will see what is missing from them: distractibility, irritability, psychomotor agitation and insomnia. These symptoms occur at both poles, so the committee left them out deliberately, to stop every agitated, sleepless depression from being counted as mixed. If a person meets full criteria for mania and for a major depressive episode at the same time, the episode is recorded as manic, with mixed features, because full mania carries the greater impairment and risk. Mixed features matter clinically. Depressive symptoms appear in about 35 percent of manic episodes, and their presence predicts a poorer outcome, a poorer response to lithium and more suicidal behaviour. Mixed features in a major depressive episode are a significant risk factor for the later development of bipolar I or II disorder. ICD-11, by contrast, keeps the mixed episode as a distinct episode type (Section 5.1).

The second departure is speed. In 1974 David L. Dunner and Ronald R. Fieve, studying patients in New York whose lithium treatment had failed to prevent relapse, noticed that the failures were concentrated among those who had had many episodes a year, and named the pattern rapid cycling. DSM-5-TR's specifier "with rapid cycling" requires at least four mood episodes in the previous 12 months, each meeting the full duration and symptom criteria for a manic, hypomanic or major depressive episode. Episodes count as separate if they are divided by a partial or full remission of at least two months or by a switch to the opposite pole. Manic and hypomanic episodes are counted as being on the same pole, the episodes may come in any order, and episodes directly caused by a substance or a medical condition are excluded. Rapid cycling is a specifier of bipolar I and bipolar II disorder only.

Rapid cycling is common, associated with poorer outcomes, and in most studies far commoner in women. Barlow and Durand cite estimates that 20 to 50 percent of patients with bipolar disorder experience rapid cycling at some point and that 60 to 90 percent of rapid cyclers are female. Two precipitants are often implicated. Antidepressants, given to people with bipolar disorder, are associated with higher rates of rapid cycling, and hypothyroidism, including the hypothyroidism that lithium itself can cause, is linked with it as well. Rapid cycling responds less well to standard treatment, including lithium; antidepressants are avoided, and treatment rests on the mood stabilisers (Section 5.7). It is often not permanent: in one follow-up about 80 percent of rapid cyclers had returned to a slower pattern within two years.

Faster patterns still are described in the research literature: ultra-rapid cycling, with episodes lasting days, and ultradian cycling, with shifts within a single day. These are not DSM categories, and they are hard to tell apart from mixed states. Notice what this means for the popular idea that a person with bipolar disorder is someone whose mood "changes every few hours". In the ordinary course of the illness, episodes last weeks to months. Mood that swings several times a day in response to events is far more typical of borderline personality disorder, or of ordinary life, than of bipolar disorder.

Exam trap

The specifier with mixed features applied to a manic or hypomanic episode requires concurrent depressive symptoms (three or more), not psychosis, anxiety or substance withdrawal; applied to a depressive episode it requires concurrent manic symptoms. Anxiety has its own specifier, with anxious distress, which applies to manic, hypomanic and depressive episodes alike; so for a manic episode, "with mixed features", "with psychotic features" and "with anxious distress" are all genuine specifiers, whereas "with limited prosocial emotions" belongs to conduct disorder (Chapter 11). Rapid cycling means four or more full episodes in 12 months and applies only to bipolar I and II; it is not a diagnosis in itself, not four mood swings in a day, and not cyclothymia, whose swings never reach episode level.

The course over a lifetime

To understand a recurrent illness you have to see it over years, not weeks, and the clearest way to do that is a life chart: a timeline on which each episode is drawn above or below a baseline of normal mood, its height showing severity and its width duration, with life events and treatments marked underneath. Kraepelin drew such charts for his patients, and Robert Post and colleagues at the National Institute of Mental Health turned them into a standard research and clinical tool, the NIMH Life Chart Method. Figure 5.8 is Rahul's chart, built from his own account and his family's, and continued for eight years beyond the admission described at the start of this section.

Rahul's chart shows most of what is known about the course of bipolar I disorder. The illness often begins with a depression, and the first mania may come years later; until it does, the correct diagnosis is impossible. More than 90 percent of people who have one manic episode go on to have more, and about 60 percent of manic episodes are immediately followed by a major depressive episode, as both of Rahul's were. About half of patients show a predominant polarity, tending to relapse mainly into depression or mainly into mania, and the polarity of the first episode tends to predict which. A manic episode with psychotic features tends to be followed by later episodes with psychotic features, and mixed features predict a worse course.

Although mania is the dramatic face of the illness, depression is its long companion. Lewis L. Judd and colleagues followed 146 people with bipolar I disorder for nearly 13 years in the NIMH Collaborative Depression Study, rating their symptoms week by week, and published the results in 2002. The patients had symptoms of some kind in almost half of all weeks, and depressive symptoms outnumbered manic or hypomanic ones by more than three to one. In a companion study of bipolar II disorder (2003) the imbalance was far greater: depressive symptoms in about half of all weeks, hypomanic symptoms in hardly any. This is why people with bipolar disorder usually seek help when depressed, and why the depressive pole drives so much of the disability and suicide risk.

Left untreated, episodes tend to come closer together as the illness goes on. Kraepelin noticed that the well intervals between episodes shortened over the first several episodes, DSM-5-TR notes the same narrowing of intervals with age in bipolar II disorder, and Post's kindling hypothesis (Section 5.5) was an attempt to explain it. Between episodes, many people return to full functioning, but recovery of work and social life lags well behind recovery from symptoms. About 30 percent of people with bipolar disorder show severe impairment in their work role, and many end up at a lower socioeconomic level than their education would predict. Problems of attention, memory and executive function persist even when mood is normal.

The most serious outcome is suicide. DSM-5-TR estimates that the lifetime risk of suicide in bipolar disorder is 20 to 30 times that of the general population and that about 5 to 6 percent of people with bipolar disorders die by suicide. Women make more attempts and men more often die. The risk is concentrated in depressive and mixed states, and it rises with a past attempt, with the proportion of time spent depressed, and with alcohol use disorder. Lithium in particular has good evidence of lowering suicide risk (Sections 5.7 and 5.9).

There is a brighter thread in the long history of the illness. Since antiquity people have wondered whether madness and genius are allied, and bipolar disorder is the case that has been studied most closely. The American psychologist Kay Redfield Jamison (born 1946) examined the lives of poets, painters and composers in Touched with Fire: Manic-Depressive Illness and the Artistic Temperament (1993) and found mood disorders, and suicides, strikingly over-represented among them. In an earlier study (1989) she had interviewed 47 eminent British writers and artists and found that 38 percent had been treated for a mood disorder, far above any population rate; half of the poets had needed medication or hospitalisation, the largest share of any group. Nancy C. Andreasen found similarly high rates of mood disorder in 1987 among writers at the University of Iowa Writers' Workshop. Jamison herself has bipolar I disorder, and her memoir An Unquiet Mind (1995) describes both the exhilaration of her early highs and the devastation that followed; with Frederick Goodwin she also wrote the standard textbook, Manic-Depressive Illness (1990, second edition 2007). Large Swedish register studies by Simon Kyaga and colleagues, covering more than a million people, later found people with bipolar disorder, and their healthy relatives, over-represented in creative professions. The relationship seems not to be simple. DSM-5-TR notes that greater creative accomplishment is associated with milder forms of bipolar disorder and that higher creativity is found in unaffected relatives, which suggests that some of the same genes, in smaller doses, may carry the spark.

Clinical link

Many people with bipolar disorder stop their medication, and two reasons recur. The first is that they miss the highs: hypomania feels like the self at its best, and life on a mood stabiliser can feel flat by comparison. The second is a fear, especially among artists and students, that treatment will dull their minds. Both deserve a serious hearing rather than a lecture. Useful facts for such a conversation: relapse after stopping lithium is common and often comes within months, as it did for Rahul; abrupt discontinuation carries a particular risk of early relapse; and when Mogens Schou in Denmark questioned 24 artists whose episodes lithium had brought under control (1979), 12 said their productivity had increased, 6 that it was unchanged and 6 that it had fallen. Psychoeducation, family-focused therapy and interpersonal and social rhythm therapy (Section 5.7) all target adherence.

Missed, mistaken and stretched: the problems of diagnosis

Bipolar disorder is one of the most frequently misdiagnosed conditions in psychiatry, and the errors run in three directions. It is missed when it is taken for unipolar depression, mistaken when it is called schizophrenia or a personality disorder, and stretched when its boundaries are widened to take in children's tantrums and ordinary moodiness.

The commonest error is to diagnose unipolar depression. DSM-5-TR gives three reasons: the first episode of bipolar disorder is often depressive, depressive symptoms are the most frequent symptoms across the long-term course, and depression is what people seek help for. In a 2000 survey of 600 support-group members of the American National Depressive and Manic-Depressive Association, reported by Robert M. A. Hirschfeld and colleagues in 2003, 69 percent of people with bipolar disorder said they had first been misdiagnosed, most often with unipolar depression. Because the criteria for a major depressive episode are identical in both disorders, nothing in the depression itself settles the matter; only a history of past hypomania or mania does. DSM-5-TR lists clues that should make a clinician ask especially carefully when a depressed person is in front of them: a family history of bipolar disorder, onset in the early twenties, many previous episodes, psychotic features, and a history of not responding to antidepressants or of becoming manic on them. Atypical features and a postpartum onset are further warnings. Brief self-report screens such as the Mood Disorder Questionnaire and the Hypomania Checklist help (they are described in the Assessment book), but the decisive step is a careful history from the patient and someone who knows them, as Geeta's husband showed. The error matters because an antidepressant given alone to a person with bipolar disorder can trigger a switch into mania or a rapid-cycling course.

The second error is to call mania schizophrenia. In the US-UK Diagnostic Project, published in 1972, John E. Cooper, Robert E. Kendell and colleagues found that psychiatrists in New York diagnosed schizophrenia far more often than psychiatrists in London; when the same patients were assessed with a standard interview, many of New York's "schizophrenics" turned out to have manic-depressive illness. Florid psychotic mania, especially when seen at Carlson and Goodwin's stage III, can be indistinguishable from acute schizophrenia in a single interview, and first-rank symptoms are no guarantee, as Pope and Lipinski showed. DSM-5-TR adds that in the United States African Americans with bipolar I disorder are more likely to be misdiagnosed with schizophrenia, through under-recognition of mood symptoms, cultural misunderstanding (cultural mistrust misread as paranoia), delayed access to care and shorter assessments. The boundary with schizoaffective disorder is drawn by the timing of psychosis: if delusions or hallucinations have been present for two weeks or more without any mood episode, the diagnosis moves towards schizoaffective disorder; if psychosis has occurred only during mood episodes, it is bipolar disorder with psychotic features (Chapter 4).

Two conditions of temperament and development cause confusion of a different kind. In borderline personality disorder (Chapter 11) mood is unstable and behaviour impulsive, but the shifts last hours rather than days, are usually provoked by events in relationships, and move among anxiety, anger, emptiness and sadness without sustained elation or a reduced need for sleep; and they are a lasting trait rather than a distinct episode. The two disorders also genuinely co-occur, and a bipolar diagnosis should be added only when there is a distinct episode or a clear increase over the person's usual baseline. In attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder (Chapter 3), distractibility, talkativeness and restlessness resemble hypomania, but ADHD begins by age 12 and is continuous, whereas mania comes in episodes, typically beginning in late adolescence or early adulthood.

The third error, stretching, became a public controversy in the United States. During the 1990s some clinicians began to diagnose bipolar disorder in children whose main problem was chronic, severe irritability and explosive temper, even without distinct episodes. Carmen Moreno and colleagues reported in 2007 that office visits by young people resulting in a bipolar diagnosis had increased about 40-fold between 1994 to 1995 and 2002 to 2003, and many of those children were given antipsychotic drugs. Follow-up studies then showed that chronically irritable children grow up to have depression and anxiety, not bipolar disorder. DSM-5's answer was a new diagnosis, disruptive mood dysregulation disorder (Section 5.3), and an insistence, repeated in DSM-5-TR, that mania in a child must be a distinct episode representing a clear change from the child's own baseline.

Stretching has also been argued for in adults, under the banner of the bipolar spectrum. Hagop S. Akiskal (1944 to 2021), working in Memphis and San Diego, argued from the 1970s onwards that between classic manic-depressive illness and unipolar depression lies a continuum of "soft" bipolar conditions. With Olavo Pinto in 1999 he sketched a ladder of types: bipolar I, bipolar II, depressions superimposed on a cyclothymic temperament, hypomania that appears only on antidepressants (his bipolar III), and depressions superimposed on a lifelong hyperthymic temperament (cheerful, energetic, overconfident; his bipolar IV). Jules Angst's long follow-up of a Zurich community cohort found that hypomanias lasting only one to three days were common and behaved like longer ones. Spectrum thinking explains why so many "unipolar" patients have some hypomanic symptoms, and it helped win the inclusion of the mixed features specifier and the "other specified" examples in DSM-5. Its critics answer that a broad spectrum invites over-diagnosis: when Mark Zimmerman and colleagues re-interviewed psychiatric outpatients in Rhode Island who said they had been diagnosed with bipolar disorder, a structured interview confirmed the diagnosis in fewer than half (2008). Both errors are real. Table 5.16 summarises the boundaries.

Table 5.16 Telling bipolar disorder from its look-alikes.
ConditionWhat overlapsWhat separates it from bipolar disorder
Major depressive disorderDepressive episodes are identicalNo hypomania or mania ever; ask the person and an informant
SchizophreniaDelusions, hallucinations, disorganised speechIn bipolar disorder psychosis occurs only during mood episodes and is usually mood-congruent
Schizoaffective disorderMood episodes with psychosisPsychosis for 2 weeks or more without a mood episode
Borderline personality disorderMood lability, impulsivity, self-harmShifts last hours and follow interpersonal events; no sustained elation or reduced need for sleep; a trait, not an episode
ADHDDistractibility, talkativeness, restlessnessOnset by age 12; continuous rather than episodic
Disruptive mood dysregulation disorderSevere irritability in a childChronic irritability and outbursts, without a distinct episode of grandiosity and reduced sleep
Substance-induced or medical causeA full manic syndromeTimed with a drug or illness; settles within about a month of stopping the drug
Exam trap

When a question asks what best distinguishes a primary psychotic disorder from a mood disorder with psychotic features, the answer is the mood congruence of the psychotic symptoms (together with their confinement to mood episodes). "Presence of delusions" cannot separate them, since both have delusions; the duration of depressive episodes and comorbid anxiety are irrelevant. For the differential with unipolar depression, the deciding fact is a history of hypomania or mania, never anything in the current depressive episode, whose criteria are identical in both disorders.

5.5 Biological CausesLower yield · 1 Q

When John Cade's lithium calmed a man who had been manic for years (the story that opened this chapter), it carried a message that went beyond one patient. If a simple salt can switch an illness off, the illness is likely, at least in part, to be a matter of biology. In the decades since, researchers have followed that thought into genes, neurotransmitters, hormones, sleep, brain circuits and the immune system. Two cautions will help you weigh what they found. First, most of the evidence concerns depression; where bipolar disorder differs, that is said. Second, a biological difference found in depressed people may be a cause of the illness, a consequence of it, or a scar it leaves behind, and many of the findings in this section have been hard to sort into those three. The basic workings of synapses, hormones and sleep belong to the Physiological book, so only what the mood disorders need is explained here. The psychological and social causes follow in Section 5.6, and the two sets meet in the diathesis-stress model (Book 2, Section 1.7).

Genes: family, twin, adoption and molecular studies

The first question to ask of any disorder is whether it runs in families. In a family study researchers start from a person with the disorder, the proband, and count how often it appears among his first-degree relatives (parents, siblings and children), compared with the relatives of people without it. For the mood disorders the answer is clear. Across studies the rate of mood disorder in the relatives of probands is consistently about two to three times the rate in the relatives of controls, and DSM-5-TR puts the risk of major depressive disorder in the first-degree relatives of a depressed person at two to four times that of the general population, higher still when the proband's depression began early or keeps recurring. Bipolar disorder runs in families even more strongly. Against a general-population risk of about 1 percent, a first-degree relative of a person with bipolar disorder has a risk of about 5 to 10 percent, roughly a tenfold increase.

Family studies also revealed an asymmetry. The relatives of bipolar probands have raised rates of bipolar disorder and of unipolar depression, whereas the relatives of unipolar probands have raised rates mainly of unipolar depression. That pattern was one of the reasons Karl Leonhard, Jules Angst and Carlo Perris separated unipolar from bipolar illness (Section 5.1). But families share kitchens, quarrels and poverty as well as genes, so a family study alone cannot tell inheritance from upbringing.

Twin studies can. Identical, or monozygotic, twins share all their genes; fraternal, or dizygotic, twins share on average half, like any brothers and sisters. If a disorder is partly genetic, an identical co-twin should share it more often than a fraternal one. The proportion of pairs in which both twins have the disorder is the concordance rate, and from the gap between identical and fraternal concordance researchers estimate heritability, the proportion of the variation in risk within a population that is due to genetic differences. In a careful study of the Maudsley Hospital twin register in London published in 2003, Peter McGuffin and colleagues found that when one twin had bipolar disorder, the co-twin had a mood disorder of some kind in 66.7 percent of identical pairs but only 18.9 percent of fraternal pairs; when the first twin had unipolar depression, the figures were 45.6 and 20.2 percent. They estimated the heritability of bipolar disorder at about 85 percent. Other studies have given figures from about 60 percent (a Swedish national study of families in 2009) to around 90 percent in some twin samples, which places bipolar disorder among the most heritable conditions in psychiatry, alongside schizophrenia. Yet even for bipolar disorder the concordance of identical twins is only about 40 to 70 percent. If genes were everything, it would be 100 percent.

Major depressive disorder is heritable too, but less so. In a meta-analysis of the twin studies published in 2000, Patrick F. Sullivan, Michael C. Neale and Kenneth S. Kendler estimated its heritability at 37 percent, and DSM-5-TR gives about 40 percent. Several studies find it higher in women than in men; in an Australian register of 2,662 twin pairs, for example, heritability was 36 to 44 percent in women and 18 to 24 percent in men. Two further findings from twin research matter for theory. The environmental part of the risk comes mainly from experiences unique to each twin (the non-shared environment) rather than from the home they grew up in together, and, in Kendler's studies of female twins in Virginia, the same genes appeared to raise the risk of both depression and generalised anxiety, with psychological and social factors deciding which disorder appears. DSM-5-TR adds that the personality trait of neuroticism carries a substantial share of the genetic liability to depression.

Twin studies also say something about the two poles. Among identical twin pairs who are both affected, about 80 percent are also concordant for polarity: if one is unipolar, the other usually is too. McGuffin's group concluded that the genetic liability to depression is largely shared by unipolar and bipolar disorder, whereas the liability to mania is partly separate. Adoption studies point the same way as the twin studies. In a 1977 study, Julien Mendlewicz and John Rainer found mood disorders far more often among the biological parents of adoptees with bipolar disorder than among the parents who had raised them.

Which genes, then? The early hope was for a single major gene, and in 1987 it seemed to have been found. Janice A. Egeland and colleagues reported in Nature that bipolar disorder in a large Old Order Amish family in Pennsylvania was linked to markers on chromosome 11. By 1989 the finding had dissolved: when the family tree was extended and two previously well members fell ill, the linkage disappeared. The lesson has been borne out ever since. Mood disorders are polygenic: they depend on many thousands of common genetic variants, each with a tiny effect, interacting with one another and with the environment. Only huge samples can detect them. Genome-wide association studies, scanning the whole genome in tens or hundreds of thousands of people, found 44 risk variants for major depression in 2018 (Naomi Wray and colleagues) and 102 in 2019 (David Howard and colleagues), and dozens of regions for bipolar disorder, among them genes for an L-type calcium channel (CACNA1C) and for a protein that anchors ion channels in the nerve cell membrane (ANK3). Bipolar disorder shares much of its genetic risk with schizophrenia, and bipolar II disorder appears to be partly distinct from bipolar I. A person's polygenic risk score, the sum of all these small effects, so far explains only a small share of who becomes ill, and it cannot be used to diagnose anyone.

Key study: The serotonin transporter gene and stress (Caspi and colleagues, 2003)

If genes and stress both matter, perhaps a gene matters only under stress. Avshalom Caspi, Terrie E. Moffitt and colleagues tested that idea in the Dunedin study, a cohort born in New Zealand in 1972 to 1973 and assessed repeatedly from the age of 3. In 847 members they looked at a variation in the promoter of the serotonin transporter gene, 5-HTTLPR, which comes in a short and a long form, and recorded the stressful life events each person had experienced between the ages of 21 and 26 and whether he or she had been maltreated in childhood. People with one or two short alleles who had suffered several stressful events were much more likely to be depressed at 26 than long-allele carriers with the same number of events. Among those with two short alleles, severe childhood maltreatment raised the probability of adult depression from about 30 to 63 percent, whereas it made no difference to those with two long alleles. Published in Science, the study became one of the most cited in psychiatry, the model example of a gene-environment interaction. It has not survived. A 2009 meta-analysis of 14 studies by Neil Risch and colleagues found no interaction; a broader 2011 review by Katja Karg and colleagues found support, especially for childhood maltreatment; and very large studies since, including a collaborative analysis of more than 38,000 people (2018) and a study of hundreds of thousands of people by Richard Border and colleagues (2019), found no reliable effect of this or any other "candidate gene" for depression. The idea that genes and environment interact survives; the single-gene version of it does not.

One more mechanism connects genes and experience. Experience can change whether genes are switched on or off, through chemical tags on DNA and its packaging proteins, without changing the genetic code itself. Such epigenetic changes are one route by which early adversity might leave a lasting biological mark, and several epigenetic mechanisms have been implicated in depression, though none is yet a usable test.

Exam trap

Bipolar disorder has among the highest heritability of all psychiatric disorders (about 60 to 85 percent, up to about 90 in some twin studies); major depressive disorder is moderately heritable (about 37 to 40 percent, Sullivan, Neale and Kendler, 2000). Neither is "caused solely by genetics": identical twins are concordant for bipolar disorder in only 40 to 70 percent of pairs. Concordance is the share of twin pairs in which both are affected; heritability is a population statistic, not the chance that an individual will inherit the disorder. The Caspi gene-by-stress finding (2003) is famous and should be known, but so should its failure to replicate.

The monoamine hypotheses: how drugs revealed a theory

The most famous biological theory of depression was not deduced from the brain. It was inferred backwards from drugs, discovered by accident in the 1950s. To follow the story you need only a few facts about the monoamines, a family of neurotransmitters that includes noradrenaline (called norepinephrine in American books), serotonin (5-hydroxytryptamine) and dopamine. After a monoamine is released into the synapse and acts on receptors, its action is ended mainly by reuptake, when a transporter pumps it back into the neuron that released it; inside the neuron, an enzyme called monoamine oxidase (MAO) breaks it down. Drugs can raise the amount of a monoamine acting at the synapse by blocking its reuptake or by blocking MAO, and lower it by stopping the neuron from storing it.

The first clue came from India. The snakeroot plant, Rauwolfia serpentina (sarpagandha), had been used in Indian medicine for centuries, and in 1949 the Bombay physician Rustom Jal Vakil reported a trial of it in high blood pressure in the British Heart Journal. Its active ingredient, reserpine, was isolated in 1952 and soon prescribed widely for hypertension; the American psychiatrist Nathan Kline also tried it for schizophrenia. In 1954 the American physician Edward Freis reported that some hypertensive patients treated for long periods with large doses of reserpine had become seriously depressed. At about the same time pharmacologists found that reserpine empties the stores of monoamines in nerve terminals, and that reserpine-treated animals became sluggish and immobile. Later reviews of the clinical reports suggested that true depressive illness had occurred in only a minority of patients, some of whom had been depressed before, but the inference was irresistible: deplete the monoamines and mood may fall.

The second clue was the mirror image. Iproniazid, a drug developed for tuberculosis, proved a disappointment against the disease, but in the early 1950s doctors noticed that tuberculosis patients receiving it became cheerful and energetic, much less concerned about their illness than they had any right to be. It was found to inhibit MAO, and after Kline and others tested it in depressed patients, iproniazid was marketed as the first antidepressant in 1957. The third clue arrived the same year from Switzerland. Roland Kuhn (1912 to 2005), a psychiatrist at the cantonal hospital at Münsterlingen, was testing a compound that the Geigy company hoped would be a second chlorpromazine. It did nothing for schizophrenia, but it lifted what Kuhn called vital depression, and in 1957 he reported the effect. The compound was imipramine, the first tricyclic antidepressant. Within a few years Julius Axelrod showed that drugs of this kind block the reuptake of noradrenaline, work that earned him a share of the Nobel Prize in 1970. So both new classes of antidepressant raised the monoamines at the synapse, while the drug that depleted them could cause depression. The drugs themselves are taught in Section 5.7; here they matter as evidence.

The theory was put into words in 1965 by Joseph J. Schildkraut (1934 to 2006) of Harvard in a review in the American Journal of Psychiatry that became the most cited paper the journal had ever published. His catecholamine hypothesis proposed that some, if not all, depressions are associated with an absolute or relative deficiency of catecholamines, particularly noradrenaline, at functionally important receptor sites in the brain, and that elation, conversely, may be associated with an excess. (The catecholamines are noradrenaline, adrenaline and dopamine.) Schildkraut himself was careful to say that the data then available could neither confirm nor reject the hypothesis. Two years later the British psychiatrist Alec Coppen argued that serotonin deficiency was the more important, a view sometimes called the serotonin hypothesis or indoleamine hypothesis, and later supported by findings of low levels of serotonin's breakdown product, 5-HIAA, in the cerebrospinal fluid of some depressed patients, especially those who made violent suicide attempts (Section 5.9). The two ideas are often joined as the monoamine hypothesis of depression: depression results from underactivity at noradrenergic and serotonergic synapses.

A cleverer version tried to explain why depression and mania can occur in the same person. In 1974 Arthur J. Prange and colleagues proposed the permissive hypothesis. Low serotonin, on this view, does not itself cause either pole. It is a vulnerability that "permits" the noradrenaline system to swing out of control: with low serotonin, low noradrenaline produces depression and high noradrenaline produces mania. Serotonin is the dampener whose failure lets the catecholamines range too widely. The theory neatly explains why a single vulnerability could underlie both poles, and it fits the observation that people with low serotonin function are more impulsive and more labile in mood. Dopamine has its own place in this story, mainly at the elevated pole. Amphetamine and cocaine, which release dopamine, produce states that resemble mania; the dopamine precursor L-dopa can produce hypomania in people with bipolar disorder; and dopamine-blocking antipsychotic drugs treat mania. Low dopamine activity in the brain's reward system is also a candidate explanation for anhedonia, the loss of pleasure in depression.

For all its elegance, the monoamine theory ran into problems, and they are among the most examined points in this chapter. Table 5.17 sets them beside the hypotheses.

  • The time lag. An antidepressant blocks reuptake or MAO within hours of the first dose, but mood does not begin to lift for two to four weeks or more. If low monoamines were the cause, correcting them should help at once.
  • Depletion does not cause depression in everyone. In tryptophan depletion studies, volunteers drink an amino acid mixture without tryptophan, the building block of serotonin, which lowers brain serotonin for a few hours. In a key study in 1990 Pedro Delgado and colleagues found that this brought back depressive symptoms in many patients who had recovered on antidepressants.
