Later work showed that it lowers mood mainly in people who are vulnerable (those who have been depressed, or have a family history) and hardly at all in healthy people without such a history. Low serotonin, then, is not sufficient to cause depression on its own; it may unmask a vulnerability or undo a drug's effect.
  • The drugs are modestly effective and not specific. Many depressed patients do not respond, and in trials the advantage of antidepressants over placebo is real but modest. The largest analysis, a network meta-analysis of 522 trials by Andrea Cipriani and colleagues in 2018, found all 21 antidepressants studied more effective than placebo, but the effects were small to moderate. Drugs acting on quite different systems also relieve depression, among them ketamine, which works through the glutamate system within hours (Section 5.7).
  • Direct evidence of low monoamines is inconsistent. Measurements of monoamines and their breakdown products in blood, urine and cerebrospinal fluid have given mixed results, and the abnormalities found apply to subgroups rather than to depression as a whole. In 2022 Joanna Moncrieff and colleagues published an umbrella review of the research on serotonin and depression and concluded that it provided no consistent evidence that depression is caused by lowered serotonin activity or concentrations. Critics replied that few researchers had believed the simple "low serotonin" version for many years, that the review left out some relevant evidence, and that the review said nothing about whether antidepressants work, which is a separate question answered by treatment trials. The fairest summary is that the simple "chemical imbalance" story told in advertisements is not supported, while the monoamine systems remain part of a more complicated picture.

The time lag prompted the first revision. If the drugs work only after weeks, the important change must be something the brain does in response to the drug over weeks. In 1975 Jerzy Vetulani and Fridolin Sulser showed that chronic, but not acute, treatment with antidepressants reduced the sensitivity of noradrenergic receptors (the beta-adrenergic receptor system) in the brains of rats, on a time scale that matched the clinical response. Pinel and Barnes add that autopsy studies found more noradrenaline and serotonin receptors in the brains of depressed people who had not been treated, a sign of up-regulation, the compensatory increase in receptors that follows a shortage of transmitter. The receptor sensitivity hypothesis holds that depression involves abnormally sensitive or numerous receptors, and that antidepressants work by gradually adjusting them. For serotonin drugs a similar delayed change was proposed: the autoreceptors that normally brake serotonin neurons gradually become less sensitive, so that serotonin release rises only after some weeks. These ideas moved attention downstream from the synapse into the cell, and from there to the growth and survival of neurons themselves, a story taken up below, after the stress hormones.

Table 5.17 The monoamine hypotheses of mood disorder and their problems.
HypothesisWho and whenCentral claimMain problem
CatecholamineJoseph Schildkraut, 1965Depression from a deficiency of noradrenaline; mania from an excessDrugs act in hours, mood lifts in weeks
Serotonin (indoleamine)Alec Coppen, 1967Depression from low serotonin functionTryptophan depletion lowers mood only in vulnerable people
PermissiveArthur Prange and colleagues, 1974Low serotonin permits noradrenaline to swing low (depression) or high (mania)Hard to test directly; too simple for the evidence
DopamineDeveloped from stimulant and antipsychotic effectsHigh dopamine in mania; low reward-system dopamine in anhedoniaEffects not specific to mood disorders
Receptor sensitivityVetulani and Sulser, 1975, and othersAbnormal receptor sensitivity; antidepressants adjust it over weeksExplains the lag but not why receptors change
Exam trap

Schildkraut (1965) proposed the catecholamine hypothesis, centred on noradrenaline, not serotonin; the serotonin version is associated with Coppen (1967), and the permissive hypothesis (Prange and colleagues, 1974) says low serotonin permits noradrenaline to swing either way. The monoamine theory was built by reasoning backwards from drug effects (reserpine depletes monoamines and lowered mood in some patients; iproniazid and imipramine raise them). The classic objection is the delay: synaptic effects in hours, clinical benefit in weeks. Do not write that the 2022 Moncrieff review showed that antidepressants do not work; it was about the cause of depression, not the effect of treatment.

Stress hormones: the HPA axis and the thyroid

Investigators first suspected the endocrine system for a simple reason: people with certain hormonal diseases become depressed. Patients with Cushing's disease, in which a pituitary tumour drives the adrenal glands to pour out too much cortisol, very often develop depression (and sometimes mania), and patients with an underactive thyroid look strikingly like depressed patients, slowed, tired, heavy and low. Two hormonal systems have dominated the research since.

The first is the hypothalamic-pituitary-adrenal axis, or HPA axis, the body's main stress-hormone system. When a threat is perceived, the hypothalamus releases corticotropin-releasing hormone (CRH); CRH signals the pituitary gland to release adrenocorticotropic hormone (ACTH) into the blood; and ACTH stimulates the cortex of the adrenal glands to release cortisol. Cortisol mobilises energy for the emergency and then, acting on receptors in the hippocampus, hypothalamus and pituitary, switches the system off again. This is a negative feedback loop, like a thermostat. In many people with depression, especially severe or melancholic depression, the loop runs hot: cortisol output over 24 hours is raised, the normal daily rhythm of cortisol is flattened, and the adrenal glands may even be enlarged. The finding fits the close link between severe life stress and the onset of depression.

Key study: The dexamethasone suppression test (Carroll, 1968 and 1981)

If the HPA axis is overactive in depression, its brake should be weak. Dexamethasone is a synthetic glucocorticoid that mimics cortisol at the feedback sites, so in a healthy person a dose of it at night suppresses the body's own cortisol for the whole of the next day. The Australian psychiatrist Bernard J. Carroll (1940 to 2018) and colleagues reported in 1968 that severely depressed patients resisted this suppression, and in 1981 Carroll's group in Michigan published a standardised version: 1 mg of dexamethasone by mouth at 11 p.m., with blood cortisol measured at intervals the following day. Cortisol that escaped above a set level was non-suppression, read as a sign that the patient's adrenal output was strong enough to overwhelm the feedback signal. About half of depressed patients, more in severe and psychotic depression, showed it, and Carroll presented the dexamethasone suppression test as a specific laboratory test for melancholia. The 1981 paper became one of the most cited in psychiatry, because it promised the first biological test for a psychiatric disorder. The promise failed. Non-suppression turned up in anxiety disorders, dementia, eating disorders, alcohol withdrawal, recent weight loss and even acute grief, and in 1987 an American Psychiatric Association task force concluded that the test had little place in routine diagnosis. It survives as a research tool and as evidence that HPA overactivity is part of the biology of severe depression; non-suppression usually normalises as the patient recovers.

Attention then moved upstream to CRH itself. In 1984 Charles Nemeroff and colleagues reported raised concentrations of CRH in the cerebrospinal fluid of depressed patients, and later work found fewer CRH receptors in the frontal cortex of people who had died by suicide, as if the receptors had been turned down in response to too much CRH. Injected into animals, CRH produces many of the signs of depression: poor appetite, disturbed sleep, reduced interest in sex and heightened anxiety. Early adversity appears to set the system's thermostat high. In a study published in 2000, Christine Heim, Nemeroff and colleagues found that women who had been abused in childhood produced exaggerated ACTH and cortisol responses to a laboratory stressor in adulthood, especially if they were currently depressed. This is one of the most plausible routes by which early experience might become a lasting biological vulnerability.

The second system is the thyroid. Hypothyroidism can mimic depression closely, which is why thyroid function is checked in every new case. Mild, subclinical hypothyroidism is more common among depressed patients than in the general population, and about a quarter of depressed patients show a blunted rise in thyroid-stimulating hormone (TSH) when they are given thyrotropin-releasing hormone (TRH), the hypothalamic signal that normally triggers it. Arthur Prange, of the permissive hypothesis, showed in 1969 that adding small doses of the thyroid hormone triiodothyronine (T3) speeded and strengthened the response to imipramine, and T3 augmentation is still used for depression that responds poorly to an antidepressant alone (Section 5.7). In bipolar disorder thyroid problems are linked with rapid cycling; lithium itself can cause hypothyroidism; and high doses of thyroid hormone have been tried in rapid-cycling patients. Sex hormones also matter, as the timing of many depressions around puberty, the premenstrual phase, childbirth and the perimenopause shows (Sections 5.2 and 5.3).

Exam trap

In depression the HPA axis is overactive, not underactive: cortisol is raised, and on the dexamethasone suppression test about half of severely depressed patients fail to suppress cortisol (non-suppression). "Depressed patients show exaggerated suppression" is the reverse of the truth. The test is not a diagnostic test for depression, because non-suppression also occurs in many other conditions. Hypothyroidism mimics depression; T3 is used to augment antidepressants.

Neuroplasticity: the neurotrophic hypothesis

Two threads now meet. The monoamine story left a puzzle about the time lag, and the stress-hormone story raised a worry: long exposure to high cortisol is harmful to neurons, particularly in the hippocampus, the structure that helps to switch the HPA axis off and that is crucial for memory. In 1997 Ronald S. Duman, George Heninger and Eric J. Nestler of Yale drew the threads together in a molecular and cellular theory of depression, now usually called the neurotrophic hypothesis or neuroplasticity theory of depression. Stress, through cortisol and other signals, reduces the production of brain-derived neurotrophic factor (BDNF), a protein that keeps neurons healthy, helps them grow new connections and supports the birth of new neurons in the adult hippocampus. Starved of BDNF, neurons in the hippocampus and prefrontal cortex shrink and lose connections, and depression follows. Antidepressants, on this account, work not by raising monoamines as such but by triggering, over weeks, a rise in BDNF and a recovery of neuroplasticity. That timing is exactly what the monoamine theory could not explain.

The evidence comes from several directions. Brain imaging shows that the hippocampus is smaller in people with long-standing recurrent depression; Yvette Sheline and colleagues reported this in 1996, and later work linked the reduction to the total time a person had spent depressed. Barlow and Durand describe a finding that suggests the difference may come before the illness: healthy girls whose mothers had recurrent depression already had smaller hippocampi than girls whose mothers were well. In rats, chronic, but not acute, treatment with antidepressants increases the birth of new neurons in the hippocampus, as do electroconvulsive shocks and exercise, and in 2003 Luca Santarelli, René Hen and colleagues showed in mice that when this neurogenesis was blocked by irradiating the hippocampus, antidepressants no longer had their behavioural effects. In people, blood levels of BDNF tend to be low during depression and to rise with successful treatment, but only in those who improve, which has led to proposals that BDNF might one day serve as a biomarker. Ketamine fits the theory too: Duman's laboratory showed in 2010 that it rapidly triggers the growth of new synaptic connections in the prefrontal cortex of rats, a plausible reason for its action within hours.

The theory has its own problems. How much new neurons are born in the adult human hippocampus is still debated. Low BDNF is not specific to depression; it is found in bipolar disorder (in both poles) and in other conditions. And as with the monoamines, it is hard to tell whether reduced plasticity causes depression or results from it. Still, the neurotrophic hypothesis moved the field from thinking of depression as a shortage of a chemical to thinking of it as a failure of the brain's ability to adapt, and that shift has shaped almost all later work.

Sleep and biological rhythms

Disturbed sleep is so common in mood disorders that it appears in the criteria for both poles: insomnia or hypersomnia in depression, decreased need for sleep in mania. Sleep laboratories have shown that the disturbance goes deeper than how many hours are slept. Normally, after falling asleep, a person passes through progressively deeper stages of non-REM sleep and enters the first period of rapid eye movement (REM) sleep, the stage most associated with dreaming, after about 90 minutes; REM periods then lengthen as the night goes on. In depression, REM sleep arrives much sooner. This shortened REM latency (the time from falling asleep to the first REM period) was proposed as a biological marker of depression by David J. Kupfer in 1976. Depressed people also have more intense REM sleep, with more eye movements (raised REM density), more REM in the first part of the night, less deep slow-wave sleep, and more broken sleep with early waking. Some of these features persist after recovery in people with recurrent depression, as if they were part of the vulnerability rather than only a symptom, although none of them is specific to depression.

Two observations suggest that the sleep changes are more than side effects. Almost all the older antidepressants suppress REM sleep, and in the 1970s Gerald Vogel reported that selectively depriving depressed patients of REM sleep, by waking them whenever it began, could itself relieve depression. More striking still, keeping a depressed person awake for a whole night, or for the second half of it, often lifts the depression by the next day. Barlow and Durand note that the improvement is temporary: the depression returns once the person sleeps normally again, in sleep-laboratory studies sometimes after a single nap. In people with bipolar disorder, the same sleep loss that relieves depression can tip them into mania. Thomas Wehr and colleagues argued in 1987 that sleep reduction is a "final common pathway" to mania: whatever starts an episode, whether a family crisis, a new baby, exam nights or long-distance travel across time zones, often does so by cutting sleep, and lost sleep then feeds the mania that further reduces sleep. Rahul's nine sleepless nights were not just a symptom of his episode but part of its engine, and his mother later recalled that it had begun during the late nights of his sister's wedding preparations.

Sleep is one of many daily rhythms set by an internal biological clock, and that clock is kept on time by external cues called zeitgebers (German for "time givers"), the most powerful of which is light. In 1988 Cindy L. Ehlers, Ellen Frank and Kupfer proposed the social zeitgeber theory of depression. Human beings, they pointed out, also keep time by social cues: the hour the alarm goes off, when meals are eaten, when the spouse comes home, when work starts. Life events can disrupt these social zeitgebers (a spouse who used to wake you and cook breakfast dies; a baby arrives; a job moves to night shifts) and the disrupted social rhythms destabilise biological rhythms of sleep, hormones and temperature. In vulnerable people the resulting disorder of rhythms triggers an episode. The theory explains why some life events that are not losses at all, such as a promotion with new hours, can set off episodes. A Pittsburgh study in 1998 found that events which disrupted social rhythms preceded manic episodes more often than depressive ones. The theory gave rise to a treatment, interpersonal and social rhythm therapy, which helps people with bipolar disorder keep regular daily routines (Section 5.7). The seasonal pattern of some mood disorders, and its treatment with bright light, are described in Section 5.3.

Exam trap

Depression shortens REM latency (REM comes early), raises REM density and reduces slow-wave sleep; it does not lengthen REM latency. Total or late-night sleep deprivation improves depression briefly; in bipolar disorder sleep loss can trigger mania. A zeitgeber is a "time giver"; the social zeitgeber theory is that of Ehlers, Frank and Kupfer (1988). Do not confuse it with Seligman's learned helplessness or Lewinsohn's reinforcement theory (Section 5.6).

Kindling and sensitisation: why episodes beget episodes

Kraepelin noticed something odd about his patients' life histories. Their first episodes usually followed some obvious misfortune, but later episodes came more and more often with little or no provocation, as if the illness had learned to run by itself. A century later the American psychiatrist Robert M. Post, of the National Institute of Mental Health, offered an explanation borrowed from epilepsy research.

In the late 1960s the psychologist Graham V. Goddard and his colleagues Dan McIntyre and Curtis Leech gave rats one mild electrical pulse a day through an electrode in the amygdala. At first the pulses did nothing visible. But after a number of days each pulse began to produce a small convulsion, and with continued stimulation the convulsions became stronger until each pulse set off a full seizure; eventually seizures could occur even without stimulation. They called this progressive sensitisation kindling, after the small sticks used to start a fire, and found that the changes it produced were permanent (Pinel and Barnes describe it as an animal model of epilepsy in their chapter on brain damage and neuroplasticity). Post, who had also studied how repeated doses of cocaine produce stronger and stronger behavioural effects in animals, saw a parallel with recurrent mood disorders.

His kindling hypothesis, set out in a much-cited 1992 paper in the American Journal of Psychiatry on the "transduction of psychosocial stress into the neurobiology of recurrent affective disorder", makes four claims. First, early episodes of a mood disorder are typically set off by major stressors. Second, each stressor and each episode leaves a lasting trace in the brain, changes in which genes are switched on, beginning with fast-acting "immediate early" genes and continuing in longer-lasting changes in neuropeptides and receptors, so that the threshold for the next episode is lowered. This has two aspects, often distinguished as stress sensitisation (less and less stress is needed to provoke an episode) and episode sensitisation (episodes themselves make further episodes more likely). Third, with enough repetition episodes begin to occur spontaneously, without any trigger. Fourth, as the threshold falls, episodes come faster, so the well intervals shorten. Figure 5.9 shows the idea.

The hypothesis has real support. In a large sample of women from the Virginia twin registry, Kenneth Kendler, Laura Thornton and Charles Gardner reported in 2000 that the link between stressful life events and the onset of a depressive episode was strongest for first episodes and grew weaker with each successive episode, up to about the ninth, after which it stayed flat, which is just what kindling predicts. Danish case-register studies by Lars Kessing and colleagues found that the risk of a new episode rises with the number of previous episodes, and Pinel and Barnes note that untreated mood episodes in bipolar disorder tend to become both more severe and more frequent. The hypothesis also had a practical influence. It encouraged trials of anticonvulsant drugs, such as carbamazepine, as mood stabilisers (Section 5.7), and it strengthened the case for treating the first episodes vigorously and for long-term prevention, on the argument that every episode prevented also protects against the next.

There are criticisms. Kindling in mood disorders is an analogy, not a demonstration: no one has shown that the brain changes of kindled seizures happen in depression. In a careful review in 2005, Scott Monroe and Kate Harkness pointed out that a weakening link between major life events and later episodes can mean two different things. Either the person has become sensitised, so that minor stresses, which life-event interviews often fail to record, now set off episodes, or the illness has become autonomous and needs no stress at all; the two versions make different predictions and are hard to tell apart. Not every patient follows a kindling course, and some have stable or improving courses. Post also described patients in whom lithium, once stopped, worked less well when it was restarted, but how often this happens is disputed.

Exam trap

The kindling hypothesis of mood disorders (Robert Post, 1992) holds that each episode lowers the threshold for future episodes, so that early episodes follow major stress and later ones need little or none, and episodes come faster. It does not say that bipolar disorder is caused solely by genetics, that antidepressants permanently cure depression, or that anxiety and depression are unrelated. Goddard's kindling (1969) is the animal model of epilepsy on which the analogy rests; the hypothesis about mood disorders is Post's.

The depressed brain: circuits and imaging

Modern imaging has moved the search from single chemicals to circuits: networks of regions that together generate, colour and regulate mood. No single "depression centre" has been found, but a consistent set of differences has emerged (Table 5.18). Structural scans have found reduced grey matter in the prefrontal cortex, the hippocampus, the amygdala and the cingulate cortex, and functional studies find altered activity in frontal, cingulate and insular cortex, the amygdala, the thalamus and the striatum. The broad picture is of a mismatch between regions that generate negative emotion, which are overactive, and the prefrontal regions that should regulate it, which are underactive.

At the top of the hierarchy, the dorsolateral prefrontal cortex, involved in planning, working memory and the deliberate control of emotion, tends to be underactive in depression, which fits the poor concentration, indecision and slowed thinking. Richard J. Davidson's EEG studies found that depressed people tend to show relatively less activity over the left frontal region, which he linked to a weakened approach system, the drive to seek out rewards. Deeper in the brain the amygdala, which detects threat and emotional significance, responds more strongly than usual to sad or threatening faces in depressed people, even when the faces are flashed too briefly to be consciously seen, and this overreaction settles with antidepressant treatment. The reward circuit of the ventral striatum responds weakly to rewards, a plausible basis for anhedonia. And the network of regions active when the mind is at rest and turned inward, the default mode network, appears overconnected in depression, which may be the brain's signature of rumination (Section 5.6).

One small region has drawn particular attention: the subgenual cingulate cortex, the part of the anterior cingulate that lies just below the front of the corpus callosum, known as Brodmann area 25. In 1997 Wayne Drevets and colleagues reported in Nature that its grey matter was reduced in people with familial depression, both unipolar and bipolar. Two years later Helen S. Mayberg showed with PET scanning that when healthy people were made sad, activity in area 25 rose while activity in the dorsolateral prefrontal cortex fell, and that in depressed patients who recovered on fluoxetine the reverse change occurred. Area 25, she concluded, behaves like a hub that sets the brain's emotional tone, and in treatment-resistant depression it appeared stuck "on".

Key study: Stimulating area 25 (Mayberg and colleagues, 2005)

If an overactive area 25 helps keep depression going, could quietening it help patients for whom nothing else had worked? Mayberg, the neurosurgeon Andres Lozano and colleagues in Toronto implanted electrodes in the white matter next to the subgenual cingulate in six patients with severe depression that had failed to respond to drugs, psychotherapy and electroconvulsive therapy, and connected them to a pacemaker-like stimulator under the skin. Some patients described an immediate sense of calm or of a "void" lifting when the current was switched on in the operating theatre. After six months, four of the six had a sustained response, with activity in area 25 reduced on their scans. The report, in Neuron in 2005, made deep brain stimulation for depression famous, and a larger series of 20 patients reported in 2008 found substantial improvement in about 60 percent. But a large sponsored trial that compared real with sham stimulation was stopped early when an interim analysis showed no clear advantage at six months; its report appeared in 2017. The study is best seen as powerful evidence that a specific circuit matters in depression, and as a reminder that small open trials of dramatic treatments often look better than the controlled trials that follow (the treatment itself is in Section 5.7).

Table 5.18 Brain regions implicated in depression.
RegionTypical finding in depressionWhat it may contribute
Dorsolateral prefrontal cortexUnderactive, often more on the leftPoor concentration, slowed thinking, weak control of emotion
Subgenual cingulate (area 25)Reduced grey matter; overactive in sad mood and resistant depressionA hub of negative mood; target of deep brain stimulation
AmygdalaOveractive to sad and threatening facesBias towards negative information
HippocampusSmaller in recurrent, long-standing depressionMemory problems; weaker brake on the HPA axis
Ventral striatum (reward circuit)Weak response to rewardAnhedonia
Default mode networkOverconnected at restRumination

Inflammation and the immune system

When you have a bad bout of flu you feel tired, lose your appetite, want to be left alone and take no pleasure in anything. That cluster, called sickness behaviour, is produced not by the virus directly but by cytokines, the signalling proteins of the immune system, acting on the brain; it is an adaptive response that conserves energy for fighting infection. Its resemblance to depression is obvious, and the inflammation hypothesis takes it seriously. A 2010 meta-analysis by Yekta Dowlati and colleagues found higher blood levels of two inflammatory cytokines, interleukin-6 and tumour necrosis factor alpha, in depressed patients than in controls. When patients with hepatitis C or melanoma are treated with interferon-alpha, a cytokine drug, a substantial minority develop depression, and in a 2001 trial Dominique Musselman and colleagues showed that starting an antidepressant (paroxetine) beforehand reduced that risk. Inflammation is raised by obesity, chronic illness, poor sleep and childhood adversity, all of which are risk factors for depression, so it may be one of the pathways through which they act. Inflammation is not raised in every depressed person, however, and anti-inflammatory drugs have helped only some: in one trial of the anti-inflammatory antibody infliximab (2013), only patients with high levels of inflammation at the start benefited. The current view is that inflammation contributes to a subgroup of depressions, perhaps those with prominent fatigue, sleep change and anhedonia, rather than to depression as a whole.

The biology of bipolar disorder in particular

Much of the biology above applies to both poles: bipolar disorder shows HPA axis dysregulation, low BDNF in both mania and depression, and marked disturbances of circadian rhythm, which are found even in patients' unaffected relatives. What is special about it? The strongest single fact is its heritability, among the highest in psychiatry, and the genetic findings point repeatedly to ion channels and the signalling that controls how excitable neurons are, as the calcium channel gene CACNA1C illustrates. Dopamine, as you saw, is the transmitter most closely tied to the manic pole, and the reward hypersensitivity theory of bipolar disorder, which proposes a reward system that overreacts both to the prospect of reward and to its loss, links that chemistry to the psychology of goal striving (Section 5.6). The clock genes provide another link: mice with a mutation in the Clock gene, reported in 2007, sleep less, are hyperactive and seek reward more, a mania-like profile that lithium reverses.

Lithium itself has been a probe of the illness ever since Cade. It is a simple ion, yet it acts inside neurons on second-messenger systems, the chains of chemical signals that carry a receptor's message into the cell. It blocks an enzyme needed to recycle inositol, a component of one such system, which led Michael Berridge and colleagues in the 1980s (most fully in 1989) to propose the inositol depletion hypothesis: lithium damps down neurons that are firing excessively by starving them of inositol. It also inhibits an enzyme called glycogen synthase kinase 3 (GSK-3), which regulates cell survival and the biological clock, and it appears to protect neurons; people taking lithium tend to have larger grey matter volumes than those who are not. Which of these actions explains its effect on mood is still unknown. Pinel and Barnes point out that progress has been slowed by two gaps: no one fully understands how the mood stabilisers work, and there is no good animal model of a disorder whose essence is the alternation of two opposite states over months.

The biological findings of this section do not add up to a single cause, and they are not meant to. Genes set a level of vulnerability; stress hormones, rhythms, inflammation and the brain's capacity for plasticity are some of the ways that vulnerability and experience meet; kindling describes how the meeting changes over a lifetime. None of these explains why one person in a family becomes ill and another does not, or why an episode starts on a particular day. For that, biology has to be joined by the psychological and social causes in Section 5.6, the two together forming the diathesis-stress model (Book 2, Section 1.7).

Scan Your Brain

Fill in the blanks. The answers are below; cover them first.

  1. A manic episode must last at least , or any length of time if hospitalisation is needed.
  2. If psychotic features are present, an episode that otherwise looks hypomanic is, by definition, .
  3. Bipolar II disorder requires at least one hypomanic episode and at least one episode, and never a manic episode.
  4. Cyclothymic disorder in adults requires at least years of numerous subthreshold hypomanic and depressive periods.
  5. Four or more full mood episodes in the previous 12 months earn the specifier "with ".
  6. The feature that best distinguishes a mood disorder with psychotic features from a primary psychotic disorder is the mood of the psychotic symptoms.
  7. Joseph Schildkraut's 1965 hypothesis linked depression to a deficiency of the catecholamine .
  8. About half of severely depressed patients fail to suppress after a night-time dose of dexamethasone.
  9. In depression the time from falling asleep to the first REM period, the REM , is shortened.
  10. Robert Post's hypothesis holds that each episode lowers the threshold for the next.

Answers: (1) one week, (2) manic, (3) major depressive, (4) 2, (5) rapid cycling, (6) congruence, (7) noradrenaline (norepinephrine), (8) cortisol, (9) latency, (10) kindling (sensitisation).

5.6 Psychological and Social CausesMedium yield · 8 Qs

Section 5.5 showed that mood disorders run in families and that the depressed brain differs from the healthy one in its chemistry, its stress hormones and its sleep. Yet the genes explain only part of the story. The heritability of major depression is only about 37 percent, which means that most of the differences between people in their risk of depression come from somewhere else. Think of two sisters who share half their genes and grew up in the same house. Both lose their jobs in the same month. One is upset for a fortnight and then starts applying for work; the other stops sleeping, stops eating and, six weeks later, cannot get out of bed. What happened in the mind and in the life of the second sister to turn a vulnerability into an episode?

This section takes the psychological and social answers roughly in the order in which they were proposed: the psychodynamic idea of loss, the behavioural idea of lost reward, learned helplessness and its descendants, Aaron Beck's cognitive theory, rumination, the interpersonal theories and the study of life events, and finally the special triggers of bipolar episodes. Each began as a clinician's hunch, and each was then put to the test. As you read, keep one question in mind, because examiners and interviewers both ask it: does the theory describe a cause of depression, or only a feature of being depressed?

Loss and anger turned inward: the psychodynamic theories

The first modern psychological theory of depression came from psychoanalysis. In 1911 Karl Abraham (1877 to 1925), a Berlin psychoanalyst and one of Freud's closest followers, published a paper on the psychoanalytic treatment of "manic-depressive insanity". He noticed that the endless self-accusations of his melancholic patients sounded oddly like accusations aimed at someone else. His suggestion was that the depressed person harbours intense hostility towards a loved person, finds that hostility unbearable, and turns it back against the self.

Sigmund Freud (1856 to 1939) developed the idea in his essay Mourning and Melancholia, written in 1915 and published in 1917. He began with a comparison that still repays thought. Grief (mourning) and severe depression (which he called melancholia) look alike: both bring painful dejection, loss of interest in the outside world, loss of the capacity to love and a slowing of activity. But melancholia has one feature that grief lacks, a fall in self-regard so deep that it pours out in self-reproach and self-revilement, even an expectation of punishment. Freud put the difference in a famous line: "In mourning it is the world which has become poor and empty; in melancholia it is the ego itself."

Why should a loss empty the self rather than the world? Freud's answer had three steps. First, depression follows a loss, which may be real (a death) or symbolic (a rejection, a disappointment, a fall in status), and sometimes the person does not know what has been lost. Second, the person's attachment to the lost person was ambivalent, a mixture of love and hate. Third, rather than letting the lost person go, as the mourner gradually does, the melancholic takes the lost person into the self through introjection, so that "the shadow of the object fell upon the ego". The hostility once felt towards the lost person is now aimed at the part of the self that has become that person. The result is anger turned inward: the self-hatred of depression is really disguised rage at someone else, and suicide is its most extreme form. Freud also offered an account of mania, as a state in which the ego has, for the moment, triumphed over the loss.

Later analysts reshaped the theory. Melanie Klein (1882 to 1960) described in 1935 a depressive position in infancy, the stage at which the baby realises that the mother it loves and the mother it hates are the same person and feels guilt and a wish to make amends; a child who does not work through it carries a vulnerability to depression into adult losses. Edward Bibring (1894 to 1959), writing in 1953, broke with the idea of aggression altogether. For Bibring depression was the emotional expression of the ego's helplessness: the painful awareness that one cannot live up to strongly held aspirations, to be worthy and loved, to be strong and secure, or to be good. The core of depression, on this view, is a collapse of self-esteem, not hidden anger. Bibring's emphasis on helplessness and self-esteem was a bridge to the cognitive theories that came later. Finally, John Bowlby (1907 to 1990), in Loss: Sadness and Depression (1980), the third volume of his attachment trilogy, argued that early separation from or loss of a caregiver, or insecure attachment, leaves a child with a working model of the self as unlovable and of others as unavailable, and that this model makes later losses devastating. René Spitz's anaclitic depression in separated infants (Section 5.3) was part of the evidence he drew on.

How has the psychodynamic account fared? Its central observation, that depression very often follows a loss, has been confirmed again and again by the life-events research you will meet later in this section. Its central mechanism has not. If depression were anger turned inward, depressed people should be unusually unable to express anger at others; in fact Myrna Weissman and Eugene Paykel's study of depressed women (1974) found them more, not less, openly hostile towards their husbands and children. Aaron Beck began his career trying to prove the related idea that depressed patients have a hidden need to suffer, and his own dream studies and laboratory experiments persuaded him that it was wrong (Book 2, Chapter 5). Early loss of a parent does raise the risk of later depression, but only modestly, and research by Tirril Harris, George Brown and Antonia Bifulco (1986) suggested that what matters is less the death itself than the poor care that often follows it. The psychodynamic tradition's lasting contributions are its attention to loss, to self-esteem and to early relationships; its therapies are taught in Book 2, Chapter 3.

Exam trap

Match the phrase to the name. Loss of a loved object, introjection and anger turned inward: Freud's Mourning and Melancholia (1917), building on Karl Abraham (1911). The ego's helplessness to meet its aspirations: Edward Bibring (1953). The depressive position: Melanie Klein (1935). Attachment and loss: John Bowlby. Anaclitic depression: René Spitz (1946). Do not confuse Bibring's ego helplessness with Seligman's learned helplessness, which came from experiments on dogs. And an option such as "repressed unconscious sexual conflicts" is a caricature: Freud's theory of depression is about loss and ambivalence, and in any case it is never the answer to a question about Beck.

Too little reward: the behavioural theories

Behaviourists distrusted talk of introjected objects, so they asked a plainer question: what happens to a depressed person's behaviour, and what keeps it that way? In 1973 Charles B. Ferster (1922 to 1981), who had worked with B. F. Skinner on schedules of reinforcement, described depression as a sharp fall in the frequency of positively reinforced behaviour, together with a rise in avoidance and escape. Seen this way, a depressed man who no longer phones his friends, cooks or plays cricket is like a rat whose lever-pressing has been put on extinction: the behaviours that used to pay off have stopped paying, and so they fade.

The best-known behavioural theory came a year later from Peter M. Lewinsohn of the University of Oregon. In 1974 he proposed that depression results from a low rate of response-contingent positive reinforcement: rewards that follow from one's own actions, such as a friend's laugh at your joke or the pleasure of finishing a chapter. The rate can be low for three reasons. Some people have few potentially rewarding events in their lives, because their interests are narrow. Some live in environments that supply few rewards, because of poverty, isolation, unemployment or the death of a partner. And some lack the skills, social skills above all, to obtain the rewards that are available. Whatever the reason, the loss of reward produces low mood and fatigue; low mood makes the person do less; doing less brings still fewer rewards; and the circle tightens. Lewinsohn added a twist. In the short run, depressive behaviour is often rewarded by sympathy and concern, which can keep it going; in the long run, as the interpersonal theories below show, the same behaviour drives other people away.

The theory made predictions that could be checked with diaries and observers. Lewinsohn and his colleagues asked people to record, day by day, their mood and the pleasant activities they had done, and found that the two rose and fell together. When they watched depressed people in group discussions, the depressed members spoke less, responded to others less, and so received less reward from the group. One of their most interesting findings came in 1980, when Lewinsohn, Walter Mischel and their colleagues had observers rate the social competence of depressed patients and of controls in group interactions. The depressed patients rated themselves about as the observers did. It was the non-depressed people who rated themselves more favourably than the observers did, and as the patients recovered their self-ratings became rosier too. Normal mood, it seemed, came with a slight "illusory glow", an idea you will meet again as depressive realism.

The weakness of the theory is the direction of cause. Anhedonia and fatigue are symptoms of depression, so a depressed person would be expected to do fewer pleasant things whether or not low reward started the episode, and diary studies alone cannot tell the two apart. The theory's great strength is practical. If too little reward maintains depression, then scheduling rewarding activity should relieve it, and it does: activity scheduling became part of Beck's cognitive therapy, and behavioural activation, a treatment built on Ferster's and Lewinsohn's ideas, is now one of the best-supported treatments for depression (Book 2, Section 5.10).

Exam trap

"A deficiency of reinforcement in the environment" or "a low rate of response-contingent positive reinforcement" is Peter Lewinsohn's behavioural theory (1974), developed from Charles Ferster (1973). It is the wrong answer when the stem asks what maintains depression in Beck's model. Lewinsohn is not the author of learned helplessness (Seligman) or of the response styles theory (Nolen-Hoeksema).

Learned helplessness

Some of the most influential ideas in psychology began as an experiment that went wrong. In the mid-1960s, in Richard Solomon's laboratory at the University of Pennsylvania, two graduate students, J. Bruce Overmier and Martin E. P. Seligman (born 1942), were studying how fear learned in one situation affects avoidance learning in another. Dogs were first given tones paired with brief electric shocks while they were restrained in a hammock-like harness. Then they were placed in a shuttle box, a two-compartment box with a low barrier in the middle, where a shock came through the floor and the dog could escape it simply by jumping the barrier. Normal dogs learn this within a few trials. These dogs did not. They ran about for a few seconds, then lay down and whimpered and took the shock until it ended. Overmier and Seligman reported in 1967 that earlier inescapable shock interfered with later escape learning. But why?

Seligman and his fellow student Steven F. Maier suspected that the dogs had learned something about the relationship between their actions and the shock: that nothing they did made any difference. To test this they needed to separate the shock itself from the lack of control over it, and they invented a design so neat that it is still the standard method in research on control: the triadic design. Its key element is the yoked control, an animal that receives exactly the same events as a partner but has no control over them. Figure 5.10 shows the design, and the Key study box tells the story.

Key study: failure to escape traumatic shock (Seligman and Maier, 1967)

Twenty-four dogs were divided into three groups of eight. Dogs in the escape group were placed in a harness and given a series of shocks, which they could turn off by pressing a panel with their heads. Each dog in the yoked group was wired to a partner in the escape group: it received exactly the same shocks, at the same moments and for the same length of time, but its own panel did nothing, and the shock stopped only when its partner pressed. A third, no-shock group received no shock at all. Twenty-four hours later every dog was tested in the shuttle box.

The escape dogs and the no-shock dogs quickly learned to jump the barrier. The yoked dogs did not: six of the eight failed to escape on nine or more of the ten trials, lying down and whining while the shock continued. Because the escape and yoked dogs had received physically identical shocks, the difference could not be due to the shock. It was due to the lack of control. Seligman and Maier concluded that the yoked dogs had learned that responding and relief were independent, and that this expectation undermined their motivation to try. Criticisms followed: Jay Weiss argued that uncontrollable shock simply depleted the brain's noradrenaline, and others proposed that the dogs had merely learned to keep still. The experiments also raised serious ethical concerns that would make them hard to repeat today.

From these experiments came the concept of learned helplessness: the passivity, slowed learning and emotional disturbance that follow exposure to uncontrollable events, caused by the expectation that one's responses will not affect what happens. Seligman argued that it involves three deficits. There is a motivational deficit (the animal stops trying), a cognitive deficit (when a helpless dog did by chance jump the barrier, it often failed to learn from its success and lay down again on the next trial) and an emotional deficit (the animals became passive, lost weight and developed stress ulcers). The deficit could be prevented and cured. Dogs that had first learned to escape shock in the shuttle box were protected against later inescapable shock, a kind of immunisation; and helpless dogs recovered when the experimenters dragged them repeatedly across the barrier until they discovered for themselves that moving ended the shock.

In Helplessness: On Depression, Development, and Death (1975), Seligman proposed that learned helplessness is a laboratory model of human depression, especially of depression that follows uncontrollable losses. He listed the parallels: passivity, a negative expectation that one's actions will fail, loss of appetite and weight, social and sexual withdrawal, depletion of noradrenaline, the tendency to wear off with time and, strikingly, the reversal of helplessness in animals by antidepressant drugs and electroconvulsive shock. Human experiments seemed to agree. In 1974 Donald Hiroto exposed college students to a loud noise; one group could stop it by pressing a button, a yoked group could not. Later, in a "finger shuttle box" where moving a hand from one side to the other ended the noise, the yoked students were far slower to escape. Hiroto and Seligman (1975) went on to show that helplessness learned on one task, such as unsolvable discrimination problems, spilled over into failure on a different task, such as solving anagrams.

As a theory of human depression, however, learned helplessness soon ran into trouble. Many human participants did not become passive after uncontrollable failure; some became angry and tried harder. The theory could not say why some people exposed to uncontrollable events become depressed and others do not, or why some helplessness is brief and narrow and some is lasting and general. And it contained a paradox, pointed out by Lyn Abramson and Harold Sackeim in 1977: if depressed people believe that they have no control over what happens, why do they blame themselves so bitterly for it? The answer to these problems, as the next subsection shows, turned out to lie in how people explain what happens to them.

Half a century later the story took another turn. In a 2016 review called "Learned helplessness at fifty", Maier and Seligman concluded, from neuroscience research largely done in Maier's laboratory, that the original theory had the causal story backwards. Passivity in the face of prolonged aversive events is not learned at all: it is the brain's default response, driven by serotonin neurons in a brainstem region called the dorsal raphe nucleus. What the escape animals learn is control. A region of the medial prefrontal cortex detects that their actions work and suppresses the default response. Helplessness, on this account, is what happens when control is never detected, which helps to explain why experiences of mastery protect against later stress.

Exam trap

Learned helplessness: Overmier and Seligman (1967) and Seligman and Maier (1967), with dogs; Seligman's book Helplessness (1975). The crucial group is the yoked group, which received the same shocks as the escape group without control, so the deficit comes from uncontrollability, not from shock as such. Prior controllable experience gives immunisation. Do not attribute learned helplessness to Lewinsohn, Bandura, Bibring or Beck, and do not confuse it with Seligman's later learned optimism (1990), the teachable explanatory style of positive psychology (Book 2, Chapter 6).

Explaining bad events: attributions and hopelessness

In 1978 Lyn Y. Abramson, Seligman and the British psychologist John D. Teasdale published "Learned helplessness in humans: critique and reformulation". Their attributional reformulation kept the idea that uncontrollable bad events can lead to depression but added a step in the middle. When something bad happens, people ask why, and the answer they give, their attribution, decides whether helplessness follows and what form it takes. Attributions vary along three dimensions, and Table 5.19 shows them for a single event in the life of Rekha, a 21-year-old MSc student in Hyderabad who has just failed her mid-semester statistics examination.

Table 5.19 The three attributional dimensions of Abramson, Seligman and Teasdale (1978), applied to Rekha's failed examination.
DimensionDepressive explanationNon-depressive explanationWhat the depressive pole predicts
Internal or external"I failed because I am stupid""The paper was unusually hard"Loss of self-esteem
Stable or unstable"I will always be stupid""I had fever that week"Helplessness that lasts (chronicity)
Global or specific"I mess up everything I try""Statistics is my one weak subject"Helplessness that spreads to other areas (generality)

The reformulation solved the old theory's problems at a stroke. Internality explains the self-blame paradox: Rekha feels helpless and guilty because she blames a defect in herself. The authors called this personal helplessness (I cannot solve this problem, although others could), as opposed to universal helplessness (nobody could solve it), and only personal helplessness lowers self-esteem. Stability explains why some helplessness lasts, and globality explains why some spreads from one area of life to all of them. A person who habitually explains bad events in internal, stable and global terms has a depressive attributional style (or explanatory style), and this style is the diathesis: it does nothing until a bad event arrives, and then it turns the event into hopelessness. The mirror image, explaining good events in external, unstable and specific terms ("I only passed because the questions were easy"), belongs to the same style. The Attributional Style Questionnaire (Christopher Peterson and colleagues, 1982) measures it; tests are taught in the Assessment book.

The evidence for a link between attributional style and depression is strong; whether the style comes first is harder to show. A 1986 meta-analysis of more than a hundred studies by Paul Sweeney, Karen Anderson and Scott Bailey confirmed that depressed people make more internal, stable and global attributions for bad events. In a neat prospective test, Gerald Metalsky and colleagues (1982) measured college students' attributional styles before a midterm examination; among students who did badly, those with a more depressive attributional style showed a greater rise in depressed mood afterwards (a 1987 follow-up by Metalsky, Halberstadt and Abramson showed that the style predicted the mood that lasted for days afterwards, not just the immediate reaction). Children tell a more complicated story. In a five-year longitudinal study, Susan Nolen-Hoeksema, Joan Girgus and Seligman (1992) found that in young children it was stressful events, not attributional style, that predicted later depressive symptoms; as stressed children grew older they developed more negative styles, and from then on the style did predict depression after further bad events. Explanatory style seems to be partly a product of early adversity and partly a cause of what follows.

In 1989 Abramson, Gerald I. Metalsky and Lauren B. Alloy revised the theory once more. Their hopelessness theory moved the emphasis from attributions to the expectation they produce. Hopelessness, in their definition, has two parts: the expectation that highly desired outcomes will not occur, or that dreaded ones will, and the expectation that nothing one can do will change this. They argued that hopelessness is a sufficient cause of a particular subtype, hopelessness depression, marked by sad mood, lack of energy, retarded initiation of action, apathy and suicidal thinking. Three cognitive diatheses make hopelessness likely after a negative event: the tendency to see its causes as stable and global, to infer that it will have dire consequences, and to infer that it reveals something deficient about oneself. The theory deliberately links depression to suicide, and you will see in Section 5.9 that hopelessness predicts suicide better than depression itself does.

The strongest test of the cognitive vulnerability idea was the Temple-Wisconsin Cognitive Vulnerability to Depression Project, run by Alloy at Temple University in Philadelphia and Abramson at the University of Wisconsin. First-year university students who were not depressed were screened with questionnaires measuring dysfunctional attitudes and hopelessness-producing inferences, and those scoring very high (high risk) or very low (low risk) were followed with repeated interviews for several years. Among students who had never been depressed before, 16 percent of the high-risk group but only 2.7 percent of the low-risk group developed a first episode of major depression during follow-up, and 46 percent against 14 percent developed minor depressive symptoms. Here, at last, was evidence that a negative cognitive style comes before depression and predicts it. A later study by Gerald Haeffel and Jennifer Hames (2013) even suggested that cognitive vulnerability can be "caught": first-year students assigned to room with a highly vulnerable roommate became more vulnerable themselves.

Alloy and Abramson are also known for an earlier and more surprising finding. In 1979, in a paper subtitled "Sadder but wiser?", they gave depressed and non-depressed students a button and a green light. On each trial the student chose whether to press, and the light came on with probabilities that the experimenters had arranged, sometimes quite unrelated to pressing. Afterwards each student estimated how much control the button had given them. The depressed students' estimates were accurate. The non-depressed students overestimated their control, especially when the light came on often or when it won them money, showing an illusion of control. This finding, called depressive realism, turned the question round: perhaps depression is not so much a distortion of reality as the loss of a mildly optimistic bias that protects most people. Later work has tempered the claim; a 2012 meta-analysis by Michael Moore and David Fresco found the overall effect small and dependent on the conditions of the task. But it remains a useful warning against assuming that whatever a depressed person believes must be false.

Exam trap

Keep this family of theories straight. Learned helplessness: Seligman and Maier (1967), Seligman (1975). Attributional reformulation: Abramson, Seligman and Teasdale (1978): bad events attributed to internal, stable, global causes ("I am stupid"). The opposite poles are external, unstable and specific (not "local" or "temporary" in the theory's own terms). Hopelessness theory: Abramson, Metalsky and Alloy (1989). Depressive realism ("sadder but wiser"): Alloy and Abramson (1979), not Beck. Personal helplessness lowers self-esteem; universal helplessness does not.

Beck's cognitive theory as a theory of cause

The most examined psychological theory of depression is that of Aaron T. Beck (1921 to 2021), set out in his book Depression: Clinical, Experimental, and Theoretical Aspects (1967). Book 2, Section 5.3 teaches the model in detail as a basis for therapy, and the opening story of that chapter tells how Beck's patients' "second stream" of self-critical thoughts, and his research on their dreams, turned him from psychoanalysis. Here is the model in brief. At its centre is the negative cognitive triad: negative views of the self ("I am worthless"), of the world or ongoing experience ("nothing ever works out for me") and of the future ("it will always be like this"). The triad is fed by automatic thoughts, the quick, plausible, unbidden thoughts that pop up in a situation ("I'll never pass"); these grow out of deeper schemas or core beliefs formed early in life ("I am incompetent", "I am unlovable"), linked to them by conditional rules called dysfunctional assumptions ("If I am not the best, I am a failure"); and they are kept believable by systematic errors of reasoning, the cognitive distortions (arbitrary inference, selective abstraction, overgeneralisation, magnification and minimisation, personalisation and dichotomous thinking). The other symptoms follow: if you expect every effort to fail you stop trying, and if the future is hopeless, death can look like the only way out.

What concerns this book is the causal claim. Beck's theory is a diathesis-stress theory (Book 2, Section 1.7). Negative schemas laid down by early experiences of loss, rejection or criticism are the diathesis. They lie dormant, sometimes for years, until a stress that matches them, a rejection for the person whose schema is "I am unlovable", a failure for the person whose schema is "I am incompetent", switches them on. Beck (1983) described two personality styles that carry different vulnerabilities: the sociotropic person, whose worth rests on being liked and close to others, and the autonomous person, whose worth rests on achievement and independence. Once active, the schema biases attention, interpretation and memory, the triad and the distortions take over, and the depression maintains itself. The theory therefore makes three testable claims: depressed people think more negatively than others; the negative thinking is present before the depression; and it remains present, though hidden, after recovery.

The first claim is beyond doubt. On every measure, depressed people's thinking about themselves, their world and their future is more negative than that of non-depressed people. They also show biases in the way they process information: they recall more negative than positive events when depressed; in experiments by Ian H. Gotlib and his colleagues they keep looking longer at sad faces once their attention has landed there; and, as J. Mark G. Williams showed in work beginning in the 1980s, they find it hard to retrieve specific memories, producing an overgeneral autobiographical memory ("I always failed at school") instead of a particular day.

The second and third claims ran into a serious difficulty. In 1981 Peter Lewinsohn and his colleagues measured depression-related thinking in a large community sample of people who were not depressed and followed them up. Negative thinking did not predict who became depressed; it appeared with the episode and disappeared when the episode ended. Other studies found that negative thinking improves just as much when depression is treated with drugs as when it is treated with cognitive therapy. Negative cognition, it seemed, might be a symptom of depression rather than its cause.

The reply from cognitive researchers was that Beck had always said the schemas were latent, and a latent structure cannot be detected by simply asking people about their attitudes when they feel fine. It has to be activated. In 1988 Jeanne Miranda and Jacqueline B. Persons showed that people with a history of depression endorsed dysfunctional attitudes no more than others when their mood was normal, but endorsed far more of them after a brief experimental induction of sad mood. That same year Teasdale proposed the differential activation hypothesis: what makes people vulnerable to relapse is how readily an ordinary dip in mood reactivates the old negative patterns of thinking. The decisive study came in 2006, when Zindel V. Segal and his colleagues measured cognitive reactivity in patients who had recovered from depression. Those whose dysfunctional attitudes rose most after a sad mood induction were far more likely to relapse over the following eighteen months, and patients who had recovered with cognitive therapy showed less reactivity than those who had recovered with medication. Together with the Temple-Wisconsin results, this work supports the view that negative cognitive structures exist before depression, can be dormant, and predict both onset and relapse. These findings also led directly to mindfulness-based cognitive therapy (Book 2, Section 5.9).

Two qualifications remain. First, negative thinking is not specific to depression: anxious people also think negatively. Beck's own answer was the content-specificity hypothesis (1976): depressive thoughts concern loss and failure, often in the past, while anxious thoughts concern danger and threat in the future, and the evidence partly supports him. Second, the matching of sociotropy with interpersonal stress and of autonomy with achievement stress has had mixed support, somewhat better for sociotropy. The fairest summary is that negative cognition is both a vulnerability factor that can lie dormant until activated and a powerful maintaining factor once an episode begins. The success of cognitive therapy, especially in preventing relapse (Book 2, Section 5.11), is consistent with the theory, though it does not prove it, since successful drug treatment also changes how people think.

Exam trap

Beck's cognitive triad is negative views of the self, the world (ongoing experience) and the future. It is not id, ego and superego (Freud's structural model), not past, present and future (the past is not one of the three), not self, mother and father, not self, others and environment, not thoughts, feelings and behaviours (the three linked elements of the general CBT model), not mood, behaviour and somatic state, and not body, mind and spirit. If a stem describes "the pattern of negative thoughts about self, world and future", the name is the cognitive triad: automatic thoughts, cognitive distortions and dysfunctional assumptions are other parts of the model. Asked what maintains depression in Beck's model, choose the triad (with the schemas and distortions behind it); repressed conflicts, deficient reinforcement (Lewinsohn) and insecure attachment alone (Bowlby) belong to other theories.

Rumination and the gender gap

Everybody feels low at times. Why does a bad mood lift in a day for some people and deepen into weeks of depression for others? Susan Nolen-Hoeksema (1959 to 2013), then at Stanford University, proposed in 1991 that the answer lies less in what happens to people than in what they do with their sadness once it arrives. Her response styles theory contrasts two ways of responding to low mood. The first, rumination, is repetitive, passive focusing on one's symptoms of distress and on their possible causes and consequences, without doing anything to solve the problem: "Why do I feel so bad? What is wrong with me? I will never be able to study if I keep feeling like this." The second, distraction, is turning attention away from the mood to pleasant or neutral activities: a walk, a film, a game of badminton with friends.

Return to Rekha. After the failed examination she lies awake every night replaying the paper, asking why she always messes things up, and imagining her parents' faces when the results arrive. She skips the library because sitting at a desk makes her think about it more, and she turns down her roommate's invitations because she "is not in the mood". Nolen-Hoeksema would predict that Rekha's low mood will last longer and go deeper than that of a classmate who failed the same paper and spent the evening at the cinema. Rumination does this, the theory says, in four ways: it brings more negative memories and thoughts to mind through mood-congruent recall, it impairs problem solving, it interferes with the ordinary activities that might bring relief, and over time it wears out the patience of the people who might support her.

The evidence came from both experiments and natural events. In laboratory studies by Nolen-Hoeksema and her colleagues, mildly depressed students who were asked to spend a few minutes focusing on their feelings and their causes became more depressed, recalled more unhappy memories, were more pessimistic about the future and were worse at solving interpersonal problems than similar students asked to think about neutral scenes; non-depressed students were hardly affected either way. Then nature provided a test. Nolen-Hoeksema had measured ruminative style and depression in a group of Stanford students shortly before the Loma Prieta earthquake struck the San Francisco Bay area on 17 October 1989. Followed up about ten days and seven weeks later, the students who had been ruminators before the earthquake had more depression and more post-traumatic stress symptoms after it, even allowing for how depressed they had been beforehand (Nolen-Hoeksema and Morrow, 1991). Later work divided rumination into brooding, a moody comparison of one's present state with an unachieved standard ("Why can't I handle things better?"), which predicts depression, and reflection, a purposeful turning inward to understand and solve a problem, which is much less harmful (Treynor, Gonzalez and Nolen-Hoeksema, 2003). The Ruminative Responses Scale measures these styles.

Nolen-Hoeksema's interest in rumination began with a puzzle you met in Section 5.2: from early adolescence onwards women are about twice as likely as men to become depressed. Her studies found that women, on average, ruminate more than men in response to low mood, while men are more likely to distract themselves (or, less healthily, to drink). Since rumination prolongs depression, a sex difference in rumination could account for part of the sex difference in depression. It is only part. Women also face more of the adversities that cause depression: more childhood sexual abuse, more poverty, more discrimination, more violence from partners and, in many families, heavier caring burdens with less power over their own lives. David Barlow's group has argued that these experiences build a sense that life is uncontrollable, which links back to learned helplessness. Hormonal changes at puberty may add a biological contribution (Section 5.5). The gender gap, like depression itself, has several causes acting together.

Indian context

Indian research has given the "social disadvantage" explanation of women's depression unusually clear support. In a community survey of women in Goa, Vikram Patel and his colleagues (2006) found that common mental disorders were strongly associated with markers of gender disadvantage, such as sexual and physical violence by the husband, little say in household decisions and little support from the family. For a clinician this means that asking about violence, debt, autonomy and support at home is part of assessing a depressed woman, not an optional extra.

Exam trap

The response styles theory of rumination is Susan Nolen-Hoeksema's (1991), not Seligman's or Beck's; it explains part of the female excess in depression, which is why "men are more vulnerable to depression than women" is a false statement. Do not confuse three "ruminations": depressive rumination (repetitive "why me?" thinking about one's distress, past and present), worry (repetitive "what if?" thinking about future threats, the core of generalised anxiety disorder, Chapter 6), and rumination disorder, a feeding disorder in which food is repeatedly regurgitated and re-chewed (Chapter 8).

Depression between people: the interpersonal theories

The theories so far look inside the depressed person. In 1976 the American psychologist James C. Coyne looked at what happens between the depressed person and everyone else. His interactional account goes like this. A person who is becoming depressed feels insecure and asks others for reassurance ("Do you still care about me?"). The others give it, but the depressed person doubts that they mean it ("you're only saying that because you feel sorry for me") and asks again. As the requests continue, the others grow frustrated and begin to feel low themselves. They go on saying the right things while their tone and their behaviour say something else, and the depressed person, quite accurately, senses the rejection behind the words. That confirms the doubts, the requests for reassurance increase, and the spiral continues until the others withdraw altogether.

Coyne tested part of this in a simple experiment. Students had a short telephone conversation with a woman they did not know who was, unknown to them, either a depressed psychiatric outpatient, a non-depressed outpatient or a woman from the community. After talking with a depressed woman, the students reported feeling more depressed, anxious and hostile themselves, and they were less willing to have any further contact with her. Later studies found the effect weaker among strangers than Coyne's first results suggested but robust in close relationships, where it matters most. Thomas E. Joiner and his colleagues sharpened the idea into the concept of excessive reassurance seeking. In a 1992 study of college roommates, the roommates' views were measured five weeks after the students' own symptoms: among the men, mildly depressed students were rejected by their roommates if they sought a great deal of reassurance and also had low self-esteem, but not if they did not seek reassurance or had high self-esteem.

Constance Hammen added the idea of stress generation (1991). Following women with unipolar depression, women with bipolar disorder, women with chronic medical illness and healthy women for a year, she found that the depressed women experienced more stressful life events than the healthy women, and especially more interpersonal events to which they had themselves contributed, such as quarrels and break-ups. Depression, in other words, is not only a result of stress but also a cause of it, a point that the life-events research below also had to face. Marriage shows the same two-way traffic. Depression and marital distress go together, and longitudinal studies suggest that the direction of cause often differs between the sexes: for many women marital problems lead to depression, while for many men depression leads to withdrawal and marital problems.

These findings explain why one of the best-supported treatments for depression, interpersonal psychotherapy, developed by Gerald Klerman and Myrna Weissman, focuses on four interpersonal problem areas: grief, role disputes, role transitions and interpersonal deficits (Book 2, Section 3.7).

Social origins: life events, vulnerability and the Camberwell study

Is depression "often triggered by a stressful event", as one of the statements in a past paper puts it? It is, and the most famous evidence for it came from a sociologist rather than a psychiatrist. George W. Brown and his colleague Tirril Harris, working at Bedford College in the University of London, spent the late 1960s and early 1970s interviewing women in Camberwell, a largely working-class area of south London. Their book, Social Origins of Depression: A Study of Psychiatric Disorder in Women (1978), is one of the classics of psychiatric epidemiology.

Brown and Harris began by solving a measurement problem. Ask a depressed person whether anything bad happened before she fell ill and her answer is coloured by her depression: she may remember more, or judge ordinary events as worse, than she would have done when well. Checklists of life events, such as the Social Readjustment Rating Scale of Holmes and Rahe (1967), also ignore context: "loss of job" means something different to a single mother living from one wage to the next and to a woman whose husband earns a good salary. Brown and Harris developed a long semi-structured interview, the Life Events and Difficulties Schedule (LEDS), in which the interviewer collects the full circumstances of each event and a panel of raters, who do not know whether the woman became depressed, judges its contextual threat: how threatening such an event would be for an average person in exactly those circumstances.

Key study: social origins of depression (Brown and Harris, 1978)

Brown and Harris interviewed a random community sample of 458 women in Camberwell, as well as women attending psychiatric services, and dated the onset of any depression in the previous year against the events and difficulties in their lives. Most onsets were preceded by what they called provoking agents: a severe event (one with marked long-term threat, usually involving loss or disappointment, such as a death, a separation, a discovery of infidelity or the loss of a home) or a major difficulty (an ongoing problem, such as bad housing, a husband's drinking or a child's illness, lasting at least two years). But most women who had a provoking agent did not become depressed. Who did depended on four vulnerability factors: having no intimate, confiding relationship with a husband or partner; having three or more children under the age of 14 living at home; having lost one's mother before the age of 11; and having no employment outside the home. Among women with a provoking agent, about one in ten of those with a confiding relationship with a husband or boyfriend became depressed, against about one in four of those whose confidant was someone else, and more still of those with no confidant at all. Working-class women had much higher rates. They did suffer somewhat more severe events and major difficulties, but Brown and his colleagues showed that this explained only a small part of the class difference: the main reason was that working-class women, who more often carried vulnerability factors, were much more likely to break down once a provoking agent occurred.

Criticisms: the study was retrospective, covered women only and one area of one city, and Christopher Tennant and Paul Bebbington (1978) argued that the published figures did not justify separating "vulnerability factors" from "provoking agents" statistically. Replications have generally confirmed the importance of severe events and of lacking a confidant, less consistently the other three factors.

Brown and Harris added a third group of influences, symptom formation factors, which did not decide whether a woman became depressed but shaped how severe and what form the depression took; past losses and previous episodes of depression were among them. Figure 5.11 puts the model together.

Push a case through the model. Lakshmi is 38 and lives in a small town near Madurai. She was married at 19 and has three children, aged 4, 9 and 12. Her mother died when she was eight, and she was brought up by an aunt. She has never worked outside the home. Her husband works on a construction site in Dubai and phones once a week; her mother-in-law, who lives with them, criticises her cooking and her mothering, and there is nobody she tells how she really feels. In March her husband loses his job, and the family cannot pay the instalments on the loan they took to build the upper floor of their house. Within two months Lakshmi is waking at three every morning, has lost interest in her children's schoolwork and her temple visits, and says that she has brought bad luck on the family.

In Brown and Harris's terms, the job loss and the threat to the house are a severe event, and the unpayable loan is a major difficulty. Lakshmi carries all four vulnerability factors: no confidant, three children under 14, loss of her mother before 11 and no employment outside the home. The model predicts a high risk, and it also predicts that her neighbour, who has the same kind of event but a husband she can talk to and a job at the cooperative bank, will probably weather it. Later work by Brown, Harris and Catherine Hepworth (1995) added another lesson from Lakshmi's case: the events most likely to provoke depression are those that carry humiliation (being shamed or devalued, as by her mother-in-law's criticism) and entrapment (being stuck in a punishing situation with no way out, as with the debt). Loss alone is less potent than loss combined with humiliation or entrapment.

Research since 1978 has confirmed and refined the picture. Severe events precede most first episodes of depression; they are somewhat less important for later episodes, which is what the kindling hypothesis of Section 5.5 predicts. But most people who experience a severe event do not develop a mood disorder: estimates are that only somewhere between a fifth and a half do, which is why vulnerability matters. And the relationship runs both ways. Using twin data, Kenneth S. Kendler and his colleagues (1999) estimated that about a third of the association between stressful life events and depression is not stress causing depression at all, but vulnerable people (partly for genetic reasons) selecting themselves into difficult relationships and risky situations where bad events are common, a form of gene-environment correlation that links to Hammen's stress generation.

Clinical link

The Camberwell findings translate directly into assessment. When you take the history of a depressed client, date the onset and ask what happened in the months before it, in detail and in context: not "any stress?" but "what was happening at home, at work, with money, with your health, with the people close to you?" Then ask about the protective side: is there someone you can tell how you really feel? Lack of a confidant, isolation and entrapment are targets for treatment in their own right, and in Indian families they often mean involving the spouse or the wider family in the treatment plan.

Exam trap
