Brown and Harris's four vulnerability factors are lack of a confiding relationship, three or more children under 14 at home, loss of the mother before age 11, and no employment outside the home. They are vulnerability factors, not provoking agents: on their own they do little, but they raise the risk that a severe event or major difficulty (the provoking agents) leads to depression. The study was of women in Camberwell, London (1978). "Depression is often triggered by a stressful event" is an accurate statement.

Psychological and social triggers of bipolar episodes

Bipolar disorder is the most heritable of the mood disorders (Section 5.5), and for a long time its episodes were thought to arrive with no psychological cause at all. That turned out to be wrong. Stressful events matter in bipolar disorder too, but the triggers for mania are partly different from the triggers for depression. Consider Venkat, a 29-year-old software engineer in Bengaluru who was diagnosed with bipolar I disorder at 24 and has been well for three years on lithium. In January he is promoted to team leader and given charge of a product launch. For six weeks he works with colleagues in California, staying up most nights to match their time zone and sleeping four hours a day; then he flies to San Francisco for the launch. Within ten days of his return he is sleeping two hours a night, has booked a car he cannot afford, and has written a long email to the company's chief executive explaining how he would reorganise the whole firm. He has also, he admits, stopped taking his lithium a month earlier because "it was slowing me down just when I needed to be sharp".

Several of the triggers in Venkat's story have been studied directly. Negative life events, as in unipolar depression, tend to precede bipolar depressions. Manic symptoms, by contrast, are triggered by a particular kind of positive event. In a prospective study published in 2000, Sheri L. Johnson and her colleagues found that in people with bipolar I disorder, goal attainment events (a promotion, an acceptance letter, falling in love, a big win) were followed by increases in manic symptoms over the next two months, while depressive symptoms did not change after them. The explanation they offered draws on the behavioural activation system, a brain system that responds to cues of reward and drives approach and goal pursuit, described in this context by Richard Depue and his colleagues. People prone to bipolar disorder appear to have an oversensitive behavioural activation system: when a goal comes within reach it switches into overdrive, producing excessive confidence, energy and goal-directed activity, the beginnings of mania. Cognitive style in bipolar disorder reflects the same thing. People with the disorder share the negative cognitive styles of depression but add ambitious striving for goals, perfectionism and harsh self-criticism.

Sleep loss is the second trigger. In 1987 Thomas A. Wehr and his colleagues at the US National Institute of Mental Health proposed that sleep reduction is a "final common pathway" to mania: whatever the original cause (a new baby, jet lag, an examination, a quarrel that keeps you awake), losing sleep can itself set off mania in vulnerable people, and the resulting mania reduces sleep still further, a vicious circle. The third trigger is disruption of daily routines. The social zeitgeber theory of Cindy Ehlers, Ellen Frank and David Kupfer (1988), taught in Section 5.5, argues that the regular social cues of daily life (meal times, work hours, bedtimes) keep the body's clocks in time, and that events which upset those routines can destabilise mood. Venkat's months of night work and his flights across time zones disrupted all three.

Finally, the family atmosphere matters. Expressed emotion, the level of criticism, hostility and emotional over-involvement shown by relatives towards a patient, was first studied in schizophrenia (Chapter 4). In 1988 David J. Miklowitz and his colleagues showed that patients with bipolar disorder who returned after hospital to families high in expressed emotion, or whose relatives showed a negative affective style in conversation with them, were much more likely to relapse within nine months. That finding is the basis of family-focused therapy (Section 5.7). And Venkat's decision to stop lithium is a reminder of perhaps the most common trigger of all: many people miss the energy and confidence of their highs and stop their medication, and stopping, especially stopping suddenly, sharply raises the risk of relapse.

Exam trap

Positive events can trigger mania: goal attainment events (Johnson and colleagues, 2000) and an oversensitive behavioural activation system are the best-studied psychological triggers. Sleep loss (Wehr, 1987) is both a symptom and a trigger of mania. High expressed emotion predicts relapse in bipolar disorder (Miklowitz, 1988) as well as in schizophrenia, so it is not a schizophrenia-only concept.

Putting the causes together

No single theory in this section, and no single biological finding in Section 5.5, explains depression on its own, and the theories are not really rivals. They describe different links in a chain that the diathesis-stress model (Book 2, Section 1.7) holds together. A person inherits genes that make the stress-response system more reactive. Early adversity, such as the loss of a mother or harsh and critical parenting, lays down negative schemas, a pessimistic attributional style and an insecure model of relationships. Years later a severe event arrives, perhaps one the person's own behaviour helped to bring about, and it lands hardest on someone who carries vulnerability factors and has nobody to confide in. The event activates the dormant schemas; hopelessness follows; rumination deepens and prolongs the low mood; withdrawal cuts off reward; repeated requests for reassurance wear out the people who might help; and the biological changes of a depressive episode take hold. With each episode, according to the kindling hypothesis, less stress is needed to start the next. Table 5.20 summarises the theories, and the treatments that follow from them are the subject of Section 5.7 and of Book 2.

Table 5.20 Psychological and social theories of the mood disorders.

5.7 Treating Mood Disorders: Drugs, ECT and Other Physical TreatmentsLower yield · 2 Qs

The chapter opened with John Cade's discovery, in 1949, that a cheap salt could calm a man who had been manic for years. Almost everything in this section grows out of that moment and the decade that followed it. Within ten years psychiatry had lithium for mania, the first antidepressants for depression (Section 5.5 tells how iproniazid and imipramine were discovered by accident) and a much safer form of the convulsive therapy that had been invented in the 1930s. Today the treatments for mood disorders are among the most effective in all of medicine, and also among the most misunderstood. This section explains what the drugs and physical treatments are, how they are thought to work, what they do to the people who take them, how well they work, and where the psychotherapies taught in Book 2 fit alongside them. Doses and prescribing belong to psychiatrists, but a clinical psychologist must know all of this: your clients will ask you about their tablets, and you may be the first person to notice a dangerous side effect.

The goals of treatment: response, remission, relapse and recurrence

Before comparing treatments, you need the words that are used to judge them. In 1991 a task force led by Ellen Frank, with David Kupfer and others, proposed consensus definitions that are now used in every trial and guideline, and Figure 5.12 draws them. A response is a clinically meaningful improvement, usually defined as a fall of at least 50 percent on a depression rating scale such as the Hamilton Rating Scale for Depression (the scales themselves are in the Assessment book). Remission is more than response: the person has only minimal symptoms and is, for practical purposes, well again. A relapse is the return of the full syndrome during remission, before recovery; it is the same episode coming back because treatment was stopped too soon. Recovery is remission that has lasted long enough (usually several months) for the episode to be considered over. A recurrence is a new episode after recovery.

These definitions map onto three phases of treatment. The acute phase (usually 6 to 12 weeks) aims at remission, not merely response, because people who respond but keep residual symptoms relapse far more often than those who become fully well, as Eugene Paykel and his colleagues showed in 1995. The continuation phase aims to prevent relapse: guidelines recommend continuing an antidepressant that has worked for at least six months, and often a year, after remission, and then withdrawing it slowly. The maintenance phase aims to prevent recurrence. It is recommended for people at high risk, such as those who have had several episodes, and it may last for years; in bipolar disorder, maintenance treatment with a mood stabiliser is usually lifelong. Finally, depression that has not responded to at least two adequate trials (adequate in dose and duration) of different antidepressants is usually called treatment-resistant depression.

The antidepressants: classes and how they act

Pinel's textbook groups the antidepressants into five classes in the order in which they appeared: the monoamine oxidase inhibitors, the tricyclic antidepressants, the selective reuptake inhibitors, the atypical antidepressants and, most recently, drugs that block the glutamate NMDA receptor. All but the last increase the activity of one or more of the monoamine neurotransmitters, noradrenaline, serotonin and dopamine, at the synapse, which is what gave rise to the monoamine hypotheses of Section 5.5. They do this in two main ways. Some block reuptake, the pumping of the transmitter back into the neuron that released it, so that more remains in the synapse; others block its breakdown. Table 5.21 compares the classes, and the paragraphs that follow explain the entries that examiners ask about.

Table 5.21 The main classes of antidepressants.
ClassExamplesHow it actsMain side effectsCautions
Monoamine oxidase inhibitors (MAOIs)Phenelzine, tranylcypromine; moclobemide (reversible)Block monoamine oxidase, the enzyme that breaks down monoaminesDizziness on standing, insomnia, weight gainTyramine-rich foods cause a hypertensive crisis; many drug interactions
Tricyclics (TCAs)Imipramine, amitriptyline, clomipramine, nortriptylineBlock reuptake of noradrenaline and serotonin; also block other receptorsDry mouth, constipation, blurred vision, urinary retention, sedation, weight gain, low blood pressure on standingToxic to the heart; dangerous in overdose; confusion in the elderly
Selective serotonin reuptake inhibitors (SSRIs)Fluoxetine, sertraline, escitalopram, paroxetineBlock serotonin reuptake selectivelyNausea, headache, sleep change, early restlessness, sexual dysfunctionDiscontinuation symptoms; suicidality warning in the young; bleeding and low sodium in the elderly
Serotonin-noradrenaline reuptake inhibitors (SNRIs)Venlafaxine, duloxetineBlock reuptake of both, without most tricyclic receptor effectsAs SSRIs, plus sweating; raised blood pressureMarked discontinuation symptoms
Atypical antidepressantsBupropion, mirtazapine, trazodone, agomelatineVaried (see text)Varied: insomnia (bupropion); sedation and weight gain (mirtazapine)Bupropion lowers the seizure threshold; agomelatine needs liver tests
NMDA receptor antagonistsEsketamine (nasal spray); ketamineBlock glutamate NMDA receptors; act within hoursDissociation, sedation, raised blood pressureGiven under supervision for treatment-resistant depression; misuse potential

Monoamine oxidase inhibitors (MAOIs) were the first antidepressants. Monoamine oxidase is an enzyme inside neurons, and also in the gut and liver, that breaks down noradrenaline, serotonin and dopamine; block it and more of these transmitters is available for release. Iproniazid, the original MAOI, was withdrawn because it damaged the liver, and the MAOIs still used are phenelzine, tranylcypromine and isocarboxazid. They work well, and seem especially useful for depression with atypical features, but they carry a famous danger called the cheese reaction. Many foods contain tyramine, an amine that raises blood pressure: aged cheeses, cured and fermented meats and fish, yeast extracts, soy sauce, some wines and beers. Normally the monoamine oxidase in the gut wall and liver destroys tyramine before it reaches the circulation. In a person taking an MAOI it passes through intact, releases noradrenaline from nerve endings, and can cause a hypertensive crisis: a pounding headache, palpitations and a surge in blood pressure that can end in a stroke. Patients on these drugs must follow a low-tyramine diet and avoid many medicines, including some cold remedies, other antidepressants and the painkiller pethidine. Moclobemide is a reversible inhibitor of monoamine oxidase A (RIMA): because tyramine can displace it from the enzyme, the cheese reaction is much less likely and the diet need not be strict.

The tricyclic antidepressants (TCAs), named for the three rings in their chemical structure, began with imipramine and include amitriptyline, clomipramine and nortriptyline. They block the reuptake of noradrenaline and serotonin. Their side effects are easiest to remember if you know that they also block three other kinds of receptor. Blocking muscarinic acetylcholine receptors produces the anticholinergic side effects: dry mouth, constipation, blurred vision, difficulty passing urine, a fast heart rate and, in older people, confusion. Blocking histamine receptors produces sedation and weight gain. Blocking alpha-1 adrenergic receptors produces dizziness and falls on standing up (postural hypotension). Most dangerous of all, tricyclics slow conduction in the heart. In overdose they cause abnormal heart rhythms, seizures and coma, and a few weeks' supply can kill, which is why they are prescribed cautiously, and in small quantities, to anyone at risk of suicide. Tricyclics are still used, partly because they are cheap and effective in severe depression; clomipramine is also used for obsessive-compulsive disorder (Chapter 6), imipramine for bedwetting (Chapter 8) and low-dose amitriptyline for chronic pain and migraine.

The selective serotonin reuptake inhibitors (SSRIs) changed the treatment of depression. Fluoxetine (Prozac), approved in the United States in December 1987, was the first to become widely used; an earlier SSRI, zimelidine, had been withdrawn in 1983 because of a rare nerve disorder. Pinel points out that fluoxetine is no more effective than imipramine. Its success came from its safety and tolerability: because SSRIs act mainly on serotonin reuptake and hardly at all on the other receptors, they lack most tricyclic side effects and are far safer in overdose. Sertraline, citalopram, escitalopram, paroxetine and fluvoxamine followed, and SSRIs are now the usual first-line antidepressants. They are also used for most anxiety disorders, obsessive-compulsive disorder and bulimia nervosa. Their own side effects are real: nausea and other stomach upsets, headache, sleeplessness or drowsiness, restlessness and agitation in the first weeks, and sexual dysfunction (reduced desire, delayed orgasm or ejaculation), which is common, under-reported and a frequent reason for stopping. Older people may develop low blood sodium, and SSRIs increase the risk of bleeding, especially with aspirin or anti-inflammatory painkillers. Stopping an SSRI suddenly, particularly a short-acting one such as paroxetine, can produce a discontinuation syndrome of dizziness, electric-shock sensations, irritability, flu-like symptoms and vivid dreams; fluoxetine, which lingers in the body for weeks, causes it least.

The serotonin-noradrenaline reuptake inhibitors (SNRIs) block the reuptake of both transmitters, like the tricyclics, but without most of the tricyclics' receptor effects. Venlafaxine raises blood pressure at higher doses and has troublesome discontinuation symptoms; duloxetine is also used for the pain of diabetic nerve damage. The atypical antidepressants are a catch-all class of drugs with different mechanisms. Bupropion blocks the reuptake of dopamine and noradrenaline (a noradrenaline-dopamine reuptake inhibitor); it causes little sexual dysfunction or weight gain and is also used to help people stop smoking, but it lowers the seizure threshold and is avoided in epilepsy and eating disorders. Mirtazapine blocks alpha-2 receptors that normally act as brakes on the release of noradrenaline and serotonin, and also blocks some serotonin and histamine receptors (a noradrenergic and specific serotonergic antidepressant, NaSSA); it is sedating and increases appetite, which is useful when insomnia and weight loss dominate the picture. Trazodone mainly blocks serotonin 5-HT2 receptors, is very sedating, and rarely causes priapism, a painful, prolonged erection. Agomelatine stimulates melatonin receptors and blocks serotonin 5-HT2C receptors, and is meant to restore disturbed circadian rhythms; its users need liver function tests.

The newest class works through a different transmitter, glutamate. A single low-dose infusion of ketamine, an anaesthetic that blocks the NMDA receptor, can lift severe depression within hours, an effect discussed later in this section. Esketamine, one of ketamine's two mirror-image forms, was approved in the United States in 2019 as a nasal spray for treatment-resistant depression, taken together with an oral antidepressant. Because it causes dissociation (feeling detached from one's body or surroundings), sedation and a rise in blood pressure, and because ketamine is a drug of abuse, it is given only in a clinic, with the patient observed for about two hours afterwards. Two other recent drugs act on GABA receptors as neurosteroids: brexanolone (2019, by infusion) and zuranolone (2023, by mouth) were approved in the United States specifically for postpartum depression.

Exam trap

First antidepressant: iproniazid, an MAOI originally developed for tuberculosis. First tricyclic: imipramine. First widely used SSRI: fluoxetine (1987); a question asking for "the first SSRI" expects fluoxetine (Pinel calls it the first to be developed), although strictly zimelidine reached the market first and was withdrawn in 1983. The cheese reaction (tyramine, hypertensive crisis) belongs to the MAOIs; the anticholinergic side effects and danger in overdose belong to the tricyclics; sexual dysfunction and discontinuation symptoms are the SSRI problems. Watch the acronyms: in clinical use SNRI means serotonin-noradrenaline reuptake inhibitor (venlafaxine, duloxetine), although Pinel uses "SNRI" for selective norepinephrine (noradrenaline) reuptake inhibitors such as reboxetine, a class more often abbreviated NRI or NARI; NaSSA is mirtazapine, NDRI is bupropion, RIMA is moclobemide.

Using antidepressants: the lag, the warnings and the switch

Rekha, the student you met in Section 5.6, is now in her third week of a moderate depressive episode, and the psychiatrist at the university health centre starts her on fluoxetine 20 mg a day alongside cognitive therapy. Her case shows the practical problems of antidepressant treatment, and the first is time. Antidepressants block reuptake within hours, yet the mood usually takes two to four weeks to begin lifting, and the full effect may take six weeks or more. This therapeutic lag is one of the strongest arguments against the simple monoamine hypothesis (Section 5.5). Sleep, appetite and energy often improve first, and some improvement is often visible in the first fortnight, but Rekha needs to be told in advance that her mood may lag behind, or she may decide in week two that the tablets "don't work" and stop them.

The second problem is that side effects arrive before benefits. Rekha feels nauseated and jittery in the first week, sleeps badly and, a few days later, tells her therapist that she has started thinking more about death. That last complaint must be taken seriously. In 2004 the US Food and Drug Administration (FDA) added a "black box" warning, its strongest, to all antidepressants after pooled analyses of trials in children and adolescents found that suicidal thinking and behaviour were reported in about 4 percent of young people on an antidepressant against about 2 percent on placebo; there were no completed suicides in the trials. In 2007 the warning was extended to young adults up to the age of 24. One explanation is that the early activation produced by the drugs, restlessness and restored energy, can arrive before the mood improves, giving a hopeless person the energy to act. The warning does not mean that antidepressants should be withheld from the young: untreated depression is itself the greatest risk, and after the warning some studies found that prescriptions fell and suicide attempts rose. It means close monitoring in the first weeks and after any change of dose. Among antidepressants, fluoxetine has the best evidence in adolescents, especially combined with cognitive behaviour therapy.

Third, some drug combinations are dangerous. Too much serotonin produces the serotonin syndrome: a combination of mental changes (agitation, confusion), autonomic overactivity (fever, sweating, fast pulse, diarrhoea) and neuromuscular overactivity (tremor, muscle jerks and clonus, overactive reflexes), which can be fatal. It typically follows combining two serotonergic drugs, above all an SSRI with an MAOI, but also SSRIs with the painkiller tramadol, the antibiotic linezolid or the herbal remedy St John's wort. For this reason a washout period is needed when switching: about two weeks between most SSRIs and an MAOI, and about five weeks after stopping fluoxetine because of its long half-life.

Fourth, and most important for this chapter, antidepressants can trigger mania. In a person with bipolar disorder, or with an undiagnosed bipolar vulnerability, an antidepressant given on its own can cause an antidepressant-induced switch into hypomania or mania, or speed up the cycling between episodes (Section 5.4); the risk is higher with tricyclics and venlafaxine than with SSRIs or bupropion. That is one reason why taking a family history of bipolar disorder, and asking about past highs, matters before any antidepressant is started, and why antidepressants are used in bipolar disorder, if at all, only together with a mood stabiliser. DSM-5 counts a full manic episode that emerges during antidepressant treatment and persists beyond the drug's direct effect as evidence of bipolar I disorder.

Exam trap

Fluoxetine is not a treatment for acute mania: an antidepressant given alone in bipolar disorder can precipitate a switch into mania or rapid cycling. The first-line drugs for acute mania are lithium, valproate and the atypical antipsychotics. Do not confuse serotonin syndrome (rapid onset after a serotonergic drug or combination; clonus, overactive reflexes, diarrhoea) with neuroleptic malignant syndrome (slower onset after an antipsychotic; "lead-pipe" rigidity, fever, confusion; Chapter 4). The lag of two to four weeks before mood lifts is normal, not a sign of failure.

How well do antidepressants work?

Pinel gives the sobering arithmetic. In clinical trials about 50 percent of depressed patients improve on antidepressants, but about 35 percent improve on placebo, so the drug adds benefit for roughly 15 people in every hundred beyond what they would have gained anyway. In 2008 Irving Kirsch and his colleagues analysed all the trial data that drug companies had submitted to the FDA for four newer antidepressants, including unpublished trials. The average difference between drug and placebo was only about 1.8 points on the 52-point Hamilton scale, smaller than the 3 points that the UK's National Institute for Health and Care Excellence then treated as clinically significant. The difference grew with severity and became clinically meaningful only in very severe depression, largely because placebo worked less well there. A 2010 analysis by Jay Fournier and colleagues reached a similar conclusion: little advantage over placebo in mild depression, a substantial one in very severe depression.

These findings are often misreported as "antidepressants don't work". The largest analysis, a 2018 network meta-analysis by Andrea Cipriani and colleagues of 522 trials with more than 116,000 participants, found that all 21 antidepressants studied were more effective than placebo, though the effects were modest and differed between drugs. The fair conclusion is that antidepressants help, that their advantage over placebo is modest on average and largest in severe depression, and that they are not the first or only answer for mild depression, where guidelines favour psychological treatment.

Key study: STAR*D (Rush and colleagues, 2006)

What should a doctor do when the first antidepressant fails? The Sequenced Treatment Alternatives to Relieve Depression study, funded by the US National Institute of Mental Health and led by A. John Rush, enrolled 4,041 outpatients with major depression in ordinary psychiatric and primary-care clinics. All began on the SSRI citalopram; those who did not remit could move through up to three more steps, switching to another drug (sertraline, bupropion, venlafaxine, and later mirtazapine, nortriptyline or tranylcypromine), adding a second drug (such as bupropion, lithium or thyroid hormone) or, at step two, adding or switching to cognitive therapy. About a third remitted at step one, about 30 percent of those who entered step two remitted then, and only about 14 percent at each of steps three and four. The published cumulative remission rate was about two-thirds (67 percent), but relapse was commoner in people who needed more steps, and a 2023 reanalysis argued that, counted strictly, the cumulative figure was nearer 35 percent. The lessons: many patients need more than one attempt, persistence pays at first, and each failed step makes the next success less likely.

For treatment-resistant depression the main strategies are to optimise (check that the dose was adequate and the tablets actually taken), to switch to a drug of another class, to combine two antidepressants with different actions, or to augment, adding a drug that is not itself an antidepressant. The augmenting drugs with the best evidence are lithium, thyroid hormone (T3) and certain atypical antipsychotics such as aripiprazole and quetiapine. Adding a psychological treatment, electroconvulsive therapy and the newer treatments described below complete the options.

Lithium: the gold standard mood stabiliser

A mood stabiliser is a drug that treats mania, depression or both in bipolar disorder and, above all, prevents new episodes without pushing the person into the opposite pole. Lithium was the first, and it remains the standard against which the others are measured. You read in the chapter opening how Cade found it and how Mogens Schou's trials in Denmark established it. The decisive step was the demonstration of prophylaxis, the prevention of further episodes: in 1967 Poul Baastrup and Schou reported that patients kept on lithium had far fewer relapses, and when critics called prophylactic lithium "another therapeutic myth" in 1968, Baastrup, Schou and their colleagues answered in 1970 with a double-blind trial in which patients switched to placebo relapsed far more often than those who continued lithium. The United States approved lithium for mania in 1970.

Lithium is used for acute mania (although it takes a week or more to act, so it is often combined at first with an antipsychotic), for the long-term prevention of both manic and depressive episodes, and to augment antidepressants in resistant unipolar depression. It has one property no other psychiatric drug can match: it lowers the risk of suicide. In one large study of more than 20,000 patients, the rate of completed suicide was 2.7 times higher among people taking valproate than among those taking lithium, and meta-analyses of trials confirm a marked anti-suicidal effect. Classic lithium responders tend to have euphoric manias, episodes with full recovery in between and relatives who also responded to lithium; people with rapid cycling or mixed states respond less well. Even so, Barlow and Durand note that only about half of patients respond well at first, and many relapse over the years even while taking it. How lithium works is still uncertain; leading ideas involve its effects on second-messenger systems inside neurons (it inhibits the enzyme inositol monophosphatase and the enzyme glycogen synthase kinase-3) and its neuroprotective actions.

Lithium's great drawback is its therapeutic index, the gap between an effective dose and a toxic one, which is narrow. The body handles lithium like sodium: it is not broken down, and the kidneys excrete it almost entirely. Whenever the body is short of sodium or water, the kidneys hold on to sodium and lithium together, and the blood level rises. Blood levels must therefore be measured regularly, about 12 hours after the last dose, weekly when treatment starts and after every change of dose, and every three to six months once the person is stable, along with tests of kidney and thyroid function. Table 5.22 shows what the levels mean.

Table 5.22 Serum lithium levels and what they mean (12-hour levels; exact targets vary between guidelines).
Serum level (mmol/L)MeaningWhat you may see
Below about 0.4Usually too low to protectHigher risk of relapse
About 0.6 to 0.8 (0.4 to 0.6 in many older people; up to 1.0 after a relapse on lithium)Usual maintenance rangeFine tremor, thirst, passing more urine
About 0.8 to 1.2Range often used in acute maniaMore side effects
Above about 1.5ToxicityCoarse tremor, vomiting, diarrhoea, unsteadiness, slurred speech, drowsiness
Above about 2.0Moderate to severe toxicity (severe usually above 2.5): an emergencyConfusion, muscle twitching, seizures, coma; risk of death or kidney damage

Even at therapeutic levels, lithium has side effects. Early ones are nausea, loose stools, a fine tremor of the hands, thirst and passing large volumes of urine. With time many people gain weight, and lithium can cause an underactive thyroid (hypothyroidism, commoner in women), an overactive parathyroid gland with raised blood calcium, acne and worsening of psoriasis. It can stop the kidneys from responding to the hormone that concentrates urine, a condition called nephrogenic diabetes insipidus, and years of treatment can slowly reduce kidney function. Taken in early pregnancy, lithium slightly raises the risk of a heart malformation called Ebstein's anomaly, though recent studies suggest the risk is smaller than once feared.

Return to Venkat from Section 5.6. After his manic relapse he restarts lithium and is well for a year. Then, in a hot May, he develops a bout of gastroenteritis with vomiting and diarrhoea, and a colleague gives him ibuprofen for his aching muscles. Four days later his wife notices that his hands are shaking coarsely, his speech is slurred, he walks like a drunk man and he seems confused. His lithium level is 2.1 mmol/L. Every element of the story is a classic cause of lithium toxicity: loss of fluid and salt (vomiting, diarrhoea, heavy sweating in the heat), and a drug that reduces the kidneys' excretion of lithium (anti-inflammatory painkillers such as ibuprofen; diuretics, especially thiazides; and certain blood-pressure drugs called ACE inhibitors). Treatment is to stop lithium, restore fluids and, if the level is very high or the patient very ill, remove the lithium by haemodialysis. People on lithium and their families should be taught these warning signs, and told to drink enough water, keep their salt intake steady and tell every doctor that they take lithium.

The other great problem is adherence. Kay Redfield Jamison, who has bipolar disorder herself (Section 5.4), described in An Unquiet Mind (1995) how she repeatedly stopped lithium because she missed her highs and hated its effects, and paid for it with severe episodes. Stopping lithium suddenly carries a particular risk of rebound mania, so when it has to be stopped it should be tapered over weeks.

Exam trap

Lithium is the gold-standard mood stabiliser for both the treatment and the prophylaxis of bipolar disorder, and the only one with a clear anti-suicidal effect. Fluoxetine (an antidepressant that can trigger mania), haloperidol (an antipsychotic used short term for agitation) and lorazepam (a benzodiazepine used as an adjunct for agitation and sleep) are not mood stabilisers. A fine tremor is a common side effect at therapeutic levels; a coarse tremor with vomiting, unsteadiness and confusion means toxicity, usually above about 1.5 mmol/L. Dehydration, diarrhoea, a low-salt diet, NSAIDs and thiazide diuretics raise lithium levels.

The anticonvulsants and antipsychotics

Lithium does not suit everyone, and since the 1970s several drugs developed for epilepsy have proved to be mood stabilisers too. Valproate (sodium valproate, or divalproex in the United States) treats acute mania, works relatively well in mixed states and rapid cycling, and is used for prevention; it has become one of the most widely prescribed mood stabilisers in the world. Its side effects include nausea, tremor, weight gain, hair loss and, rarely, serious liver and pancreatic damage. Its gravest drawback is in pregnancy. Valproate is a major teratogen: it causes neural tube defects such as spina bifida and other malformations, and up to 30 to 40 percent of children exposed in the womb have developmental problems. Regulators now say it should not be used by women and girls who could become pregnant unless no other treatment works and pregnancy is reliably prevented.

Carbamazepine, first tried in mania by Japanese psychiatrists in the early 1970s, is an alternative antimanic and preventive drug. It causes dizziness, double vision and unsteadiness, can lower the blood sodium and, rarely, the white blood cell count, and it speeds up the liver's breakdown of many other drugs, including the contraceptive pill. It can also cause Stevens-Johnson syndrome, a life-threatening reaction in which the skin and mucous membranes blister and peel; the risk is strongly linked to a gene variant, HLA-B*1502, found mainly in people of Asian ancestry, for whom testing is recommended before treatment. Lamotrigine works the other way round from most mood stabilisers. It does little for acute mania, but it delays the return of bipolar depression and is used for long-term prevention when depressions dominate the illness. It too can cause Stevens-Johnson syndrome, so its dose must be increased very slowly, and more slowly still when it is combined with valproate, which raises lamotrigine levels.

The antipsychotics, taught in Chapter 4, are now the fastest treatment for acute mania. A 2011 network meta-analysis by Cipriani and colleagues found that, as a group, antipsychotics such as haloperidol, risperidone and olanzapine worked better and faster in acute mania than the mood stabilisers. The atypical (second-generation) antipsychotics, olanzapine, risperidone, quetiapine and aripiprazole among them, are widely used both for acute mania and for maintenance, at the price of weight gain and metabolic problems. The older antipsychotic haloperidol is effective but causes movement side effects and is used mainly in the short term for severe agitation. Benzodiazepines such as lorazepam and clonazepam are useful adjuncts for agitation and sleeplessness in the first days of a manic episode, and lorazepam is the first treatment for catatonia, but they treat neither the mania itself nor its recurrence.

Bipolar depression is the hardest phase to treat. Antidepressants added to a mood stabiliser have disappointed: in the STEP-BD programme, a large US study of bipolar disorder, Gary Sachs and colleagues (2007) found that adding an antidepressant (bupropion or paroxetine) to a mood stabiliser produced no more durable recoveries than adding placebo. The treatments with better evidence are quetiapine, lurasidone, the combination of olanzapine with fluoxetine, lamotrigine (for prevention) and lithium itself. Table 5.23 brings the drugs for bipolar disorder together.

Table 5.23 Drugs used in bipolar disorder.
DrugMain usesKey side effectsSpecial cautions
LithiumAcute mania; prevention of both poles; augmenting antidepressantsTremor, thirst and polyuria, weight gain, hypothyroidism, kidney effectsNarrow therapeutic range; blood levels; lowers suicide risk
ValproateAcute mania, mixed states, rapid cycling; preventionNausea, tremor, weight gain, hair loss; rare liver damageMajor teratogen; avoid in women who could become pregnant
CarbamazepineMania; preventionDizziness, double vision, rash, low sodium, low white cellsSerious skin reactions (HLA-B*1502); many drug interactions
LamotriginePreventing bipolar depressionHeadache, rashNot antimanic; very slow dose increase
Atypical antipsychoticsAcute mania (olanzapine, risperidone, quetiapine, aripiprazole); bipolar depression (quetiapine, lurasidone)Weight gain, raised blood sugar and lipids, sedationMetabolic monitoring (Chapter 4)
Haloperidol; benzodiazepines (lorazepam, clonazepam)Severe agitation and insomnia in mania (adjuncts)Movement side effects; sedation and dependenceShort-term use only
Exam trap

"Which is NOT a first-line treatment for acute mania?" The answer is the antidepressant (fluoxetine). Lithium, valproate and the atypical antipsychotics (olanzapine, risperidone, quetiapine, aripiprazole) are all first line; carbamazepine is an alternative. Lamotrigine is a mood stabiliser for bipolar depression, not mania. Haloperidol and lorazepam help with agitation but are adjuncts. Valproate's special danger is teratogenicity; carbamazepine's and lamotrigine's is serious rash.

Electroconvulsive therapy

No treatment in psychiatry has a worse reputation or better results than electroconvulsive therapy (ECT), the deliberate induction of a brief generalised seizure by passing an electric current through the brain. Its story begins with a mistaken idea. In the early 1930s the Hungarian psychiatrist Ladislas Meduna (1896 to 1964) believed, wrongly, that epilepsy and schizophrenia rarely occurred together, and reasoned that seizures might cure schizophrenia. In Budapest in 1934 he induced seizures in a catatonic patient with injections of camphor, and soon switched to a drug called pentylenetetrazol (Cardiazol, or Metrazol in the United States). The patients improved, but chemically induced seizures were unpredictable, and patients dreaded the terrifying minutes between the injection and the convulsion.

In Rome, Ugo Cerletti (1877 to 1963), who had been studying epilepsy, learned that pigs at the city slaughterhouse were stunned with electric current before slaughter and survived it if they were not killed. With his younger colleague Lucio Bini (1908 to 1964), who built the apparatus, he gave the first electroconvulsive treatment in April 1938 to a man found wandering, deluded and incoherent, near Rome's railway station; over a course of treatments he improved markedly. Within a few years ECT had spread around the world. It proved far more effective in severe depression than in schizophrenia, and it was the most effective treatment for depression available until the antidepressants arrived.

Early ECT was given unmodified: the patient was awake beforehand and had a full convulsion, which could fracture vertebrae and was frightening to see. It was also overused, sometimes as a means of controlling difficult patients, and those abuses, and the portrayal of ECT as punishment in the film One Flew Over the Cuckoo's Nest (1975), shaped public opinion for decades. Modern modified ECT is a different procedure. From the 1950s onwards a short-acting general anaesthetic and a muscle relaxant (succinylcholine) were added, so that the patient is asleep, the muscles barely twitch, and oxygen is given throughout. The current is a series of brief pulses lasting a few seconds; the seizure, monitored by electroencephalogram, lasts under a minute or so; and the patient wakes a few minutes later. Treatments are usually given two or three times a week, typically for 6 to 12 sessions. In India, the Mental Healthcare Act 2017 prohibits ECT without anaesthesia and muscle relaxants (section 95), allows ECT for a minor only with the guardian's informed consent and the prior permission of the Mental Health Review Board, and forbids ECT as part of emergency treatment (section 94) (Book 2, Section 1.10).

The electrodes can be placed in two main ways. In bilateral ECT (usually bitemporal) one electrode goes on each temple; it works quickly and reliably but causes more memory disturbance. In unilateral ECT both electrodes go over the right hemisphere (in right-handed people, the side not dominant for language), which causes less memory loss but needs a dose well above the patient's seizure threshold, the minimum charge that produces a seizure, to be equally effective. Clinicians often start with right unilateral ECT and move to bilateral if the response is slow.

What is ECT used for? Its main indication is severe depression, especially when it comes with psychotic features (delusions of guilt, poverty or disease), when the risk of suicide is high, when the person is refusing food and fluids, or when drugs have failed or cannot be tolerated. It is also highly effective in catatonia (usually after lorazepam has been tried), in severe or drug-resistant mania, and in neuroleptic malignant syndrome. Because it involves only a brief anaesthetic, it is often safer than drugs in pregnancy and in frail older people. A 2003 meta-analysis by the UK ECT Review Group, published in The Lancet, confirmed that real ECT is more effective than simulated ECT (anaesthesia without the current) and than drug treatment in the short term, and bilateral more effective than unilateral. Its weakness is relapse. In a 2001 trial by Harold A. Sackeim and colleagues, 84 percent of patients given placebo after a successful course of ECT relapsed within six months, against 60 percent of those given nortriptyline and 39 percent of those given nortriptyline with lithium. (The authors concluded that without active treatment "virtually all" remitted patients relapse within six months, which is why some textbooks, Barlow and Durand among them, say that all the placebo patients relapsed; the measured figure was 84 percent.) Continuation treatment, with drugs or with spaced "maintenance" ECT, is essential.

Consider how this applies to Lakshmi from Section 5.6. Suppose that, despite sertraline, her depression deepens over the summer: she stops eating, lies silent in a darkened room, and tells her aunt that she has ruined her family and deserves to be punished, a mood-congruent delusion of guilt. She has lost several kilograms in a month. This is a severe depressive episode with psychotic features and dangerous refusal of food, exactly the situation in which a psychiatrist would recommend ECT; with consent (or, under the Act's provisions, with the appropriate safeguards) she might receive a course of right unilateral or bilateral modified ECT, together with an antidepressant and an antipsychotic, and continue medication afterwards to prevent relapse.

The side effects are mostly short-lived: headache, aching muscles, nausea and confusion for an hour or so after each treatment. The main concern is memory. During a course of ECT people have difficulty laying down new memories (anterograde amnesia), which recovers within weeks after the course ends. They may also lose memories of events in the weeks and months before and during the course (retrograde amnesia); much of this returns, but some people are left with permanent gaps in their autobiographical memory, more often after bilateral and high-dose treatment. Serious medical complications are rare, and the death rate, about two per 100,000 treatments, is roughly that of a brief anaesthetic. There are no absolute contraindications, but raised pressure inside the skull, a recent heart attack or stroke and a high anaesthetic risk call for great caution. How ECT works is still not known. The seizure itself, not the electricity, seems to be what matters; the seizure threshold rises over a course of treatment, suggesting an anticonvulsant effect, and ECT also changes stress hormones, increases BDNF and, in imaging studies, enlarges the hippocampus (Section 5.5).

Exam trap

ECT was introduced by Ugo Cerletti and Lucio Bini in Rome in 1938. Ladislas Meduna (1934) induced seizures chemically, with camphor and then pentylenetetrazol (Cardiazol or Metrazol). Insulin coma therapy was Manfred Sakel's, and prefrontal leucotomy was António Egas Moniz's (1935). Modern ECT is modified (anaesthesia plus muscle relaxant). Its best indications are severe depression with psychotic features or high suicide risk, food refusal, catatonia and treatment resistance. Memory effects are mostly temporary and are less with right unilateral placement. It is not a first-line treatment for mild depression.

Newer and other physical treatments

Repetitive transcranial magnetic stimulation (rTMS) uses an electromagnetic coil held against the scalp to induce small electric currents in the cortex beneath it, without a seizure and without anaesthesia. Transcranial magnetic stimulation was introduced by Anthony Barker and his colleagues in Sheffield in 1985. For depression, pulses are usually delivered at high frequency over the left dorsolateral prefrontal cortex, a region that is underactive in depression (Section 5.5), in sessions given five days a week for four to six weeks; the patient is awake throughout and can go back to work the same day. The US FDA cleared rTMS for depression in 2008. Meta-analyses show it is better than sham stimulation, but it is less effective than ECT in severe and psychotic depression. Side effects are mainly headache and scalp discomfort; seizures are rare. A faster form, intermittent theta-burst stimulation, takes about three minutes a session instead of more than half an hour and appears to work as well.

Vagus nerve stimulation (VNS) uses a pacemaker-like device implanted under the skin of the chest, with a wire to the left vagus nerve in the neck, which carries signals to the brainstem and from there to the limbic system. Developed for epilepsy, it was approved in the United States for chronic treatment-resistant depression in 2005, but its benefits are slow, appearing over months, and the evidence for them is weak; it causes hoarseness and coughing during stimulation. Deep brain stimulation (DBS) goes further, implanting electrodes deep in the brain. Helen Mayberg's work on the subgenual cingulate cortex (Section 5.5) produced dramatic results in some patients in 2005, but a large multicentre trial failed to separate active from sham stimulation, and DBS for depression remains experimental.

Two older treatments use the body clock. Bright light therapy means sitting near a light box giving about 10,000 lux (many times brighter than ordinary room light) for about 30 minutes soon after waking. It is the standard treatment for depression with a seasonal pattern (Section 5.3), often working within one or two weeks, and there is some evidence that it helps non-seasonal depression too; it can cause eye strain and headache and, in bipolar disorder, can trigger hypomania. Sleep deprivation therapy exploits a strange fact first noticed in the 1960s: a single night without sleep lifts mood the next day in roughly half of depressed people (estimates range from about 40 to 60 percent). Unfortunately most relapse after their next night's sleep, so sleep deprivation is used mainly in combination with bright light, lithium and a gradual shift of the sleep schedule (chronotherapy) to hold the improvement.

Finally, ketamine. In 2000 Robert Berman and colleagues at Yale gave a single low-dose infusion of ketamine to seven depressed patients and saw significant improvement within three days, and in 2006 Carlos Zarate and colleagues at the US National Institute of Mental Health found that a single infusion relieved treatment-resistant depression within two hours, with the benefit lasting about a week. Ketamine also reduces suicidal thoughts quickly. No other antidepressant acts within hours, which is why ketamine has transformed research into the biology of depression, pointing to glutamate and to the rapid growth of synaptic connections. Its drawbacks are the brevity of the effect, dissociation and rises in blood pressure during the infusion, and the risks of misuse. Table 5.24 summarises the physical treatments other than standard drugs.

Table 5.24 Physical treatments for mood disorders other than standard drugs.
TreatmentHow it is givenBest evidence forMain drawbacks
Electroconvulsive therapyBrief seizure under anaesthesia and muscle relaxant, 2 or 3 times a weekSevere, psychotic or suicidal depression; catatonia; resistant maniaMemory loss (mostly temporary); high relapse without continuation treatment
Repetitive transcranial magnetic stimulationMagnetic coil over left prefrontal cortex; awake; daily for 4 to 6 weeksDepression that has not responded to drugsLess effective than ECT; time-consuming
Vagus nerve stimulationImplanted stimulator on the left vagus nerveChronic treatment-resistant depressionSlow and weak effect; surgery
Deep brain stimulationElectrodes implanted in deep brain targetsExperimental onlyBrain surgery; large trial negative
Bright light therapyAbout 10,000 lux for about 30 minutes each morningDepression with seasonal patternEye strain; can trigger hypomania
Sleep deprivationOne night without sleep, often with light and lithiumRapid but brief reliefRelapse after the next sleep
Ketamine and esketamineIntravenous infusion; esketamine nasal sprayTreatment-resistant depression; acute suicidal thinkingShort-lived effect; dissociation; misuse

Where the psychotherapies fit

Drugs and physical treatments are only half the treatment of mood disorders. The psychotherapies are taught in Book 2, so here the aim is simply to place them alongside the treatments in this section. For depression, three psychological treatments have the strongest evidence. Cognitive therapy, built on the theory described in Section 5.6, is described in Book 2, Sections 5.3 and 5.4; its distinctive advantage is that its benefits outlast treatment, lowering the risk of relapse after it ends. Behavioural activation, built on Lewinsohn's theory, is in Book 2, Section 5.10. Interpersonal psychotherapy, built on the interpersonal findings of Section 5.6, is in Book 2, Section 3.7. For people who have recovered but have had three or more episodes, mindfulness-based cognitive therapy reduces recurrence (Book 2, Section 5.9). The NIMH Treatment of Depression Collaborative Research Program, which compared cognitive behaviour therapy, interpersonal therapy, imipramine and placebo, is described in Book 2, Section 8.8.

For mild to moderate depression, the psychological treatments are about as effective as antidepressants, and guidelines usually offer them first. For severe and chronic depression, combined treatment generally does better than either alone. The clearest example is a trial in chronic depression led by Martin Keller (2000), in which about 48 percent of patients responded to the antidepressant nefazodone alone, 48 percent to James McCullough's cognitive behavioural analysis system of psychotherapy (CBASP) alone, and 73 percent to the two together. In India, where there are far too few specialists, the PREMIUM trial in Goa (Vikram Patel and colleagues, 2017) showed that a brief behavioural activation treatment, the Healthy Activity Program, delivered by lay counsellors in primary care, helped people with moderately severe to severe depression (task-shifting is discussed in Book 2, Chapter 9).

In bipolar disorder, medication is the foundation, and psychological treatments are added to it to improve adherence, catch relapses early and stabilise daily life. Psychoeducation, teaching patients and families about the illness, its medicines and the early warning signs of relapse, reduces recurrences. Family-focused therapy, developed by David Miklowitz from the expressed emotion research of Section 5.6, combines psychoeducation with training in communication and problem solving for the whole family. Interpersonal and social rhythm therapy, developed by Ellen Frank, combines interpersonal psychotherapy with the regularisation of daily routines (fixed times for waking, meals, work and sleep), applying the social zeitgeber theory. In the STEP-BD programme, Miklowitz and colleagues (2007) found that patients with bipolar depression who received up to 30 sessions of family-focused therapy, interpersonal and social rhythm therapy or cognitive behaviour therapy recovered faster, and more often within a year (64 percent against 52 percent), than patients given a brief three-session psychoeducational control treatment.

Clinical link

A clinical psychologist does not prescribe, but you will often see a client more often than the psychiatrist does. Know the side effects that make people stop their tablets (sexual dysfunction, weight gain, sedation) so that you can encourage them to raise these with the prescriber rather than quietly stopping. Watch for new or worsening suicidal thinking in the first weeks of an antidepressant, especially in clients under 25. Recognise the signs of lithium toxicity (coarse tremor, vomiting, unsteadiness, confusion) and of a switch into hypomania, and treat them as urgent. And remember the commonest reason why mood disorders relapse: the treatment was stopped.

5.8 Suicide I: Definitions, Scale and RiskLower yield · 2 Qs

One night in a district hospital in Maharashtra, the casualty medical officer saw two people who had tried to end their lives. The first was Manoj, a 46-year-old cotton farmer from a village forty kilometres away. After a third poor crop and a final demand from a moneylender, he sat at the edge of his field, drank most of a bottle of country liquor and then swallowed a large amount of the organophosphate insecticide he used on his plants. His brother found him vomiting and barely conscious and brought him in on a tractor. By midnight he was on a ventilator in the intensive care unit, and the physicians would not say whether he would live. The second was Divya, a 19-year-old engineering student who lived in a hostel in the same town. That afternoon her semester results had shown two failed papers. She typed a long message to her parents saying she was sorry for wasting their money, locked her door and swallowed the strip of paracetamol tablets she had, fully expecting to die. Her room-mate broke the door down an hour later. The doctors judged the dose too small to cause lasting harm, and Divya was ready to go home the next day.

Which of the two attempts was the more serious? The question sounds simple, and it is a trap. Manoj's act was far more dangerous to his body, but when he wakes he may say that he never really meant to die and that the drink "did it". Divya's act was almost harmless to her body, yet she wanted and expected to die, and she wrote a farewell. Both of them are at raised risk of dying by suicide in the coming years, for different reasons. To think clearly about cases like these, clinicians need words that keep apart things that ordinary language runs together: what a person thinks, what a person intends, what a person does, and how dangerous the act is. This section supplies those words, then measures the size of the problem in the world and in India, and ends with the factors that raise and lower risk. Mood disorders matter here more than any other group of disorders, because depression and bipolar disorder are present in a large share of all suicides, which is why suicide closes this chapter. Section 5.9 then asks why people kill themselves and what prevents it. How to assess one person's risk in an interview, including the SAD PERSONS mnemonic, is taught in Book 2, Section 2.7; this section supplies the facts that such an assessment rests on.

The vocabulary of suicidal behaviour

Start with the word itself. Suicide is death caused by a self-inflicted act carried out with at least some intent to die. Both halves of the definition matter. A death is not a suicide merely because the person caused it (a drunk man who falls from a terrace did not intend to die), and a death is not a suicide merely because the person wanted to die (a wish is not an act). Because intent cannot be read off a dead body, someone must infer it from the circumstances: a note, a history of threats, the choice of a lonely place, the method. That is why suicide statistics are always partly a matter of judgement. Many deaths are equivocal: the single-vehicle crash into a tree, the drowning of a man who could swim, the overdose in a person who also used drugs for pleasure. Section 5.9 describes the method that was invented to settle such cases.

The language itself has changed. Older books, police records and exam papers say that a person "committed" suicide, a phrase inherited from the centuries when suicide was a crime and a sin (attempted suicide was an offence in India under section 309 of the Indian Penal Code until the Mental Healthcare Act 2017 changed its effect; Book 2, Section 1.10). Clinicians now prefer "died by suicide" or "took their own life", and they avoid "successful" and "failed" suicide, which imply that death is the goal of treatment. The psychologist Silvia Sara Canetto is among those who have criticised such stigmatising language. You will still meet "completed suicide" in exam questions, where it simply means an act that ended in death, as opposed to an attempt that was survived.

Suicidal behaviour is best understood as a series of steps, from thought to death, that most people never climb beyond the first. Suicidal ideation means thoughts of ending one's life. It comes in two grades. Passive suicidal ideation is a wish to be dead without any thought of acting ("I wish I could go to sleep and not wake up"; "If a bus hit me, it would be a relief"). Active suicidal ideation is thinking about killing oneself ("I have been thinking of jumping from the hostel roof"). Ideation is described by its frequency (how often the thoughts come), intensity, duration and controllability (whether the person can push the thoughts away), and by what stops the person from acting on them. A suicide plan specifies the method, the place and the time. Preparatory behaviour is anything done to put the plan into effect: buying or hoarding tablets, collecting pesticide, writing a note or a will, giving away possessions, saying goodbye, rehearsing the act.

Two further terms describe the strength of the wish and the danger of the act, and they are the two that examiners most like to set against each other. Suicidal intent is how strongly the person wished to die and how firmly they expected the act to kill them. It is a fact about the mind, so it has to be inferred from what the person says and from the circumstances of the act: was the person alone, was the act timed so that rescue was unlikely, did they take precautions against being found, did they leave a note or settle their affairs, did they believe the method would kill them, and are they now sorry or relieved to have survived? (Aaron Beck's Suicide Intent Scale of 1974 turns these questions into a rating; the scale belongs to the Assessment book.) Lethality, by contrast, is a fact about the act: its objective medical danger, how likely the method and the amount used were to cause death. Lethality is decided above all by the method, or mode, of the attempt. Hanging, a fall from a height, a firearm and the ingestion of a highly toxic pesticide are high-lethality methods; superficial cutting of the wrist and a handful of vitamin tablets are low-lethality methods; and lethality also depends on the chance of rescue (the same dose of tablets taken in a crowded flat and in a locked room in an empty house are not equally dangerous).

The reason for keeping the two apart is that they often disagree. People are poor judges of the danger of methods. Divya believed her paracetamol would kill her and was wrong: high intent, low lethality. Manoj may have meant only to stop the pain of that evening, but organophosphate insecticides are among the most lethal substances people swallow: perhaps low or uncertain intent, very high lethality. Both are serious. High intent tells the clinician what the person wants and is likely to try again with a better method; high lethality tells the clinician how close the person came to death and how dangerous the means at hand are. When an exam asks what the mode of an attempt indicates, the answer is lethality.

The remaining terms describe acts that stop short of death. A suicide attempt is a self-initiated act carried out with at least some intent to die, which the person survives; it may or may not cause injury. An interrupted attempt is one in which another person stops the individual after the act has been set in motion (the room-mate who wrestles the bottle away before it is drunk), and an aborted attempt is one in which the person stops himself or herself before harm is done (the man who stands at the railway line and walks home). Divya's attempt was neither: she swallowed the tablets, so she made an actual attempt, and the rescue came afterwards. A "suicidal gesture" is an older and now disliked phrase for a low-lethality act thought to be aimed at influencing others; it is disliked because it invites staff to dismiss the act, and because people who make "gestures" do go on to die. Table 5.25 gathers the vocabulary.

Table 5.25 The vocabulary of suicidal behaviour.
TermMeaningExample
Passive ideationA wish to be dead, with no thought of acting"I wish I would not wake up"
Active ideationThoughts of killing oneself"I keep thinking of the pesticide in the shed"
PlanMethod, place and time decided"Sunday night, from the terrace"
Preparatory behaviourSteps taken to carry out the planHoarding tablets, writing a note
IntentStrength of the wish and expectation of dyingActing alone, avoiding discovery
LethalityMedical danger of the method and amountHanging or pesticide: high
AttemptA self-initiated act with some intent to die, survivedDivya's overdose
Interrupted attemptStopped by another person once begunBottle taken from the hand
Aborted attemptStopped by the person before harmWalking away from the railway line

Exam trap

"Mode of suicide attempt indicates" (IHBAS 2020): lethality. The method decides how medically dangerous an act is. Intentionality (intent) is the wish to die and is judged from circumstances and statements, not from the method; a person can choose a harmless method with total intent. Conviction is not a standard dimension of suicide assessment (the nearest idea, the person's belief that the method would kill, is one part of intent), and survival is an outcome, not something the method "indicates". An interrupted attempt is stopped by someone else; an aborted attempt is stopped by the person.

Self-harm, parasuicide and non-suicidal self-injury

The neat ladder of ideation, intent and attempt has a problem, and British psychiatrists ran into it first. In the 1950s and 1960s, as hospitals began to treat large numbers of young people who had taken overdoses, it became clear that many of them had not wanted to die. They had swallowed tablets in a storm of anger or despair, to escape an unbearable evening, to make someone understand, or with no clear idea at all of what would happen. The Vienna-born psychiatrist Erwin Stengel argued in his book Suicide and Attempted Suicide (1964) that the people who die by suicide and the people who survive "attempts" are two overlapping but different populations: the attempters younger and more often women, the dead older and more often men. Calling every overdose an "attempted suicide" assumed an intent that many patients did not have.

In Edinburgh, the psychiatrist Norman Kreitman and his colleagues proposed a way out in a short letter to the British Journal of Psychiatry in 1969. They coined the word parasuicide (from the Greek para, beside: behaviour that resembles suicide), which Kreitman later defined as a non-fatal act in which a person deliberately causes self-injury or swallows a substance in excess of any prescribed or generally recognised therapeutic dose, whatever the motive. The point of the word was to describe the act without prejudging the intent. Over the following decades British usage moved to deliberate self-harm, and the National Institute for Health and Care Excellence (NICE) in England now uses simply self-harm: intentional self-poisoning or self-injury, irrespective of the apparent purpose. Self-harm, then, is an umbrella. It contains suicide attempts, with some intent to die, and acts with no intent to die at all, and the clinician's job after any act of self-harm is to find out which, rather than to assume.

American psychiatry took the other road and split the umbrella in two. On one side are suicide attempts. On the other is non-suicidal self-injury (NSSI): deliberate, self-inflicted damage to the surface of one's own body (cutting, burning, scratching, hitting, biting), of a kind likely to cause bleeding, bruising or pain, carried out without any intent to die. NSSI usually begins in the early to middle teens and may peak in late adolescence and the early twenties. Its most common purpose is to get rid of an unbearable feeling. In a review of the evidence in 2007, the psychologist E. David Klonsky found that affect regulation, the quick relief of tension, anger, sadness or emptiness, was the best-supported function of self-injury; self-punishment also had strong support, while ending a dissociative state, influencing other people, resisting suicidal urges and sensation seeking had more modest support. That relief is exactly what keeps it going: NSSI is maintained mainly by negative reinforcement, and when it is frequent it can come to resemble an addiction, with urges, craving and escalation.

Here is what NSSI looks and sounds like. Kavita, a 16-year-old Class 11 student in Pune, was brought to a clinic by her mother after a teacher noticed healed, parallel scars on the inside of her forearm. Kavita explained, quietly and without drama, that for about a year she had been cutting herself with the blade of a pencil sharpener two or three times a week, usually after shouting matches with her mother over marks and her phone. "My head gets too full. When I cut, it goes quiet, like a pressure cooker letting off steam. Then I feel stupid and I wear full sleeves." Asked whether she had ever wanted to die when she cut, she said no, "it is the opposite, it is how I stop myself feeling like I want to die". She had never told anyone; the relief came within seconds and lasted a few hours.

Kavita's account contains most of the features of the proposed diagnosis. When DSM-5 appeared in 2013, the American Psychiatric Association placed two proposed criteria sets in Section III, among the "conditions for further study", that is, conditions that are not yet official diagnoses but are described in detail so that researchers can study them. DSM-5-TR (2022) kept both there and renamed the first nonsuicidal self-injury disorder. In outline, its proposed criteria require self-inflicted damage to the surface of the body on 5 or more days in the past year, with the expectation of only minor or moderate harm (no suicidal intent); the act is done to obtain relief from a negative feeling, to resolve an interpersonal difficulty or to induce a positive feeling, and it follows negative feelings, a period of hard-to-control preoccupation, or frequent thoughts of self-injury. The exclusions show what the category is meant to capture. Socially sanctioned practices (body piercing, tattooing, self-flagellation in a religious procession) do not count, nor do picking a scab or biting one's nails. The behaviour must cause distress or interference, and it must not occur only during psychosis, delirium, intoxication or withdrawal, or be part of the repetitive stereotypies of a neurodevelopmental disorder.

The second proposed set is suicidal behaviour disorder. It requires a suicide attempt within the last 24 months, defined as a self-initiated sequence of behaviours that the person, at the time, expected would lead to death. The act must not meet the criteria for NSSI; the diagnosis is not given for suicidal ideation or preparatory acts alone, for an act begun during delirium or confusion, or for an act undertaken solely for a political or religious objective (a hunger strike to death or a self-immolation in protest). It is specified as current if the last attempt was within 12 months and in early remission if it was 12 to 24 months ago. Why make a "disorder" of a behaviour? The argument is that suicidal behaviour occurs across many diagnoses, carries its own risks and needs its own treatment, and that a diagnosis forces clinicians to record it. DSM-5-TR also added codes, in its section on other conditions that may be a focus of clinical attention, for current and past suicidal behaviour and for current and past NSSI, which can be recorded with any diagnosis or with none.

Two facts about NSSI matter clinically. First, the absence of suicidal intent today does not mean the absence of risk tomorrow. In clinical and community studies, NSSI usually precedes a first suicide attempt by one to two years, and multiple methods, frequent acts and an early onset predict later suicidal thoughts and attempts; Section 5.9 explains why, through the idea of acquired capability. So Kavita needs a careful suicide risk assessment, not reassurance. Second, NSSI is not a marker of borderline personality disorder. Many people who self-injure do not have that disorder, and NSSI often accompanies depression, eating disorders and substance use (Chapter 11).

Exam trap

"A proposed criterion set in the current DSM that involves cutting, burning or otherwise intentionally hurting one's own body without the intent to kill oneself" (RML 2022) is non-suicidal self-injury. The distractors are real Section III conditions too, so read the description: internet gaming disorder is persistent gaming with loss of control, and attenuated psychosis syndrome is mild, brief psychotic-like symptoms with intact reality testing. Persistent complex bereavement disorder was a DSM-5 condition for further study that DSM-5-TR moved into the main text as prolonged grief disorder (Chapter 7). Parasuicide was coined by Norman Kreitman (1969) and covers non-fatal self-injury or overdose whatever the intent; it is not a synonym for "suicide attempt". Self-harm (NICE) is the umbrella; NSSI has no intent to die; suicidal behaviour disorder needs an attempt in the last 24 months.

The scale of suicide in the world

How many people die by suicide? The World Health Organization (WHO) publishes the answer in its fact sheet on suicide, and the figures are revised every few years as new estimates come in. The edition current as this book was written (updated 3 November 2026, using estimates for 2023) says that more than 740,000 people die by suicide every year (its overview gives 746,000), that suicide was the second leading cause of death among 15- to 29-year-olds in the world in 2023, and that close to three-quarters (73 percent) of all suicides occurred in low- and middle-income countries. Earlier editions gave smaller numbers. Those published from 2021 to 2023, built on estimates for 2019, gave "more than 700,000" deaths a year (703,000), ranked suicide as the fourth leading cause of death at ages 15 to 29 and put 77 percent of suicides in low- and middle-income countries; those older numbers are the ones you will find in most textbooks, in Book 2 and in most papers set before 2025. The editions of August 2024 and March 2025, built on estimates for 2021, gave "more than 720,000" (727,000), the third leading cause at ages 15 to 29, and 73 percent. The three sets of figures do not contradict each other; they describe different years. What has not changed is the message: suicide kills more young people than almost anything else, and it is overwhelmingly a problem of poorer countries, not of rich ones.

Even these large numbers are underestimates. WHO itself warns that only about 80 of its member states have vital registration data good enough to estimate suicide rates directly, and that under-reporting and misclassification are worse for suicide than for most causes of death, because of stigma and, in some countries, because suicidal behaviour is still illegal. A family may not want a death recorded as suicide; a police officer may record a pesticide death as accidental poisoning; a hospital may record only the medical cause. Whenever you read a suicide statistic, ask who counted, and what they had reason to miss.

Deaths are only the visible tip of a much larger mass, and the image of an iceberg (Figure 5.13) is the standard way to picture it. WHO's data pages state that for each adult who dies by suicide there may be more than 20 others who attempt it. Most attempts never reach a hospital, and many people who attempt have never told anyone. Below the attempts lie the plans, and below the plans the thoughts. The best cross-national estimate of the lower layers comes from the WHO World Mental Health Surveys, in which Matthew K. Nock and his colleagues (2008) interviewed 84,850 adults in 17 countries. The lifetime prevalence of suicidal ideation was 9.2 percent, of a suicide plan 3.1 percent and of a suicide attempt 2.7 percent. Two other findings from that study deserve to be remembered. About 60 percent of the transitions from ideation to a plan or an attempt happened within the first year after the thoughts began, so the months after ideation first appears are a period of special danger. And the strongest diagnostic risk factors differed by setting: mood disorders in high-income countries, but impulse-control disorders in low- and middle-income countries, a first hint that suicide in countries like India is not simply depression by another name.

Read the iceberg from the bottom up and it becomes a continuum: most people with ideation never plan, most who plan never attempt, and most who attempt do not die. That is good news for prevention, because there are many opportunities to step in, and bad news for prediction, because the people at the top are a tiny fraction of those at the bottom (you will see the consequence in Section 5.8.7). Remember too that the continuum is not a staircase everyone climbs. Some people, often older men with a highly lethal method at hand, move from first thought to death within hours and appear in no layer below the tip.

What methods do people use? Around the world the answer depends mainly on what is available. Analysing the WHO mortality database, Vladeta Ajdacic-Gross and his colleagues (2008) found that poisoning with pesticides was common in many Asian and Latin American countries, firearms dominated in the United States, poisoning with medicines was common in the Nordic countries and the United Kingdom, hanging predominated in eastern Europe, and jumping from a height was the typical method in densely built cities such as Hong Kong. Pesticides deserve special mention. A systematic review led by Emma Mew and David Gunnell (2017) estimated, conservatively, about 110,000 deaths a year from pesticide self-poisoning between 2010 and 2014, 13.7 percent (about one in seven) of all suicides in the world. The availability of methods is not a side issue: as Section 5.9 shows, removing the most lethal method is the best-proven way of lowering a country's suicide rate.

Exam trap

WHO figures change with each edition of the fact sheet. The 2026 edition (2023 estimates): more than 740,000 deaths a year, the second leading cause of death at ages 15 to 29, 73 percent in low- and middle-income countries. The 2024 and 2025 editions (2021 estimates): more than 720,000, the third leading cause at 15 to 29, 73 percent. The 2021 to 2023 editions (2019 estimates): more than 700,000, the fourth leading cause at 15 to 29, 77 percent in low- and middle-income countries. If a paper offers "about 700,000" or "over 7 lakh", it is using the oldest of these editions; choose the option closest to the edition the question was written from. The false statement is always "most suicides occur in high-income countries".

Suicide in India

India's official suicide figures come from the National Crime Records Bureau (NCRB) of the Ministry of Home Affairs, which publishes them every year in its report Accidental Deaths and Suicides in India (ADSI). The figures are compiled from cases recorded by the police in each state and union territory, which is both their strength (they cover the whole country, every year, by state, city, age, sex, cause, method and occupation) and their weakness (they include only deaths the police record, and the "cause" is what the police officer wrote down, not a clinical finding). The most recent report at the time of writing, ADSI 2024, was published in 2026. Its main figures are in Table 5.26.

Table 5.26 Suicide in India in 2024: the main figures from the NCRB's Accidental Deaths and Suicides in India 2024.
MeasureFigure for 2024
Suicides recorded1,70,746 (1,71,418 in 2023; 1,70,924 in 2022)
Rate per lakh population12.2 (12.3 in 2023; 12.4 in 2022); 16.3 in the 53 mega cities
Male to female ratio73.5 to 26.5
Most affected ages18 to 29 years, 33.1 percent; 30 to 44 years, 32.5 percent
Leading recorded causesFamily problems (other than marriage-related) 33.5 percent; illness 17.9 percent; drug abuse or alcohol addiction 7.6 percent
Leading methodsHanging 62.3 percent; poison 24.5 percent; drowning 4.4 percent
Largest occupational groupsDaily wage earners 31.0 percent; housewives 13.0 percent; self-employed 10.5 percent
Students and farming sectorStudents 14,488 (8.5 percent); farmers and agricultural labourers 10,546 (6.2 percent)
Highest rates (per lakh)Andaman and Nicobar Islands 40.6; Sikkim 36.3; Kerala 30.2; Telangana 28.6

Several features of the Indian picture stand out against the Western one. The first is age. Two-thirds of the people who died by suicide in 2024 were aged between 18 and 44, which is the opposite of the pattern in most high-income countries, where rates climb with age and are highest in older men. In India suicide is overwhelmingly a death of young and middle-aged adults: students, young couples, workers in the prime of their earning lives. The second is the recorded causes. "Family problems" have headed the NCRB list year after year, with illness second; smaller shares are recorded for drug or alcohol addiction, marriage-related issues (5.0 percent), love affairs (4.6 percent), bankruptcy or indebtedness (4.4 percent), unemployment (1.5 percent) and failure in examination (1.2 percent), while about one death in ten has no known cause. These categories are crude: "family problems" can hide a depressive illness, and "illness" includes mental illness. But they point to something the psychological autopsy studies of Section 5.8.6 confirm: in India, acute interpersonal and family crises play a larger part than in the West. The third is method.
