Hanging accounts for more than six deaths in ten and poison for about a quarter, much of it agricultural pesticide.

The occupational figures tell a story about who is exposed. Daily wage earners are the largest single group, nearly a third of all suicides and more than a third of male suicides, a reminder of how closely suicide follows poverty and insecurity: in 2024 almost 63 percent of the people who died by suicide had an annual income below one lakh rupees. Housewives account for nearly half of all female suicides (22,113 of 45,245), and women make up a larger share than usual of the deaths the police attribute to physical abuse, to dowry and other marriage-related problems and to infertility. Students, 14,488 in 2024, are a growing group, and among children under 18, family problems, love affairs, illness and failure in examination were the leading recorded causes. The farming sector, 10,546 deaths (4,633 farmers and cultivators and 5,913 agricultural labourers), is concentrated in a few states, above all Maharashtra and Karnataka.

Geography matters too. Five states (Maharashtra, Tamil Nadu, Madhya Pradesh, Karnataka and West Bengal) reported 49 percent of the country's suicides in 2024, while Uttar Pradesh, with about 17 percent of India's population, reported only 5.4 percent. Rates per lakh were highest in the Andaman and Nicobar Islands, Sikkim and Kerala, with Telangana close behind. Some of this variation is real, and some is a matter of how thoroughly suicides are recorded. The way to find out is to count suicides independently of the police, which is what the study in the next box did.

Key study: the Million Death Study of suicide in India (Patel and colleagues, 2012)

Because NCRB figures depend on police registration, Vikram Patel, Prabhat Jha, Lakshmi Vijayakumar and their colleagues used a different source. As part of the Registrar General of India's Million Death Study, field workers visited 1.1 million homes in 6,671 randomly chosen small areas across the country and, for every death between 2001 and 2003, interviewed relatives or close associates about the circumstances (a verbal autopsy). Two physicians, working independently, assigned a cause to each death. About 3 percent of the deaths at ages 15 and above (2,684 of 95,335) were suicides. Applied to the national death count, this meant about 187,000 suicide deaths in India in 2010 at ages 15 and above (115,000 men and 72,000 women), well above the roughly 1.35 lakh suicides the police recorded for that year. The age-standardised rates, 26.3 per 100,000 for men and 17.5 for women aged 15 and above, were among the highest in the world. Forty percent of the male suicides and 56 percent of the female suicides occurred at ages 15 to 29. A 15-year-old in India had a cumulative risk of about 1.3 percent of dying by suicide before the age of 80, and the risk was highest in the south (3.5 percent for men and 1.8 percent for women). About half the suicide deaths were by poisoning, mainly pesticides. The study's conclusions were that official figures undercount suicide, that suicide in India is above all a death of the young, and that young women are at exceptional risk.

Indian context

India's first National Suicide Prevention Strategy (Ministry of Health and Family Welfare, 2022) summarises the national position in three sentences worth knowing. Suicide is now the leading cause of death among Indians aged 15 to 29. India's share of the world's suicide deaths among women rose from 25.3 percent in 1990 to 36.6 percent in 2016, so that roughly one in three women who die by suicide anywhere in the world is Indian; among men the share rose from 18.7 to 24.3 percent. And more than one lakh lives are lost to suicide in the country every year. The strategy itself, and what it aims to do, is described in Section 5.9.8.

Exam trap

India's official source is the NCRB (Ministry of Home Affairs), report Accidental Deaths and Suicides in India, based on police records. In ADSI 2024: 1,70,746 suicides, rate 12.2 per lakh, about three in four victims male, two-thirds aged 18 to 44, family problems the leading recorded cause (not unemployment, not examinations, not love affairs), hanging the commonest method, daily wage earners the largest occupational group. The independent Million Death Study (Patel and colleagues, Lancet 2012) found more suicides than the police recorded, with the highest risk in the young and in the southern states. Do not carry the Western "old men" picture into an Indian question.

Sex, age and the gender paradox

Ask who thinks about suicide, who attempts it and who dies of it, and you get three different answers by sex. In almost every country women report more suicidal ideation and make more suicide attempts than men, yet men die by suicide more often. DSM-5-TR puts the ratio of male to female suicides at about two to one on average, varying by country and culture; in India in 2024 it was about 73.5 to 26.5, close to three to one. The psychologists Silvia Sara Canetto and Isaac Sakinofsky (1998) named this pattern the gender paradox of suicide. They concluded that it is real, not an artefact of how data are collected, but that it is more bound to culture than had been assumed.

Why should the sex that attempts less die more? Several explanations fit together. The most important is method. Men more often choose highly lethal methods (firearms, hanging, jumping), while women more often take overdoses of medicine, which give time for rescue and treatment; the same intent therefore ends in death more often for men. Men also drink more, and alcohol both lowers the barrier to acting and makes the act less controlled. Men seek help for distress less often and are less likely to be in treatment when they die. And, as Canetto argued, each culture carries "scripts" about who is expected to behave suicidally, by what means and for what reasons, which shape both what people do and how coroners and police classify the result. The gender paradox is therefore not a fixed biological law but a pattern that changes where methods and scripts change.

Two exceptions prove the point. In China in the 1990s, rural young women died by suicide at higher rates than men, largely by swallowing pesticides kept in the home, so that an act that might elsewhere have been a survivable overdose became a death. India is a partial exception of the same kind. Men still die more often overall, but the female share is high by world standards and is concentrated in the young: in the Million Death Study more than half of all female suicides occurred between 15 and 29, and in ADSI 2024 girls under 18 outnumbered boys (6,032 against 5,151). Many of these deaths are by hanging or by pesticides, with little time for rescue.

Age works in a similar way. Across the life span, attempts are most frequent in adolescents and young adults, and the ratio of attempts to deaths falls steeply with age: in late life, attempts are fewer, more carefully planned, more lethal and less often preceded by warnings. DSM-5-TR notes that the likelihood of suicide attempts falls in middle and late life but the risk of death by suicide does not. In most high-income countries the suicide rate is highest in older men, especially those who are widowed, living alone, physically ill or in pain. In India, as you have seen, the peak is in young adults. Suicide in children under ten is rare. Finally, WHO lists groups at raised risk because they face discrimination or marginalisation: refugees and migrants, indigenous peoples, lesbian, gay, bisexual, transgender and intersex people, and prisoners. DSM-5-TR adds that suicide rates among transgender people are high and that transgender people are at higher risk of suicidal behaviour than cisgender people.

Exam trap

The gender paradox (Canetto and Sakinofsky, 1998): women have more suicidal ideation and attempts, men more deaths by suicide, mainly because men use more lethal methods. Do not reverse it. The exceptions are cultural, not biological: rural China (historically) and young Indian women, where highly lethal methods are close at hand. Attempts are commonest in the young; the attempt-to-death ratio falls with age; in high-income countries the highest rates are in older men, in India in young adults.

Mental disorder and suicide: what the psychological autopsies show

How do we know what was wrong with people who have died? We cannot interview them, so researchers interview those who knew them. In a psychological autopsy, trained interviewers meet the relatives, friends and sometimes the doctors of a person who has died by suicide, usually a few months after the death, and combine those accounts with medical, police and school or work records to reconstruct the person's mental state, life events and circumstances in the weeks and months before death. The method was invented in Los Angeles in the late 1950s (Section 5.9.2), and in its strongest form, the case-control study, the same interviews are carried out for a comparison group of living people or of people who died of other causes.

The Western results are remarkably consistent. In a systematic review of 76 psychological autopsy studies, Jonathan Cavanagh and his colleagues (2003) found that the median proportion of suicides with a diagnosable mental disorder was 91 percent in case series. In the case-control studies it was 90 percent among the suicides and 27 percent among the controls, and a combination of mental disorder with substance misuse preceded 38 percent of suicides but only 6 percent of control deaths. Depression was the commonest diagnosis. This is the origin of the oft-quoted statement that "about 90 percent of people who die by suicide have a mental disorder", and it is the reason that treating depression is central to prevention.

The method has weaknesses that you should be able to state. Relatives who have just lost someone to suicide search hard for an explanation, and in hindsight ordinary sadness or irritability may be remembered as illness; this is a form of recall bias. Diagnosing the dead from second-hand accounts is less reliable than diagnosing the living. And the proportion depends on where the study is done. In China, a national case-control study by Michael R. Phillips and his colleagues (2002) compared 519 suicides with 536 people who had died of other injuries. A lower proportion of the Chinese suicides had a diagnosable disorder than in Western studies, and acute stress, low quality of life and severe interpersonal conflict in the two days before death stood beside a high depression score and a previous attempt among the eight independent predictors. Most strikingly, risk climbed with the number of risk factors: none of the 265 people exposed to one or no factor had died by suicide, against 96 percent of those exposed to six or more.

Indian studies point the same way. In Chennai, Lakshmi Vijayakumar and S. Rajkumar (1999) compared 100 suicides with 100 neighbourhood controls and found that an Axis I mental disorder (odds ratio 19.5), a family history of psychopathology (12.75) and a life event in the previous month (15.1) all raised risk, and concluded that the risk factors for suicide are universal across cultures. In Bengaluru, Gopalkrishna Gururaj and his colleagues at NIMHANS (2004) studied 269 suicides and 269 living controls. The strongest associations were with a previous suicide attempt (odds ratio 42.6), interpersonal conflict and marital disharmony (28.0), alcoholism (23.4) and mental illness (11.1), with sudden economic bankruptcy and domestic violence also prominent. The authors concluded that in India, family, marriage, money and alcohol carry more weight, and mental illness relatively less, than Western studies suggest. WHO puts the same point in general terms: the link between suicide and mental disorders is well established in high-income countries, but many suicides happen impulsively in moments of crisis, when the ability to cope with financial problems, relationship conflict or chronic pain breaks down. The right conclusion is not that mental disorder is unimportant in India, but that it usually acts together with a crisis, a means and a lack of support.

Which disorders carry the highest risk? Table 5.27 gives the figures from DSM-5-TR and from the reviews that corrected some famous older estimates.

Table 5.27 Suicide risk in the main mental disorders.
DisorderSuicide riskNotes
Major depressive disorderAbout 17 times the general population rate (DSM-5-TR)Lifetime risk about 2 percent in mixed patient samples, 4 percent after admission, 8.6 percent after admission for suicidality (Bostwick and Pankratz, 2000)
Bipolar I and bipolar II20 to 30 times the general population; 5 to 6 percent die by suicideAttempts and deaths about as common in bipolar II as in bipolar I; risk tracks time spent depressed
Schizophrenia5 to 6 percent die by suicide; about 20 percent attemptHighest early in the illness; command hallucinations; post-psychotic depression (Chapter 4)
Alcohol and other substance useAlcohol involved in about a quarter to a half of suicidesIntoxication lowers restraint; comorbid depression multiplies risk (Chapter 10)
Borderline personality disorderRecurrent suicidal behaviour is a diagnostic criterionEspecially dangerous when combined with depression (Chapter 11)

Within a depressive illness, risk is not evenly spread. It is raised by hopelessness, which (as Section 5.9.3 shows) predicts suicide better than the overall severity of the depression; by anhedonia, which DSM-5-TR singles out as strongly associated with suicidal ideation; by insomnia, agitation and anxiety; by mixed features (depression with the energy and restlessness of hypomania); by psychotic features, especially delusions of guilt or worthlessness; and by comorbid alcohol use. It is also raised at particular times: in the first weeks of treatment, when energy may return before mood lifts; in the weeks after discharge from a psychiatric ward, one of the highest-risk periods known; and after a recent loss or humiliation.

Clinical link

Three moments call for extra watchfulness in a person with a mood disorder. The first is early recovery: a severely depressed, slowed man may have had the wish to die but not the energy to act, and the energy can come back before the hopelessness lifts; a sudden calm in a person who has been deeply depressed may also mean that a decision has been made. The second is the period after discharge from hospital, when the protection of the ward ends and the problems at home are waiting. The third is the start of antidepressant treatment in children, adolescents and young adults, when activation and agitation can appear (Section 5.7 explains the warning that regulators attached to these drugs). In each case the remedy is the same: closer follow-up, honest questions about suicide at every visit, and safe storage of medicines and other means at home.

Exam trap

"About 90 percent of people who die by suicide had a mental disorder" comes from Western psychological autopsy studies (Cavanagh and colleagues, 2003: median 91 percent); Chinese and Indian studies find a lower proportion and a larger role for acute crises, conflict, debt and alcohol. The famous figure that 15 percent of people with depression die by suicide (Guze and Robins, 1970) came from hospitalised patients and is too high for depression in general (Bostwick and Pankratz, 2000: about 2 to 8.6 percent depending on the setting). The old figure of 10 percent for schizophrenia has likewise been revised to about 5 percent. And bipolar II is not a "safer" bipolar disorder: its suicide risk is as high as that of bipolar I (Section 5.4). Severity of depression is associated with suicide risk, but hopelessness is the stronger predictor.

Risk factors, protective factors and warning signs

Everything in this section so far can be gathered into three kinds of fact. A risk factor is a characteristic associated with a higher probability of suicide across a population, measured over months or years: male sex, a previous attempt, depression. A protective factor is one associated with a lower probability: social support, responsibility for children, effective treatment. A warning sign is a near-term signal that a particular person may be at risk now, in the coming hours or days: talking about death, searching for a method, giving possessions away, a sudden withdrawal. Book 2, Section 2.7 teaches how warning signs are gathered at interview (including the IS PATH WARM mnemonic) and how risk is formulated; Table 5.28 sets out the risk and protective factors themselves.

Table 5.28 Risk factors and protective factors for suicide.
DomainRisk factorsProtective factors
DemographicMale sex (for death); older age in high-income countries, young adulthood in India; unmarried, widowed or divorced; unemployment, povertyBeing married, with children at home
HistoryPrevious suicide attempt (the strongest single predictor); family history of suicide; childhood abuse; previous NSSIPast crises survived with help
PsychiatricDepression, bipolar disorder, schizophrenia, alcohol and substance use, borderline personality disorder; recent discharge from hospitalEffective treatment and adherence; lithium in mood disorders
PsychologicalHopelessness; impulsivity and aggression; shame, humiliation, feeling a burden; poor problem solvingReasons for living; hope and future plans; coping and problem-solving skills
SocialLiving alone, isolation; recent loss or conflict; discrimination; a suicide in one's circleFamily and community support; religious or moral objection to suicide
SituationalAccess to lethal means (pesticides, firearms, hoarded tablets); chronic painful illness; debt, job loss, failureRestricted access to means; easy access to care

A few entries in the table deserve comment. The strongest single predictor of eventual suicide is a previous suicide attempt. In a British follow-up by Cooper and colleagues (2005), described by Barlow and Durand, of almost 8,000 people treated in emergency departments for self-harm, 60 died by suicide within four years, about 30 times the rate in the general population, and DSM-5-TR notes that 25 to 30 percent of people who attempt suicide go on to make further attempts. In the Bengaluru study, a previous attempt carried the largest odds ratio of all. But turn the fact around and it gives a warning. DSM-5-TR also points out that most deaths by suicide are not preceded by a non-fatal attempt: many people, older men above all, die at their first act, so the absence of a past attempt is never reassurance. A family history of suicide raises risk through shared genes (Section 5.9.5), shared environment and the example of a method; in one study the children of suicide attempters had six times the rate of attempts of the children of non-attempters. Hopelessness, the conviction that the future is black and cannot change, is the psychological factor with the strongest evidence (Section 5.9.3), and it is measured with Beck's Hopelessness Scale, described in the Assessment book. Access to means turns a wish into a death, which is why it is the factor on which prevention works best.

Risk factors add up, and the risk is highest where several of the weightiest converge in one person at one time. Take Sanjay, a 61-year-old retired railway clerk in Bengaluru. His wife died eight months ago, and he now lives alone in their flat. He has diabetes with painful neuropathy in both feet, he has been drinking every evening, and he has stopped going to the temple and to his morning walking group. On the telephone last week he told his daughter in Pune, "You won't have to worry about me much longer." She has since learnt that he has been collecting his sleeping tablets instead of taking them. His father, she remembers, died by suicide when Sanjay was a boy. Count them: male sex, older age, widowhood, living alone, chronic pain, alcohol, probable depression, withdrawal, a family history of suicide, a statement of intent and preparatory behaviour with a means at hand. Against these stand real protective factors (a loving daughter and two grandchildren, a lifelong faith), but protective factors do not cancel risk factors arithmetically. Sanjay needs to be seen today, and Section 5.9.8 returns to him.

If we know the risk factors so well, why can we not predict who will die? The answer is arithmetic, and Book 2, Section 2.7 works through it. Suicide is rare even among people at high risk, and when an outcome has a low base rate, any test, however good, flags far more people who would not have died (false positives) than people who would (true positives), and still misses some who will. DSM-5-TR states plainly that no clinical instrument yet has a positive predictive value high enough to predict suicidal behaviour in an individual patient, and that no single clinical or biological factor is a robust predictor, because suicidal behaviour emerges from many factors converging. The purpose of knowing the risk factors is therefore not to label people "high" or "low" risk but to recognise convergence, to understand why risk is raised now, and to act on the factors that can change: the depression, the hopelessness, the alcohol, the isolation and the tablets in the drawer.

Exam trap

"Suicidal risk is assessed to be highest when ..." (JEMAS 2025): high lethality, a specific plan, frequent suicidal ideas and a family history, because four of the weightiest markers converge. The distractors each contain something reassuring. "Disrupted biological functioning, no recent attempt, no suicidal ideation": disturbed sleep and appetite alone are non-specific, and the absence of ideation and attempts lowers risk. "Adequate biological functioning, no previous attempt, moderate intent": only one risk marker. "Frequent ideation, recent attempt, low lethality, no family history": genuinely worrying (a recent attempt is a strong risk factor), but low lethality and no family history make it less dangerous than the first option. Remember also: the strongest single predictor is a previous attempt; the strongest psychological predictor is hopelessness; and most people who die by suicide have made no previous attempt.

5.9 Suicide II: Explanations and PreventionLower yield · not asked yet

Go back to the two patients of Section 5.8. Manoj, the farmer, drank and swallowed insecticide after a ruined crop and a moneylender's final demand. Divya, the student, swallowed tablets after failing two papers, apologising to her parents for "wasting their money". Why did these two people, among the millions who face debt or failure every year, try to die? There is no single answer, and the explanations that researchers have offered work at three levels. Some look at society, and ask why whole groups and whole countries have higher or lower suicide rates. Some look at the mind, and ask what a person must feel and believe before death seems the only way out. And some look at the brain and the genes, and ask why some people are more likely than others to act on such feelings. This section takes them in roughly the order in which they appeared, from a French sociologist in 1897 to the "ideation-to-action" theories of the last twenty years, and then turns to what actually prevents suicide. Each theory will make more sense if you keep Manoj and Divya in mind and ask what it would say about them.

Durkheim and the social causes of suicide

Suicide looks like the most private act a person can perform. The French sociologist Émile Durkheim (1858 to 1917) set out to show that it is also a social fact. In Le Suicide (1897), one of the founding works of sociology, he assembled the official suicide statistics of several European countries and pointed to something that individual psychology could not explain: each society's suicide rate was remarkably stable from year to year, yet rates differed widely between societies and between groups within them. Protestants killed themselves more often than Catholics, and Catholics more often than Jews. The unmarried had higher rates than the married, and married people with children the lowest of all. Rates fell during great national wars and political crises, and they rose during economic crises, but also, surprisingly, during sudden booms. If suicide were only a matter of individual misery, Durkheim argued, these regularities would not exist. Something about the way a society holds its members must be at work.

Durkheim identified two such properties. Social integration is the strength of the bonds that attach individuals to groups: the family, the religious community, the nation. Social regulation is the degree to which a society's norms restrain individuals' desires and aspirations, telling them what they may reasonably hope for. Each can go wrong in two directions, too little or too much, and the four resulting extremes gave Durkheim his four types of suicide (Table 5.29 and Figure 5.14).

Egoistic suicide arises from too little integration. When a person's ties to others are weak, nothing outside the self gives life meaning or holds the person back in a crisis. This, for Durkheim, was why Protestants (whose religion encouraged individual judgement and had fewer shared practices) had higher rates than Catholics, why the unmarried had higher rates than the married, and why rates fell in wartime, when a common cause drew people together. Altruistic suicide arises from the opposite: integration so strong that the individual counts for little beside the group, and dies for it or at its demand. Durkheim's examples included the old and sick in some traditional societies, who were expected to die rather than burden the group, followers who killed themselves on the death of their chief, and Hindu widows who died on their husbands' funeral pyres; he also argued that the high suicide rate of soldiers reflected the altruistic spirit of military life. Modern illustrations include the kamikaze pilots of the Second World War and suicide bombers.

Anomic suicide arises from too little regulation. Durkheim used the word anomie (from the Greek for "without law") for a state of normlessness, in which sudden change leaves people without the limits that once told them what to expect. A businessman ruined overnight, a family thrown out of its accustomed place by economic collapse, and equally a person suddenly made rich, all lose the frame that held their desires in check. Durkheim also wrote of conjugal anomie, the raised suicide rate of the divorced. Fatalistic suicide, finally, arises from too much regulation: a life so tightly controlled that the future is, in Durkheim's words, pitilessly blocked. He mentioned it only in a footnote, as the suicide of slaves, of very young husbands and of childless married women, and thought it of little importance in modern societies; later writers have applied it to prisoners and to people trapped in oppressive institutions or marriages.

Table 5.29 Durkheim's four types of suicide (Le Suicide, 1897).
TypeSocial conditionDurkheim's examplesA modern illustration
EgoisticToo little integration: weak ties to family, religion, communityProtestants more than Catholics; unmarried more than married; rates fall in wartimeAn isolated migrant worker with no one to turn to
AltruisticToo much integration: the self counts for little against the groupThe old and sick expected to die; widows on the husband's pyre; soldiersKamikaze pilots; suicide bombers; ritual hara-kiri
AnomicToo little regulation: norms collapse after sudden changeEconomic crises and sudden booms; divorceA trader ruined overnight; a farmer after crop failure and debt
FatalisticToo much regulation: a future blocked by oppressive controlSlaves; very young husbands; childless married women (in a footnote)Prisoners; a young bride trapped in a harassing marriage

You can use the types to think about Manoj and Divya, provided you remember that Durkheim was explaining rates in groups, not single deaths. A wave of suicides among indebted farmers after repeated crop failures has an anomic quality: the expected order of planting, harvest and repayment has collapsed. The rising suicides of students far from home in coaching towns and hostels have an egoistic quality, and the crushing, tightly controlled pressure of examinations and family expectation could be read as fatalistic. These readings are illustrations, not findings, but they show why the theory is still taught.

Durkheim's achievement was to show that suicide has social causes that can be studied with numbers, and some of his claims have held up remarkably well: social isolation remains a strong risk factor, marriage and children remain protective on average, and suicides rise in economic recessions. The criticisms are equally important. He relied on official statistics, and the American sociologist Jack D. Douglas argued in The Social Meanings of Suicide (1967) that such statistics are themselves social products: a Catholic family, a coroner or a village policeman may conceal a suicide that a Protestant one would record, so the "Catholic advantage" could partly be an artefact. He reasoned from the rates of whole groups to the motives of individuals, a step now called the ecological fallacy. And the theory cannot say why, in a society of millions living under the same anomie, only a few people die. For that, you need to look at the mind.

Exam trap

Durkheim, Le Suicide, 1897; two dimensions, integration and regulation. Egoistic = too little integration (the isolated, the unmarried). Altruistic = too much integration (dying for the group: widows on the pyre, soldiers, hara-kiri, suicide bombers). Anomic = too little regulation (sudden change, ruin or sudden wealth, divorce; "loss of a high-prestige job" is anomic, not egoistic). Fatalistic = too much regulation (slaves, prisoners; the type Durkheim put in a footnote). Textbooks disagree about mass cult suicides: Barlow and Durand call the 1997 Heaven's Gate deaths fatalistic, others call cult suicides altruistic, so expect the question to be about the clear cases. The four types are Durkheim's, not Freud's or Shneidman's.

From Freud to Shneidman: suicide as unbearable pain

The first psychological theory of suicide was psychoanalytic. In "Mourning and Melancholia" (1917), Sigmund Freud (1856 to 1939) proposed that in melancholia the lost loved person is taken into the self (introjected), so that the anger felt towards the one who left is turned against the self (Section 5.6 explains the theory of depression it belongs to). Suicide, on this view, is murder turned round: the ego can kill itself only when it treats itself as an object, directing at itself the hostility it feels towards someone else. The American psychiatrist Karl Menninger (1893 to 1990) developed the idea in Man Against Himself (1938), proposing that every suicide contains three wishes: the wish to kill, the wish to be killed and the wish to die. These ideas capture something clinicians do see (the note that blames, the death that punishes the survivors), but they proved almost impossible to test, and the modern science of suicide began somewhere else: in a coroner's vault in Los Angeles.

Key study: Shneidman, Farberow and the suicide notes (1949 to 1957)

In 1949 Edwin S. Shneidman (1918 to 2009), a young psychologist at a Veterans Administration hospital in Los Angeles, was asked to write letters of condolence to the widows of two veterans who had killed themselves. Looking for background in the coroner's office, he found a large collection of suicide notes kept in its vaults. Together with the psychologist Norman L. Farberow, he saw an opportunity for a controlled study. They collected genuine suicide notes and, for comparison, asked non-suicidal men, matched to the writers of the genuine notes, to write the note they would leave if they were about to kill themselves. Then they compared the two sets. The genuine notes turned out to be recognisably different from the imitations: more concrete, and often taken up with practical instructions to the survivors. Their papers of 1957, and the book Clues to Suicide of the same year, launched the systematic psychological study of suicide. The lesson was that the suicidal state of mind has a structure of its own, and can be studied.

In 1958 Shneidman and Farberow founded the Los Angeles Suicide Prevention Center, soon joined by the psychiatrist Robert E. Litman, and it became the model for crisis services across the United States (Book 2, Section 9.7). The Center's staff were soon asked by the county's chief medical examiner-coroner, Theodore Curphey, to help with equivocal deaths, deaths in which the manner (natural, accident, suicide or homicide) was unclear. Their method of interviewing those who had known the dead person, which Shneidman named the psychological autopsy, is the method behind the findings of Section 5.8.6. Shneidman went on to name the whole field suicidology, to found the American Association of Suicidology in 1968, and to coin the term postvention for the care of those bereaved by suicide (Section 5.9.8).

His mature theory is captured in one word that he coined: psychache, the intolerable psychological pain of hurt, anguish, shame, guilt, humiliation, loneliness and dread. In "Suicide as psychache" (1993) and later work, Shneidman argued that suicide is caused by psychache, and that psychache in turn comes from psychological needs that are frustrated or thwarted (he drew the list of needs from the personality theory of Henry Murray, whom he regarded as his mentor): the need for love and belonging, for achievement, for autonomy, for avoiding shame. Suicide occurs when the pain is judged unbearable and death is seen as the way to stop it. In this view suicide is less a movement towards death than a movement away from pain, and the task of prevention is to reduce the pain, even a little, rather than to argue with the wish to die.

Shneidman pictured the suicidal moment with a cubic model. Imagine a cube with three dimensions, each rated from 1 (low) to 5 (high): pain (psychache), press (the stresses, inner and outer, that bear down on the person) and perturbation (the state of being agitated and upset, including the narrowing of thought and the readiness to act on impulse). Most people, most of the time, sit in the low corners of the cube. Suicide occurs in the corner where all three are at their maximum, and lowering any one of them, by relieving pain, easing the pressure or calming the agitation, moves the person out of danger. In Definition of Suicide (1985) he also listed ten features that he believed all suicides share. Among them are that the common stimulus is unbearable psychological pain, the common stressor frustrated needs, the common emotion hopelessness and helplessness, and the common purpose to seek a solution. Two more are central to prevention. The common internal attitude is ambivalence: the suicidal person wants to die and, at the same time, wants to be rescued, which is why a helpline call or a friend's question can tip the balance towards life. The common cognitive state is constriction, a tunnel vision in which the options shrink to two, an impossible life or death. Shneidman liked to say that the most dangerous word in suicidology is "only": "the only thing I can do".

Shneidman's theory is humane, clinically useful and close to what suicidal people say, and psychache can be measured with questionnaires; in a number of studies psychological pain is linked to suicidal ideation over and above depression. Its weakness is the same as that of every theory of pain and despair. Psychache is common, and most people who suffer it never try to die. A theory of suicide must also explain who acts.

Exam trap

Edwin S. Shneidman (sometimes misprinted "Edward") is the founder of suicidology. Credit him with: psychache, the psychological autopsy (with Farberow and Litman, Los Angeles), the Los Angeles Suicide Prevention Center (with Farberow, 1958), the American Association of Suicidology (1968), postvention, the cubic model (pain, press, perturbation), and constriction and ambivalence as features of the suicidal mind. Freud (1917) saw suicide as hostility turned inward; Menninger (1938) gave the wishes to kill, to be killed and to die. Durkheim's types are sociological, not psychological.

Hopelessness and escape: Beck and Baumeister

If psychological pain alone does not explain suicide, what does pain have to be joined by? The first answer to be tested properly came from Aaron T. Beck (1921 to 2021), whose cognitive theory of depression you met in Section 5.6. Beck noticed that his suicidal patients differed from his other depressed patients less in how sad they were than in what they expected. They saw no way out. Hopelessness, the expectation that the future holds nothing good and that nothing one does can change it, turns misery into a reason to die: if the pain will last for ever, ending life looks like the only way to end the pain. To measure it, Beck and his colleagues built the Beck Hopelessness Scale (1974), twenty true-or-false statements about the future (described in the Assessment book). Then they tested whether it predicted death.

Key study: hopelessness and eventual suicide (Beck and colleagues, 1985)

Beck and his colleagues Robert Steer, Maria Kovacs and B. Garrison studied 207 patients who had been admitted to hospital because of suicidal ideation (not because of a recent attempt), measuring their depression, hopelessness and many other variables at admission. They then followed the patients for five to ten years. Fourteen of them died by suicide. Of all the data collected at admission, only two measures predicted who would die: the score on the Hopelessness Scale and the pessimism item of the Beck Depression Inventory, itself a one-item measure of hopelessness. A Hopelessness Scale score of 10 or more identified 91 percent of the patients who later died by suicide. The severity of depression as a whole did not predict the deaths. The conclusion, now part of every clinical textbook, was that hopelessness is a better long-term predictor of suicide than depression itself. The criticism is the one that dogs all prediction: many patients with high scores did not die, so the scale identifies a high-risk group, not the individuals within it. And in a parallel follow-up of 413 patients admitted after suicide attempts, Beck and Steer (1989) found that hopelessness did not predict eventual suicide; a diagnosis of alcoholism and the precautions the patient had taken against discovery at the time of the attempt did.

Hopelessness is a theme that runs through every later theory, and it explains why depressive cognition matters so much for suicide. The negative triad of Section 5.6 contains it (the negative view of the future), and the hopelessness theory of depression of Lyn Abramson, Gerald Metalsky and Lauren Alloy (1989) gives it a central place. In practice, hopelessness is one of the most treatable risk factors: a short course of cognitive therapy aimed at suicidal thinking (Book 2, Sections 5.4 and 9.7) works largely by loosening the conviction that nothing can change.

A second answer, from social psychology, explains why people who have done well can suddenly be at risk. In "Suicide as escape from self" (1990), Roy F. Baumeister (born 1953) proposed an escape theory in which suicide is the last step in an attempt to escape from painful awareness of oneself. He described a causal chain of six steps, and it can be followed in Divya's afternoon.

  1. Events fall severely short of standards and expectations. The shortfall is measured against the person's own standards, which is why it can strike those with high expectations or a recent rise as well as those who have always struggled. Divya, the first in her family at an engineering college, failed two papers.
  2. The failure is attributed internally, to the self ("I am useless", not "the paper was unfair").
  3. Self-awareness becomes acute and painful: the person keeps comparing the self with the standard it has failed to meet.
  4. That awareness generates negative affect: shame, anxiety, depression.
  5. To escape the pain, the person retreats into cognitive deconstruction: a narrowed focus on the immediate present, concrete thinking about small actions, immediate goals, rigid thought and a rejection of meaning. Thinking stops at the level of "lock the door, find the tablets".
  6. The deconstructed state brings irrationality and disinhibition, so that drastic measures, which the person would normally reject, now seem acceptable. Suicide becomes the ultimate escape from the self.

The theory explains several things that hopelessness alone does not: why sudden humiliations and falls from a height of achievement are so dangerous, why the suicidal state can feel strangely calm and numb rather than desperate, and why alcohol, which also deconstructs thought and disinhibits action, so often accompanies the final step. Its limits are that it describes one pathway, the escape from failure and shame, rather than all suicides, and that cognitive deconstruction is hard to measure in the moment. The British psychologist Mark Williams developed a related account, the "cry of pain" model, in which suicidal behaviour follows a sense of defeat from which there seems to be no escape and no rescue; you will see its descendants in the next subsection.

Exam trap

"Severity of depression is associated with suicide risk" is a true statement (JEMAS 2025 asked which statement about depression was least accurate, and the answer was the false claim that men are more vulnerable to depression). But hopelessness predicts eventual suicide better than the severity of depression (Beck, Steer, Kovacs and Garrison, 1985: 207 patients admitted with ideation, 14 suicides, a Hopelessness Scale score of 10 or more identified 91 percent of the eventual suicides). The escape theory and cognitive deconstruction are Baumeister's (1990), not Beck's. Beck also built the Suicide Intent Scale and the Scale for Suicide Ideation; they belong to the Assessment book.

From ideation to action: Joiner, O'Connor and Klonsky

Look again at the iceberg of Figure 5.13. Across 17 countries, about one adult in eleven had thought seriously about suicide at some time, but fewer than one in thirty-five had made an attempt. Most people who think about suicide never act. That simple fact exposed a weakness in every theory so far: depression, hopelessness, psychache and even impulsivity explain who thinks about suicide, but, as E. David Klonsky and Alexis May (2014) pointed out, most of the standard risk factors distinguish poorly between people who attempt suicide and people who only think about it. A new generation of theories therefore adopted what Klonsky called the ideation-to-action framework: they explain, as two separate questions, how suicidal thoughts arise and how some people go on from thoughts to acts.

The first and best known is the interpersonal theory of suicide of the American psychologist Thomas E. Joiner, set out in Why People Die by Suicide (2005) and refined with Kimberly A. Van Orden and colleagues in 2010. Joiner's interest was personal: his father had died by suicide when Joiner was a young man, and he wanted to understand why. His answer was that a person dies by suicide only when two things are present together: the desire to die and the capability to act on it.

The desire comes from two interpersonal states. Perceived burdensomeness is the belief that one's existence is a burden on family, friends or society, so that "they would be better off without me"; it often involves self-hatred and is, in Joiner's view, the most dangerous of the theory's ideas. Thwarted belongingness is the painful sense of being alone and cut off, that one does not belong anywhere or matter to anyone. When both are present at once, and the person feels hopeless that either will ever change, the passive wish to die becomes active suicidal desire. The capability is different. Most people, however much they want to die, are held back by the body's deep fear of death and of pain. Acquired capability is the lowered fear of death and raised tolerance of physical pain that people acquire through repeated exposure to painful and frightening experiences: previous suicide attempts, NSSI, rehearsal of the act, violence, combat, even repeated exposure to injury and death at work. Only when desire meets capability does a serious or lethal attempt become likely (Figure 5.15).

The theory explains a good deal. It explains why a previous attempt and NSSI predict later suicide (they build capability), why people who rehearse an act are in greater danger than those who only think of it, and why groups exposed to pain and death have raised rates. Applied to Divya, it hears perceived burdensomeness in her message ("sorry for wasting your money") and thwarted belongingness in her life alone in a hostel far from home, but it would also note that a first attempt with a low-lethality method suggests that capability was low: the theory predicts that her risk will rise if attempts are repeated. The evidence is supportive but more modest than the theory's fame suggests. A meta-analysis of 122 samples led by Carol Chu (2017) found, as predicted, that the combination of thwarted belongingness and perceived burdensomeness was associated with suicidal ideation, and that the combination of all three constructs was associated with a greater number of past attempts, but the effects were modest, and other combinations of the same variables predicted almost as well. Perceived burdensomeness has the most consistent support; acquired capability is the hardest to measure.

The Glasgow psychologist Rory C. O'Connor offered a broader model, the integrated motivational-volitional model (IMV), first set out in 2011 and restated with Olivia Kirtley in 2018. It divides the path to suicide into three phases. In the pre-motivational phase, background factors (a diathesis such as perfectionism or an early adversity, the environment, and triggering life events) set the scene. In the motivational phase, the heart of the model, feelings of defeat and humiliation give rise to entrapment, the sense of being unable to escape a painful situation, and entrapment gives rise to suicidal ideation and intent. Moderators speed or slow each step: threat-to-self moderators such as rumination and poor problem solving strengthen the path from defeat to entrapment, and motivational moderators such as thwarted belongingness, burdensomeness, the absence of positive future thoughts and of social support strengthen the path from entrapment to ideation. In the volitional phase, a separate group of volitional moderators decides whether ideation becomes behaviour: access to the means of suicide, exposure to the suicidal behaviour of others, capability (fearlessness about death and tolerance of pain), planning, impulsivity, mental imagery of the act, and past suicidal behaviour. Manoj fits the model well: defeat (the failed crops) and entrapment (a debt he could see no way of paying), then a volitional phase in which alcohol (impulsivity) and a bottle of insecticide within reach (means) turned a wish into an act.

The simplest of the new theories is the three-step theory (3ST) of E. David Klonsky and Alexis M. May (2015). Step one: suicidal ideation arises from the combination of pain (usually psychological pain) and hopelessness that the pain will ever end; pain without hopelessness, or hopelessness without pain, does not produce ideation. Step two: ideation becomes strong when the pain exceeds the person's connectedness, meaning attachment to people, but also to a job, a project, a role or a sense of purpose; connectedness is protective because it gives a reason to go on even in pain. Step three: strong ideation becomes an attempt when the person has the capability to act, and the 3ST divides capability into three kinds: dispositional (largely genetic, such as low sensitivity to pain), acquired (Joiner's habituation to pain and fear) and practical (knowledge of and access to a lethal method). Each step points to an intervention: reduce the pain, restore hope, build connectedness, and remove the practical means. Table 5.30 brings the explanations of suicide together.

Table 5.30 The main explanations of suicide compared.
Theorist (year)Core ideaWhat turns thought into action?
Durkheim (1897)Too little or too much social integration and regulationNot addressed: explains rates, not individuals
Freud (1917)Anger at a lost loved person turned against the selfNot addressed
Shneidman (1985, 1993)Unbearable psychache from frustrated needsPerturbation and constriction at their peak
Beck (1985)Hopelessness about the futureNot specified
Baumeister (1990)Escape from painful self-awareness after failureCognitive deconstruction and disinhibition
Joiner (2005)Perceived burdensomeness plus thwarted belongingnessAcquired capability
O'Connor (2011, 2018)Defeat and humiliation leading to entrapmentVolitional moderators: means, capability, impulsivity and others
Klonsky and May (2015)Pain plus hopelessness; pain exceeding connectednessDispositional, acquired and practical capability
Exam trap

Joiner's interpersonal theory (2005) has exactly three constructs: perceived burdensomeness, thwarted belongingness (together the desire) and acquired capability (the ability to act). Do not swap in "hopelessness", "anomie" or "psychache", which belong to Beck, Durkheim and Shneidman. Acquired capability is a psychological change (less fear of death, more pain tolerance), not simply having a method at hand; access to means is a volitional moderator in O'Connor's IMV and "practical capability" in Klonsky and May's 3ST. The IMV's key motivational idea is entrapment after defeat and humiliation; the 3ST's first step is pain plus hopelessness, its second pain greater than connectedness.

The biology of suicide

The psychological theories all agree that some people are more ready than others to turn despair into action. Is that readiness partly biological? The question was opened by a single small study from Stockholm.

Key study: 5-HIAA and suicide (Åsberg, Träskman and Thorén, 1976)

At the Karolinska Institute, Marie Åsberg, Lil Träskman and Peter Thorén were studying depressed patients' levels of 5-HIAA (5-hydroxyindoleacetic acid), the main breakdown product of serotonin, in the cerebrospinal fluid obtained by lumbar puncture. In 68 depressed patients, they noticed that the distribution of 5-HIAA levels was bimodal: the patients fell into a low group and a high group. Then they looked at who had made suicide attempts. Patients in the low group (below 15 nanograms per millilitre) had attempted suicide significantly more often than those in the high group, and they had used more violent means. Two of the 20 patients in the low group died by suicide, and none of the 48 in the high group. The paper's title asked whether 5-HIAA was "a biochemical suicide predictor?", and it was the first evidence that a measurable property of the brain might mark suicide risk.

The finding has been replicated many times, with an important twist: low 5-HIAA is linked less to depression as such than to impulsive aggression. In 1979 Gerald Brown, Frederick Goodwin and their colleagues measured cerebrospinal fluid metabolites in 26 military men with personality disorders and no major mental illness, and found a strong negative correlation between 5-HIAA and an independently scored history of aggressive behaviour. Low 5-HIAA has since been found in people who make violent suicide attempts across many diagnoses, including schizophrenia and personality disorders, and post-mortem studies of people who died by suicide have found changes in serotonin receptors and transporters in the ventral prefrontal cortex, a region involved in restraining impulses. The emerging picture is that a weak serotonergic brake on impulse and aggression makes a person more likely to act on suicidal feelings, whatever the illness that produced the feelings. This is one reason why the three metabolites are worth keeping apart: 5-HIAA (serotonin), homovanillic acid or HVA (dopamine) and MHPG (noradrenaline).

Suicide also runs in families. A family history of suicide is a risk factor even after allowing for the mental disorders that run in families, and adoption studies, beginning with work in Denmark, found that the suicidal behaviour of adoptees is predicted by suicide in their biological relatives rather than in the adoptive families who raised them. Twin studies show higher concordance for suicidal behaviour in identical than in fraternal twins. In the largest genome-wide study of suicide attempts to date, described in DSM-5-TR, the genetic risk for depression raised the risk of attempting suicide across diagnostic groups (major depressive disorder, bipolar disorder and schizophrenia alike), and the genetic associations with attempts were partly shared with depression and partly unique. What is inherited, researchers such as David Brent and John Mann have argued, may be partly the mood disorder and partly a trait of impulsive aggression.

These strands were brought together by the psychiatrist J. John Mann and his colleagues at Columbia University in a stress-diathesis model of suicidal behaviour (1999). They studied 347 consecutive patients admitted to a psychiatric hospital, 184 of whom had attempted suicide. Surprisingly, the objective severity of the patients' current depression or psychosis did not distinguish the attempters from the others. What did were their subjective depression, more suicidal ideation and fewer reasons for living, higher lifetime aggression and impulsivity, and more often borderline personality disorder, smoking, past substance misuse, a family history of suicidal acts, head injury and childhood abuse. Mann proposed that the psychiatric illness and life events are the stress, but that suicidal acts happen only in people who also carry a diathesis: a tendency to more intense suicidal ideation and pessimism and to impulsive, aggressive action, linked in part to serotonergic hypofunction. The model explains why most people with severe depression never attempt suicide, and why treating the illness, though essential, is not the whole of prevention. Over-activity of the stress hormone system (the HPA axis of Section 5.5) has also been linked to suicidal behaviour, adding a second biological pathway by which stress may become dangerous. Lithium, as you will see in Section 5.9.8, lowers suicide risk in mood disorders by more than its effect on mood alone seems to explain, perhaps because it also reduces aggression and impulsivity.

Exam trap

Åsberg, Träskman and Thorén (1976): low CSF 5-HIAA, the serotonin metabolite, in depressed patients who made more, and more violent, suicide attempts. Not dopamine (HVA) and not noradrenaline (MHPG). Low 5-HIAA marks impulsive aggression (Brown and Goodwin, 1979) more than depression itself. Mann's stress-diathesis model (1999): illness and life events are the stress; the diathesis is a tendency to ideation, pessimism, impulsivity and aggression. Suicide has genetic and familial components partly separate from those of mood disorders.

Imitation and the media: the Werther and Papageno effects

In 1774 the young Johann Wolfgang von Goethe (1749 to 1832) published The Sorrows of Young Werther, a novel in letters whose hero, desperate over an impossible love, shoots himself. The book was a sensation across Europe, and it was blamed for a wave of suicides among young men, some of whom were said to have dressed like Werther or died with the book beside them; it was banned in several places. Two centuries later, the American sociologist David P. Phillips (1974) tested the idea properly. He compared the number of suicides in the United States in the months after suicides reported on the front pages of newspapers between 1947 and 1968 with the number expected, and found that suicides rose after such stories, and rose more the more publicity a story received. He named the phenomenon the Werther effect: imitative suicide after the publicised suicide of another person. Later work showed that the effect is strongest after the suicide of a celebrity, when the method is described in detail, and among young people, who also form most suicide clusters, groups of suicides closer together in time and place than chance would predict (in a school, a college, a town or an online community).

The media can protect as well as harm. In Vienna, where sensational reporting had accompanied a rise in suicides on the new underground railway, journalists agreed in 1987 to follow guidelines on how to report suicide, and subway suicides fell sharply afterwards. In 2010 Thomas Niederkrotenthaler and his colleagues analysed 497 suicide-related reports in the Austrian press during the first half of 2005 and related their content to short-term changes in suicide rates. Repetitive reporting of the same suicide, and reports that repeated myths about suicide, were followed by more suicides. But stories of people who had been suicidal and had found another way through a crisis were followed by fewer. They called this protective effect the Papageno effect, after the bird-catcher in Mozart's opera The Magic Flute (1791), who is about to hang himself when three boys remind him of another way out.

These findings are the basis of the WHO resource for media professionals on reporting suicide (updated in 2023); in 2019 the Press Council of India adopted guidelines for Indian journalists based on the WHO guidance. Its advice is concrete. Do not describe the method or the location in detail; do not use sensational headlines or photographs; do not present suicide as a solution, as inexplicable or as romantic; do not repeat the story prominently. Do report stories of recovery and coping, give information about where help is available, and take particular care with the suicides of celebrities. Notice how directly this connects with the theories you have read: detailed descriptions of method raise practical capability, and romantic or sensational stories turn suicide into a script.

Exam trap

Werther effect = imitative (copycat) suicide after a publicised suicide; named by David Phillips (1974) after Goethe's novel (1774). Papageno effect = the protective effect of stories of people overcoming suicidal crises; named by Niederkrotenthaler and colleagues (2010) after Mozart's character. The Werther effect is not the bystander effect, not the "contagion" of depression, and not the same as a suicide cluster (a cluster is the outcome; imitation is one mechanism). Media guidelines: no method, no location, no sensational headline, always a source of help.

Preventing suicide I: populations and means

Prevention can work at three levels, the same three introduced for mental health in general in Book 2, Section 9.6. Universal measures reach everyone (restricting lethal means, responsible media reporting, life-skills teaching in schools); selective measures reach groups at raised risk (training people who meet them, such as teachers, pharmacists and police, to recognise and refer); and indicated measures reach individuals already showing suicidal behaviour (treatment, follow-up after an attempt). WHO's implementation guide for countries, LIVE LIFE (2021), names four key interventions with the best evidence: limiting access to the means of suicide (for example pesticides, firearms and certain medicines), working with the media for responsible reporting, fostering socio-emotional life skills in adolescents, and the early identification, assessment, management and follow-up of anyone affected by suicidal behaviour. The first deserves pride of place.

Means restriction means reducing access to the methods people use to kill themselves. To many people it sounds pointless: surely a person determined to die will find another way? The evidence says otherwise, for three reasons that you have already met. Suicidal crises are often brief: many acts follow an acute crisis by minutes or hours, and most transitions from first ideation to an attempt happen within a year. Suicidal people are ambivalent (Shneidman), so delay gives the wish to live a chance to reassert itself. And when the most lethal method is not at hand, people often do not substitute another, or substitute a less lethal one, which they survive. Richard Seiden's follow-up of people restrained from jumping off the Golden Gate Bridge, described in Book 2, Section 9.7, makes the point at the level of individuals. Two natural experiments make it at the level of whole countries.

Key study: the coal gas story (Kreitman, 1976)

In the 1950s the commonest method of suicide in Britain was putting one's head in a gas oven. Domestic gas was then made from coal and contained a high proportion of carbon monoxide. During the 1960s and early 1970s the supply was changed over, region by region, to gas with far less carbon monoxide and finally to natural gas, which contains almost none. Norman Kreitman analysed suicide rates in England and Wales and in Scotland between 1960 and 1971. Suicides by domestic gas fell in every age and sex group, in step with the fall in the carbon monoxide content of the supply. Suicides by other methods generally rose, markedly so in some groups, but not enough to make up the difference, so the total suicide rate fell markedly. Kreitman concluded that a simple causal explanation was likely, and that neither better psychiatric services nor voluntary agencies such as the Samaritans could have produced changes of this size. No one had set out to prevent suicide; a change in the fuel supply had done it.

The second natural experiment was deliberate, and it is the one most relevant to India. In Sri Lanka, suicide rates rose eightfold between 1950 and 1995, to a peak of 47 per 100,000, one of the highest in the world, driven largely by the swallowing of agricultural pesticides. The government then restricted the import and sale of the pesticides most toxic to humans: the WHO Class I pesticides in 1995 and endosulfan in 1998. By 2005 the suicide rate had halved. Analysing the trends, David Gunnell and his colleagues (2007) found that the declines coincided with the bans in men and women of all ages, that about 19,769 fewer suicides occurred in 1996 to 2005 than in the previous ten years, and that changes in unemployment, alcohol use, divorce, overall pesticide use and the years of the civil war did not explain the fall. The explanation the researchers favoured is that people in crisis went on swallowing pesticides but now swallowed less toxic ones, which more of them survived. A third example concerns medicines. After the United Kingdom limited the pack sizes of paracetamol sold over the counter in 1998, Keith Hawton and his colleagues found a 43 percent fall in deaths from paracetamol poisoning over the following eleven years.

For India the lessons are direct. About a quarter of Indian suicides are by poisoning, much of it with pesticides, so the measures are the withdrawal of the most hazardous pesticides (WHO published guidance on phasing them out in 2024), locked storage of pesticides on farms, and better emergency treatment of poisoning in rural hospitals, which turns more attempts into survivals. Hanging, the commonest method in India, is much harder to restrict, because a ligature and a fixing point exist in every home; it can be prevented in hospitals, hostels and prisons by removing ligature points, but in the community, prevention of hanging has to rely on the other measures of this section. Barriers on bridges, at railway lines and on tall buildings, and safe storage of medicines at home, complete the list. School programmes that teach young people about mental health and coping, and gatekeeper training (Book 2, Section 9.7), are the other universal and selective measures with evidence behind them.

Exam trap

The best-proven population measure is means restriction. The British coal gas story (Kreitman, 1976): detoxifying domestic gas cut gas suicides and the total rate. Sri Lanka (Gunnell and colleagues, 2007): banning the most toxic pesticides halved the suicide rate between 1995 and 2005. Paracetamol pack-size limits in the UK (1998) cut poisoning deaths. "Method substitution makes means restriction useless" is the false statement. Note the two Kreitman papers: parasuicide (1969) and the coal gas story (1976).

Preventing suicide II: treatment, follow-up and India's strategy

Because most people who die by suicide have a treatable mental disorder, treating those disorders well is the second pillar of prevention. The clearest early evidence came from the Swedish island of Gotland, where in 1983 and 1984 Wolfgang Rutz and his colleagues ran an educational programme for every general practitioner on the island about recognising and treating depression. The doctors identified more depressed patients and treated them better, and the island's suicide rate fell in the following year, significantly below both its own long-term trend and the trend in Sweden as a whole. A later review of suicide prevention strategies by John Mann and colleagues (2005) concluded that the two measures with the clearest evidence were educating doctors to recognise and treat depression and restricting access to lethal means.

Some treatments lower suicide risk specifically. Lithium is the best example. In a meta-analysis of 48 randomised trials with 6,674 participants, Andrea Cipriani and his colleagues (2013) found that people with mood disorders given lithium were much less likely to die by suicide than those given placebo (odds ratio 0.13) and less likely to die from any cause, in unipolar depression as well as in bipolar disorder. The authors suggested that lithium works partly by preventing relapse and partly by reducing aggression and impulsivity, which fits Mann's diathesis. Clozapine is the equivalent in schizophrenia. In the International Suicide Prevention Trial (InterSePT), Herbert Y. Meltzer and his colleagues (2003) randomised nearly a thousand patients with schizophrenia or schizoaffective disorder at high risk of suicide to clozapine or olanzapine for two years; suicidal behaviour was significantly less frequent on clozapine, and in 2002 clozapine had become the first drug approved in the United States for reducing the risk of suicidal behaviour in these disorders. Antidepressants reduce suicidal thinking as depression lifts, but they need close monitoring in the young, and electroconvulsive therapy and ketamine or esketamine have a place when a severely depressed person is at immediate risk; Section 5.7 describes all three. Among the psychological treatments, cognitive therapy for people who have recently attempted suicide, dialectical behaviour therapy for repeated self-harm in borderline personality disorder, and the safety planning intervention of Barbara Stanley and Gregory Brown have the best evidence (Book 2, Sections 5.8 and 9.7).

The weeks after a suicide attempt, and after discharge from a psychiatric ward, are the period of highest risk, so continuity of care is itself a treatment. A follow-up appointment within days, a telephone call, or even brief caring letters or messages after discharge reduce later suicidal behaviour in several studies (Book 2, Section 9.7). Crisis services matter for the same reason: a person in an acute crisis needs someone to talk to now, not next month. India's national tele-mental health service, Tele-MANAS (14416 or 1-800-891-4416), launched in October 2022, connects callers to trained counsellors in their own language and, when needed, to specialists (Book 2, Section 1.10), and the skills of crisis intervention itself are taught in Book 2, Section 9.7.

Indian context

India released its first National Suicide Prevention Strategy in November 2022 (Ministry of Health and Family Welfare). It aims to reduce suicide mortality in the country by 10 percent by 2030, working along four lines that commentators abbreviate as "REDS": Reinforce leadership, partnerships and institutional capacity; Enhance the capacity of health services to provide suicide prevention services; Develop community resilience and societal support and reduce stigma; and Strengthen surveillance and evidence generation. Its three time-bound objectives are to establish effective surveillance mechanisms for suicide within 3 years, to establish psychiatric outpatient departments providing suicide prevention services through the District Mental Health Programme in all districts within 5 years, and to integrate a mental well-being curriculum in all educational institutions within 8 years. The legal background is section 115 of the Mental Healthcare Act 2017, which presumes, unless the contrary is proved, that a person who attempts suicide has severe stress, bars trial and punishment, and obliges the government to provide care, treatment and rehabilitation to reduce the risk of a further attempt (Book 2, Section 1.10, which also explains what changed when the Bharatiya Nyaya Sanhita, 2023, replaced the Indian Penal Code on 1 July 2024).

Prevention does not end with a death. Every suicide leaves behind people who loved the person: parents, a spouse, children, friends, classmates, and often the clinician who treated them. Those bereaved by suicide, sometimes called suicide loss survivors, face a grief that is complicated by the question "why?", by guilt ("I should have seen it"), by anger at the person who left, by the stigma that makes others avoid them, and by a raised risk of suicide themselves. Shneidman's term postvention covers the help offered to them (support groups, counselling, practical help with the police and the inquest) and the steps a school, college or workplace takes after a suicide to support those affected and to reduce the risk of imitative deaths, which is why postvention is also prevention for the next potential cluster.

Finally, return to Sanjay, the widowed railway clerk of Section 5.8.7, because most suicidal people are first noticed not by professionals but by the people around them. His daughter, worried by his remark on the telephone, rang him again that evening and did the single most useful thing a frightened relative can do. She asked him directly, in plain words, "Appa, are you thinking of ending your life?" Asking does not plant the idea (Book 2, Section 2.7); it gives permission to speak, and many people feel relief at being asked. Sanjay was silent, then said yes, and told her about the tablets. She did not argue with him, tell him he had everything to live for, or promise to keep his secret. She listened, told him she was glad he had told her, and asked him to give the tablets to his neighbour that night, which he did while she stayed on the line. She arranged for her cousin in Bengaluru to stay with him, and the next morning Sanjay saw a psychiatrist, who diagnosed a moderately severe depressive episode and referred him to the pain clinic. Those steps are the whole of first aid for suicide: ask directly; listen without judging; take it seriously and do not keep it secret; make the surroundings safe by removing the means; do not leave a person at high risk alone; connect them to help (a doctor, a counsellor, Tele-MANAS or an emergency department); and keep in touch afterwards. Behind each step lies a piece of the science of these two sections: ambivalence, which makes rescue possible; constriction, which a conversation can loosen; means restriction, which buys time; connectedness, which gives a reason to stay.

Exam trap

Drugs with specific evidence of lowering suicide risk: lithium in mood disorders (Cipriani and colleagues, 2013) and clozapine in schizophrenia (InterSePT, Meltzer and colleagues, 2003). India's National Suicide Prevention Strategy (2022): a 10 percent reduction in suicide mortality by 2030; surveillance within 3 years, suicide prevention outpatient services in every district within 5, a well-being curriculum in all institutions within 8. Section 115 of the Mental Healthcare Act 2017 presumes severe stress in a person who attempts suicide. Postvention (Shneidman) is support after a suicide. Asking directly about suicide is safe and recommended.

  8. Shneidman's word for intolerable psychological pain is .
