Answers: (1) lethality, (2) Kreitman, (3) 5, (4) gender, (5) National Crime Records Bureau (NCRB), (6) previous suicide attempt, (7) anomic, (8) psychache, (9) belongingness, (10) 5-HIAA (5-hydroxyindoleacetic acid).

The case of John Cade and W.B., revisited

Go back to the unused pantry at Bundoora, and to W.B., who had been "in a state of chronic manic excitement for five years". You can now name what Cade saw. W.B.'s restless, sleepless, interfering excitement was a manic episode, and in DSM-5-TR terms a single manic episode is enough for a diagnosis of bipolar I disorder (Section 5.4); the two courses of electroconvulsive therapy that settled him for only a few months were the best that psychiatry then had. Cade's chain of reasoning, from a toxin in the urine to uric acid to its soluble lithium salt, was a hunch, and his placid guinea pigs were probably simply sick; yet the salt worked, much as Meduna's mistaken belief that epilepsy and schizophrenia rarely occur together led to the convulsive treatments (Section 5.7). A treatment found by accident, and a theory inferred backwards from it, is the story of the monoamine hypotheses as well (Section 5.5), and how lithium works is still uncertain, though the leading ideas involve second-messenger systems inside neurons. What Cade's patients did show, through the specificity of the effect (the ten manic patients changed, the six with schizophrenia and the three with depression did not), is that mania is at least partly a disorder of the body's chemistry.

W.B.'s relapse and death are just as instructive. He stopped his tablets once he felt well, as so many people with bipolar disorder do (Section 5.4), and his son-in-law's letter about the first excitable, argumentative weeks is exactly the early warning that psychoeducation now teaches families to notice (Section 5.7). His vomiting, seizures and semi-coma were lithium toxicity, the price of a narrow therapeutic index at a time when blood levels could not be measured routinely; today 12-hour serum levels, kidney and thyroid tests and warnings about dehydration, low-salt diets and interacting drugs guard against it (Table 5.22). The same physiology, the body handling lithium like sodium, explains why lithium chloride sold as a salt substitute to heart patients on low-salt diets proved so dangerous. Schou's placebo-controlled trial and the prophylaxis studies of Baastrup and Schou (1967 and 1970) turned Cade's observation into the gold-standard mood stabiliser, which also lowers the risk of suicide (Section 5.9). And W.B.'s weeks back at his old firm are a reminder that recovery is measured in work, family and a life, which is why medication for bipolar disorder is now combined with psychoeducation, family-focused therapy and interpersonal and social rhythm therapy. A cheap salt opened the biological study of mood; the people around W.B. show why that study was never the whole story.

Chapter summary

5.1 Mood, Mood Episodes and How Mood Disorders Are Classified. Mood is the sustained state a person reports; affect is the moment-to-moment expression the clinician observes. Aretaeus of Cappadocia linked melancholia with mania, and in 1854 Baillarger (folie à double forme) and Jean-Pierre Falret (folie circulaire) described one illness with two poles. Kraepelin separated manic-depressive insanity from dementia praecox (1899); Leonhard split unipolar from bipolar forms (1957), confirmed by Angst and Perris (1966). Diagnosis starts from three episodes: major depressive (five of nine symptoms for 2 weeks, including depressed mood or anhedonia), manic (three of seven DIG FAST symptoms, four if mood is only irritable, for a week, with marked impairment, hospitalisation or psychosis) and hypomanic (4 days, observable, no marked impairment or psychosis). DSM-5 split the mood disorders into bipolar and related and depressive disorders and replaced the mixed episode with a specifier; ICD-10 codes them F30 to F39.

5.2 Major Depressive Disorder. Major depressive disorder needs at least one major depressive episode and no history of mania or hypomania. Its nine symptoms are two core mood symptoms, four bodily ones (appetite or weight, sleep, psychomotor change, fatigue) and three cognitive ones (worthlessness or guilt, poor concentration, thoughts of death); phobias, anxiety and pain are associated features. ICD-10 grades episodes mild, moderate or severe and adds the somatic syndrome. DSM-5 removed the bereavement exclusion over the objections of Allen Frances. Seligman called depression the common cold of psychopathology: 12-month prevalence is about 7 percent in the United States, lifetime prevalence about 15 to 20 percent, and India's National Mental Health Survey found 2.7 percent current and 5.2 percent lifetime. Women outnumber men about two to one from puberty. Episodes tend to recur, often present through bodily complaints (Kleinman's neurasthenia patients), and travel with anxiety and substance use.

5.3 The Other Depressive Disorders and the Specifiers. Persistent depressive disorder (dysthymia) is depressed mood more days than not for at least 2 years (1 in children), with two of six symptoms and no gap over 2 months: milder than major depression but longer; a major episode on top of it is double depression (Keller and Shapiro, 1982). Disruptive mood dysregulation disorder, created in DSM-5 after the rise in paediatric bipolar diagnoses, needs severe outbursts three or more times a week, irritable mood between them, onset before 10 and diagnosis between 6 and 18. Premenstrual dysphoric disorder needs prospective daily ratings over two cycles. Mixed anxiety and depressive disorder is the "hybrid", and Spitz described anaclitic depression (1946). The specifiers cover anxious distress, mixed features, melancholic and atypical features (separated by mood reactivity), psychotic features, catatonia, peripartum onset, seasonal pattern (Rosenthal, 1984) and, for bipolar disorders only, rapid cycling.

5.4 Bipolar and Related Disorders. A manic episode, like Rahul's, joins elevated or irritable mood and increased energy for at least a week with symptoms such as grandiosity and flight of ideas. Hypomania has the same symptoms for at least four days, observable by others, without marked impairment or psychosis. One manic episode establishes bipolar I disorder (12-month prevalence 1.5 percent in the United States in DSM-5-TR; equal sex ratio; mean onset at 22). Bipolar II disorder needs a hypomanic and a major depressive episode and never mania; it is not milder, because its burden is depression. Cyclothymic disorder is at least 2 years of swings that never reach a full episode. Rapid cycling (Dunner and Fieve, 1974) means four or more episodes in 12 months. Depression dominates the long course (Judd, 2002), suicide risk is high, and the illness is often missed, mistaken or stretched.

5.5 Biological Causes. Bipolar disorder has among the highest heritability in psychiatry (about 60 to 85 percent) and major depressive disorder a moderate one (37 percent; Sullivan, Neale and Kendler, 2000); no single gene has been found, and Caspi's 5-HTTLPR gene-by-stress finding (2003) failed to replicate. The monoamine hypotheses were inferred backwards from drugs: reserpine depleted the monoamines, while iproniazid and imipramine (Kuhn, 1957) raised them. Schildkraut's catecholamine hypothesis (1965), Coppen's serotonin hypothesis and Prange's permissive hypothesis (1974) all face the lag between the drugs' synaptic effect and recovery, which the neurotrophic hypothesis (Duman, Heninger and Nestler, 1997) explains through BDNF and the hippocampus. Depression brings an overactive HPA axis with non-suppression on Carroll's dexamethasone suppression test, shortened REM latency (Kupfer), disturbed social rhythms and altered circuits, including Mayberg's area 25. Post's kindling hypothesis (1992) holds that each episode lowers the threshold for the next.

5.6 Psychological and Social Causes. Psychodynamic theories made depression a response to loss: Abraham (1911), Freud's "Mourning and Melancholia" (1917) with introjection and anger turned inward, Klein's depressive position, Bibring's ego helplessness (1953) and Bowlby's attachment and loss. Ferster and Lewinsohn (1974) blamed a low rate of response-contingent positive reinforcement. Seligman and Maier's yoked dogs (1967) showed learned helplessness, which Abramson, Seligman and Teasdale (1978) reformulated around internal, stable and global attributions and Abramson, Metalsky and Alloy (1989) turned into hopelessness theory. Beck's cognitive theory (1967) is a diathesis-stress model built on the negative cognitive triad of self, world and future, latent schemas and distortions. Nolen-Hoeksema's rumination helps explain women's excess, Coyne and Hammen described interpersonal processes, and Brown and Harris (1978) found four vulnerability factors among Camberwell women. Goal attainment events, sleep loss and expressed emotion trigger bipolar episodes.

5.7 Treating Mood Disorders: Drugs, ECT and Other Physical Treatments. Treatment is judged by response, remission, relapse and recurrence (Frank and colleagues, 1991). The antidepressants are the MAOIs (iproniazid first; the cheese reaction), the tricyclics (imipramine; anticholinergic effects, danger in overdose), the SSRIs (fluoxetine, 1987), the SNRIs, the atypicals and ketamine. Mood lifts after two to four weeks, the advantage over placebo is modest but real (Cipriani, 2018), STAR*D showed that many patients need more than one step, and an antidepressant alone can switch a bipolar patient into mania. Lithium, Cade's discovery of 1949, is the gold-standard mood stabiliser for treatment and prophylaxis, with a narrow therapeutic index and an anti-suicidal effect; valproate, carbamazepine, lamotrigine and the atypical antipsychotics complete the list. Modified ECT (Cerletti and Bini, 1938) serves severe, psychotic or suicidal depression and catatonia; rTMS, deep brain stimulation, light therapy and ketamine are other options, alongside the psychotherapies.

5.8 Suicide I: Definitions, Scale and Risk. Suicidal behaviour runs from ideation through plans and attempts to death; intent is the wish to die and lethality the medical danger of the method. Kreitman coined parasuicide (1969), and DSM-5 made non-suicidal self-injury a condition for further study. WHO's current fact sheet (2023 estimates) gives more than 740,000 deaths a year, most in low- and middle-income countries. India's NCRB recorded 1,70,746 suicides in 2024, a rate of 12.2 per lakh, about three in four of them male, with family problems the leading recorded cause and hanging the commonest method. Women attempt more and men die more: the gender paradox. Western psychological autopsies find a mental disorder in about 90 percent of suicides, Asian studies fewer. A previous attempt is the strongest single predictor, and risk peaks when high lethality, a specific plan, frequent ideation and a family history converge.

5.9 Suicide II: Explanations and Prevention. Durkheim's Le Suicide (1897) explained rates by social integration and regulation, giving egoistic, altruistic, anomic and fatalistic suicide. Freud saw hostility turned inward, Menninger three wishes, and Shneidman, the founder of suicidology, described psychache, the cubic model and the psychological autopsy. Beck and colleagues (1985) showed that hopelessness predicts suicide better than depression, and Baumeister (1990) proposed escape from the self. Ideation-to-action theories separate desire from capability: Joiner's interpersonal theory (2005: perceived burdensomeness, thwarted belongingness, acquired capability), O'Connor's integrated motivational-volitional model and Klonsky and May's three-step theory. Åsberg and colleagues (1976) linked low CSF 5-HIAA to violent attempts, and Mann's stress-diathesis model (1999) joins the strands. Publicised suicides breed imitation (the Werther effect; Phillips, 1974), stories of coping protect (the Papageno effect), and means restriction, treatment, lithium, clozapine, follow-up, India's 2022 strategy and postvention prevent deaths.

From the past papers

  1. Which disorder is characterised by persistent sadness and loss of interest? (IHBAS 2024)

    (a) Schizophrenia (b) Depression (c) OCD (d) Bipolar-disorder

    Answer: (b) Depression. Depressed mood and loss of interest or pleasure (anhedonia) are the two core symptoms of a major depressive episode, and at least one of them must be among its five or more symptoms. Schizophrenia is defined by psychotic symptoms and OCD by obsessions and compulsions. Bipolar disorder can include depressive episodes, but it is defined by mania or hypomania, so a picture of persistent sadness and lost interest points to depression.

  2. Which disorder is characterized by persistent sadness and loss of interest? (IHBAS 2025)

    (a) Bipolar disorder (b) Major depressive disorder (c) Anxiety disorder (d) OCD

    Answer: (b) Major depressive disorder. The same question came back a year later with new distractors. The two core symptoms of major depressive disorder are exactly these; the anxiety disorders centre on fear and worry (Chapter 6), OCD on obsessions and compulsions, and bipolar disorder on episodes of elevated mood.

  3. Symptoms of major depressive disorder might include: i. specific phobias ii. losing interest or pleasure in things that you usually enjoy iii. changes in appetite iv. an excessively euphoric mood (RML 2020)

    (a) i & ii (b) iii & iv (c) ii & iii (d) i & iv

    Answer: (c) ii & iii. Loss of interest or pleasure is a core symptom and a change in appetite or weight is one of the four bodily symptoms of a major depressive episode. Phobias are common in depressed people but are only associated features (specific phobia is an anxiety disorder, Chapter 6), and an excessively euphoric mood belongs to the opposite pole, mania.

  4. Which illness is known as the common cold of psychiatry? (RML 2024)

    (a) Schizophrenia (b) Depression (c) Obsessive-compulsive disorder (d) Hysteria

    Answer: (b) Depression. Seligman called depression the "common cold of psychopathology" in the 1970s because it is by far the most common of the serious mental disorders (12-month prevalence about 7 percent in the United States, lifetime about 15 to 20 percent). Schizophrenia has a lifetime risk under 1 percent, and neither obsessive-compulsive disorder nor hysteria (the old name for the conversion and dissociative conditions, Chapter 7) carries the phrase.

  5. Identify the following statements that least accurately characterizes depression. (JEMAS 2025)

    (a) Its rate of incidence is widespread and increasing. (b) It is often triggered by a stressful event. (c) Men are more vulnerable to depression than women. (d) Severity of depression is associated with suicide risk.

    Answer: (c). The reverse is true: from puberty onwards women are about twice as likely as men to be depressed (estimates run from about 1.5 to 3). The other three are accurate. Depression is widespread and has been rising, especially among the young; severe events precede most first episodes, as Brown and Harris showed; and severity is associated with suicide risk, although hopelessness is the stronger predictor.

  6. Dysthymic or Persistent depressive disorder (PDD) differs from major depressive disorder (MDD) in the following ways: (RML 2021)

    (a) Symptoms include hallucinations and delusional thinking. (b) Symptoms only affect women during the winter months. (c) Symptoms are more severe and last longer. (d) Symptoms are less severe but last longer.

    Answer: (d). Persistent depressive disorder needs depressed mood more days than not for at least 2 years in adults (1 year in children and adolescents) with two of six symptoms: a quieter but more chronic depression than a major episode. Hallucinations and delusions are not part of it, (b) is a garbled version of the seasonal pattern specifier, which is not limited to women, and (c) gets the severity backwards.

  7. A 12-year-old boy begins to have new episodes of temper outbursts that are out of proportion to the situation. Which of the following is NOT a diagnostic possibility in this patient? (RML 2022)

    (a) Disruptive Mood Dysregulation Disorder (b) Bipolar Disorder (c) Conduct Disorder (d) Attention Deficit Hyperactivity Disorder

    Answer: (a) Disruptive mood dysregulation disorder. DMDD requires onset before age 10 (and a first diagnosis between 6 and 18), so outbursts that begin at 12 rule it out. Bipolar disorder remains possible if there are distinct episodes, and conduct disorder and ADHD can also present with temper outbursts (Chapters 3 and 11).

  8. Hybrid disorder is: (Amity 2023)

    (a) Multiple personality disorder (b) Borderline personality disorder (c) Mixed anxiety-depressive disorder (d) Schizoid personality disorder

    Answer: (c) Mixed anxiety-depressive disorder. It is the "hybrid" of two symptom sets, neither severe enough for a diagnosis on its own: ICD-10 F41.2, ICD-11 mixed depressive and anxiety disorder (placed among the depressive disorders), and absent from DSM-5-TR. Multiple personality disorder is the old name for dissociative identity disorder (Chapter 7), and borderline and schizoid personality disorders belong to Chapter 11.

  9. According to the DSM-5-TR, which of the following is NOT a specifier for a manic episode? (Real PYQ 2024)

    (a) With Mixed Features (b) With Psychotic Features (c) With Anxious Distress (d) With Limited Prosocial Emotions

    Answer: (d) With limited prosocial emotions. That specifier belongs to conduct disorder (Chapter 11), not to any mood disorder. With mixed features (three or more concurrent depressive symptoms), with psychotic features and with anxious distress are all genuine specifiers of a manic episode.

  10. Which of the following best distinguishes psychotic disorders from mood disorders with psychotic features? (IHBAS 2025)

    (a) Presence of delusions only (b) Mood congruence of psychotic symptoms (c) Duration of depressive episodes (d) Comorbidity with anxiety disorders

    Answer: (b) Mood congruence of psychotic symptoms. In mood disorders the delusions and hallucinations are usually mood-congruent (guilt, disease and deserved punishment in depression; grandiosity in mania) and occur only during mood episodes. Both kinds of disorder have delusions, so (a) cannot separate them, and the duration of depressive episodes and comorbid anxiety do not discriminate.

  11. An affective disorder in which a person swings from one mood extreme to another is classified as: (RML 2020)

    (a) Manic (b) Depressive (c) Unipolar (d) Bipolar

    Answer: (d) Bipolar. Since Leonhard's split of 1957, mood disorders are first divided into unipolar (mood moves in one direction only, almost always down) and bipolar (both poles). "Manic" and "depressive" name episodes, not classes of disorder, and unipolar is exactly what the question rules out.

  12. Which disorder involves alternating episodes of mania and depression? (IHBAS 2024)

    (a) Bipolar disorder (b) Major depressive disorder (c) Schizophrenia (d) Generalized anxiety disorder

    Answer: (a) Bipolar disorder. Alternation between the poles is the classic picture, although strictly one manic episode is enough for bipolar I disorder. Major depressive disorder requires that there has never been a manic or hypomanic episode, and schizophrenia and generalised anxiety disorder are not mood disorders.

  13. Within mood disorders, two key symptoms of a manic episode are: (RML 2021)

    (a) Hallucinations and flight of ideas (b) Distractibility and social phobia. (c) Extremely elevated mood and grandiose ideas. (d) Delusional thinking and intense fear of weight gain.

    Answer: (c). An abnormally elevated mood is the core of the episode, and grandiosity is the G of DIG FAST. Flight of ideas and distractibility are genuine manic symptoms, but each is paired with something that is not: hallucinations occur only as psychotic features and are not among the seven symptoms, social phobia is an anxiety disorder, and intense fear of weight gain is anorexia nervosa (Chapter 8).

  14. Which of the following statements concerning Bipolar disorder is INCORRECT? (RML 2022)

    (a) Bipolar-I Disorder includes mania (b) Bipolar-II Disorder includes hypomania (c) Bipolar-I Disorder includes hypomania (d) Bipolar-I Disorder includes periods of depression even if these do not reach the threshold for major depressive disorder

    Answer: (c), as keyed. Bipolar I disorder is defined by mania, and hypomania is the defining episode of bipolar II, so (c) is the statement that misdescribes bipolar I. Read it as a statement about defining features: hypomanic episodes may occur in bipolar I, but they are never what establishes it. Statement (d) is true, because subthreshold depressive periods are allowed in bipolar I.

  15. Cyclothymia is best defined as: (IHBAS 2025)

    (a) Alternating manic and depressive episodes of equal severity (b) A single hypomanic episode without depression (c) Recurrent dysthymic episodes lasting 6 months (d) At least 2 years of fluctuating hypomanic and depressive symptoms not meeting criteria for bipolar disorder

    Answer: (d). Cyclothymic disorder is at least 2 years (1 year in children and adolescents) of numerous hypomanic and depressive symptoms that never meet the criteria for a hypomanic, manic or major depressive episode, with no symptom-free stretch longer than 2 months; ICD-10 calls it a persistent instability of mood. Full alternating episodes are bipolar I, a hypomanic episode without a prior depression is recorded as other specified bipolar and related disorder, and dysthymia does not come in 6-month episodes.

  16. The "kindling hypothesis" in mood disorders suggests: (IHBAS 2025)

    (a) Each episode of depression lowers the threshold for future episodes (b) Bipolar disorder is caused solely by genetics (c) Antidepressants permanently cure depression (d) Anxiety and depression are unrelated

    Answer: (a). Robert Post (1992), borrowing Goddard's kindling of seizures in rats, proposed that each episode leaves a lasting trace that lowers the threshold for the next, so early episodes follow major stress and later ones need little or none and come faster. The other options are simply false: identical twins are concordant for bipolar disorder in only 40 to 70 percent of pairs, depression tends to recur, and anxiety and depression are closely linked.

  17. Which medication is considered the gold standard mood stabilizer for the treatment and prophylaxis of bipolar disorder? (Real PYQ 2025)

    (a) Fluoxetine (b) Lithium (c) Haloperidol (d) Lorazepam

    Answer: (b) Lithium. Since Cade's report of 1949 and the prophylaxis trials of Baastrup and Schou, lithium has been the standard for acute mania and, above all, for preventing new episodes of both poles, and it is the only mood stabiliser with a clear anti-suicidal effect. Fluoxetine is an SSRI antidepressant that can trigger mania, haloperidol an antipsychotic used short term for agitation, and lorazepam a benzodiazepine used as an adjunct; none is a mood stabiliser.

  18. Which of the following is NOT typically considered a first-line pharmacological treatment for acute mania in bipolar disorder? (Real PYQ 2024)

    (a) Lithium (b) Valproate (Divalproex) (c) Fluoxetine (d) Atypical antipsychotics (such as Olanzapine, Risperidone)

    Answer: (c) Fluoxetine. An antidepressant given on its own in bipolar disorder can precipitate a switch into mania or speed up cycling, so it has no place in treating acute mania. Lithium, valproate and the atypical antipsychotics (olanzapine, risperidone, quetiapine, aripiprazole) are all first line; carbamazepine is an alternative.

  19. Mode of suicide attempt indicates: (IHBAS 2020)

    (a) Intentionality (b) Lethality (c) Conviction (d) Survival

    Answer: (b) Lethality. The method decides how medically dangerous an act is. Intent, the wish to die, is judged from the circumstances and the person's statements rather than from the method, because people often misjudge how dangerous a method is. Conviction is not a standard dimension of suicide assessment, and survival is an outcome, not something the method indicates.

  20. Suicidal risk is assessed to be highest when the following criteria are met in comparison to others, the 'time' factor being the same for all: (JEMAS 2025)

    (a) High lethality, specific plan, frequent suicidal ideas, family history (b) Disrupted biological functioning, no recent attempt, no suicidal ideation (c) Adequate biological functioning, no previous attempt, moderate intent (d) Frequent suicidal ideation, recent suicide attempt, low lethality, absence of family history

    Answer: (a). Four of the weightiest markers converge in one person. Option (b) offers only non-specific disturbance of sleep and appetite, with no ideation and no attempt; (c) has a single marker; (d) is genuinely worrying, because a recent attempt is a strong risk factor, but low lethality and no family history make it less dangerous than (a). Remember that a previous attempt is the strongest single predictor and hopelessness the strongest psychological one.

High-yield sheet

The facts this chapter's questions ask most, most-asked first. Last read before the exam.

  1. Aaron T. Beck's cognitive theory (1967): depression is maintained by the negative cognitive triad, negative views of the self, the world (ongoing experience) and the future, fed by latent schemas, automatic thoughts and cognitive distortions. That pattern is called the cognitive triad; it is not id, ego and superego, not past, present and future, not self, mother and father, and not thoughts, feelings and behaviours. asked 8 times
  2. Persistent depressive disorder (dysthymia): depressed mood most of the day, more days than not, for at least 2 years in adults (1 year in children and adolescents), with 2 or more of 6 symptoms and never symptom-free for more than 2 months. Compared with MDD its symptoms are less severe but last longer; DSM-5 merged dysthymia and chronic major depression into it. asked 7 times
  3. Persistent sadness (depressed mood) and loss of interest or pleasure (anhedonia) are the two core symptoms of major depressive disorder; appetite or weight change is one of the nine symptoms. Specific phobias (an anxiety disorder) and an excessively euphoric mood (mania) are not. asked 3 times
  4. A major depressive episode needs five or more of nine symptoms (depressed mood plus SIG E CAPS) over at least 2 weeks, most of the day, nearly every day, at least one being depressed mood or loss of interest or pleasure. One week is the minimum for mania and four days for hypomania. asked 5 times
  5. Bipolar I disorder requires at least one manic episode (a week, or any duration if hospitalised); depressive episodes are common but not required, and subthreshold depressive periods are allowed. Bipolar II disorder requires at least one hypomanic and one major depressive episode and never a manic episode; it is not simply milder and needs no psychotic features. DSM-5-TR gives bipolar I a 12-month US prevalence of 1.5 percent and an equal sex ratio. asked 4 times
  6. A mood disorder in which a person swings from one mood extreme to the other, or has alternating episodes of mania and depression, is bipolar disorder (Leonhard's unipolar-bipolar split, 1957); unipolar means depression only, and "manic" and "depressive" name episodes. asked 2 times
  7. Mania versus hypomania: the same symptoms (three of seven DIG FAST, four if the mood is only irritable); mania lasts at least 1 week and brings marked impairment, hospitalisation or psychotic features; hypomania lasts at least 4 consecutive days, is observable by others and has none of the three. Psychosis makes any elevated episode manic. asked 3 times
  8. Disruptive mood dysregulation disorder (DSM-5, 2013, after the rise in paediatric bipolar diagnoses): severe temper outbursts three or more times a week with persistently irritable mood between them, for 12 months, in two of three settings; onset before age 10, first diagnosis between 6 and 18. A new onset at 12 rules DMDD out; bipolar disorder, conduct disorder and ADHD remain possible. asked 2 times
  9. Specifiers of a manic episode include with mixed features (three or more concurrent depressive symptoms), with psychotic features and with anxious distress. "With limited prosocial emotions" is a specifier of conduct disorder, not of any mood disorder. asked 2 times
  10. Lithium (John Cade, 1949) is the gold-standard mood stabiliser for both the treatment and the prophylaxis of bipolar disorder and the only one with a clear anti-suicidal effect; its therapeutic index is narrow (toxicity usually above about 1.5 mmol/L). Fluoxetine, haloperidol and lorazepam are not mood stabilisers. asked once
  11. Fluoxetine (an SSRI) is not a first-line treatment for acute mania and can trigger a switch into mania; the first-line drugs are lithium, valproate and the atypical antipsychotics (olanzapine, risperidone, quetiapine, aripiprazole). Haloperidol and lorazepam are adjuncts. asked once
  12. Suicide risk is highest when high lethality, a specific plan, frequent suicidal ideas and a family history converge. The strongest single predictor is a previous attempt; the strongest psychological predictor is hopelessness. asked once
  13. Women are about twice as likely as men to have depression from puberty onwards, so "men are more vulnerable to depression than women" is the false statement; depression is widespread and increasing, often triggered by a stressful event, and its severity is associated with suicide risk. asked once
  14. Depression is "the common cold of psychiatry" (Seligman's "common cold of psychopathology", 1970s): 12-month prevalence about 7 percent in the United States, lifetime about 15 to 20 percent. asked once
  15. The two key symptoms of a manic episode are extremely elevated mood and grandiose ideas. Hallucinations are not among the DIG FAST symptoms, social phobia is an anxiety disorder, and intense fear of weight gain is anorexia nervosa. asked once
  16. The mode (method) of a suicide attempt indicates its lethality, the medical danger of the act; intent is the wish to die and is judged from circumstances and statements, not from the method. asked once
  17. The "hybrid" disorder is mixed anxiety and depressive disorder: ICD-10 F41.2 and ICD-11 mixed depressive and anxiety disorder; it is absent from DSM-5-TR. asked once
  18. Cyclothymic disorder: at least 2 years (1 in children) of numerous hypomanic and depressive symptoms that never meet the criteria for a hypomanic, manic or major depressive episode, never symptom-free for more than 2 months (ICD-10 F34.0: persistent instability of mood). asked once
  19. What best distinguishes a primary psychotic disorder from a mood disorder with psychotic features is the mood congruence of the psychotic symptoms, together with their confinement to mood episodes; delusions occur in both. asked once
  20. The kindling hypothesis (Robert Post, 1992): each episode lowers the threshold for future episodes, so later episodes need less stress and come faster; it rests on Goddard's kindling of seizures in rats. asked once
  21. Anaclitic depression (1946), the withdrawal and decline of infants separated from their caregivers, and hospitalism (1945) were described by René Spitz, not Piaget, Skinner or Eysenck. asked once
  22. Seasonal affective disorder (Rosenthal and colleagues, 1984) is coded in DSM-5 as the "with seasonal pattern" specifier of recurrent major depressive disorder or of bipolar I or II disorder, not a separate disorder; it is treated with light therapy. asked once
